Summary 
This addendum contains the following fix parts: 
a)
A funding proposal summary titled “Climate Investor Two” submitted by the Nederlandse
Financierings-Maatschappij voor Ontwikkelingslanden N.V. (FMO);
b)
No-objection letters issued by the national designated authorities or focal point(s);
c)
Environmental and Social report(s) disclosure;
d)
Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment;
e)
Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s
assessment; and
f)
Gender documentation of the funding proposal.
These documents are presented as submitted by the accredited entity and the national designated 
authority(ies) or focal point(s), respectively. Pursuant to the Comprehensive Information Disclosure 
Policy of the Fund, the funding proposal titled titled “Climate Investor Two” by the Nederlandse 
Financierings-Maatschappij voor Ontwikkelingslanden N.V. (FMO) is being circulated on a limited 
distribution basis only to Board Members and Alternate Board Members to ensure confidentiality of 
certain proprietary, legally privileged or commercially sensitive information of the entity.

Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template 
Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-
reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc. 
Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details 
provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be 
reflected in all relevant documents.  
The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not 
exceed 60. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual 
service standard time. 
The recommended font is Arial, size 11.  
Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and Programme funding proposals 
will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, 
subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. 
Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.4.    
 
Please submit the completed proposal to: 
fundingproposal@gcfund.org 
Please use the following name convention for the file name: 
“FP-[Accredited Entity Short Name]-[Country/Region]-[YYYY/MM/DD]”

A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages) 
The water cycle is one of the most important and often overlooked, parts of the global climate crisis. The 
feedback loop is complicated, as water can become a substantial carbon emitter when managed 
improperly, with methane emissions from untreated waste and wastewater accounting for 7% of global 
greenhouse gas emissions, while riverine, marine, and coastal ecosystems have the potential to act as a 
powerful ‘carbon sinks’ that sequester and avoid carbon emissions. The imbalance between the water 
cycle and the climate is threatening many livelihoods, as growing economies and populations are 
reducing freshwater availability, putting over USD 6 trillion of developing nations’ GDP at riski. The World 
Health Organization estimates that there already are 750 million people with no access to clean water, 
and 2.5 billion people lacking access to proper sanitation. Even the oceans, which cover 71% of the 
earth’s surface and hold 96.5% of all earth’s water, are under threat with unprecedented pollution rates, 
decreasing biodiversity, and rising sea levels. At current rates, by 2050 there will be a great greater 
volume of plastic in the oceans than all marine life combined.  
There’s a clear need for climate-resilient infrastructure to mitigate the risks described above. This 
necessity is even further amplified in developing countries. Inadequate water and sanitation infrastructure 
are estimated to be related to 80% of all illnesses in the developing worldii. In total, the World Bank 
estimates that by 2050, water shortages caused by climate change could reduce the GDP of developing 
countries in Asia by USD 4.4 trillion and developing countries in Africa by USD 1.6 trillioniii. Climate 
finance is responding; however, investment requirements far exceed the current level of financingiv. 
Climate Investor Two (“CI2”) has the mission to develop and construct infrastructure projects in 
developing countries in the water, sanitation, and ocean sectors that reduce the effects and 
consequences of climate change by ameliorating GHG emissions and by increasing resilience. To do so, 
CI2 will deploy an innovative whole-of-life financing approach utilizing two independent but operationally 
inter-linked funds. The two funds focus on different stages of the project lifecycle; the Development Fund 
(“DF2”) provides development loans to early-stage projects and their ecosystems, whereas the 
Construction Equity Fund (“CEF2”) provides equity during the construction phase. With a Green Climate 
Fund (“GCF”) participation of USD 145 million, the prospective fund sizes are estimated at a USD 100 
million DF2 and a USD 1 billion CEF210 (out which 80% would be eligible for GCF proceeds – a USD 80 
mln. DF2 and a USD 800 mln. CEF2). GCF participation would enable CI2 to scale significantly and 
provide scarce, highly additional donor capital to the structure which is required to crowd in further private 
sector participation. 
CI2 will realize climate adaptation benefits in the water sector by building infrastructure that sources, 
transports, and treats the water necessary for both municipal and industrial users to be shielded from the 
consequences of climate change. In the sanitation sector, CI2 will realize mitigation and adaptation 
benefits through waste and wastewater treatment, reducing methane emissions and contributing to water 
re-use. Finally, in the ocean sector, CI2 will invest in physical infrastructures, such as environmental 
services for ports or green shipping initiatives that ameliorate GHG emissions. Additionally, CI2 will 
pursue investments in nature-based solutions to protect, preserve and restore riverine, wetland, 
coastland, and marine ecosystems (hereafter: “ecosystems”). Ecosystems are an important part of 
nature’s defenses against climate change and an important part of people’s livelihoods. They are currently 
 
8 The USD 145 mln. will be subscribed to the Development Fund as a reimbursamble grant. USD 100 mln. will then be 
subscribed to the Construction Equity Fund as equity (same structure as FP099). However, do note that the GCF FAA will 
therefore be for a reimbursamble grant. 
9 Investment period of the CEF is 15 years  
10 The CEF includes three different tiers: (i) USD 200 mln. in junior-equity provided by Donors, (ii) USD 400 mln. of 
mezzanine provided by impact investors and DFIs and (iii) USD 400 mln. senior equity provided by commercial and 
institutional investors.

 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 6 OF 63 
 
under stark threat facing ecological degradation at a rate ten to hundreds of times higher than the average 
over the past 10 million years, and with more than 50% of natural ecosystems at risk of being lostv. 
Through investments in nature-based solutions, CI2 targets cross-cutting climate change mitigation and 
adaptation outcomes that take advantage of the natural ability of restored ecosystems to sequester 
carbon and foster ecosystem-based adaptation. 
By developing and constructing additional water supply, treatment, distribution, and waste-water 
treatment facilities, as well as physical and nature-based oceans infrastructure, CI2 will have a 
quantifiable and measurable social and environmental impact in developing countries. CI2 aims to 
construct up to USD 2.96 billion in infrastructure projects in developing countries (core indicator 3). The 
projects financed, developed, and constructed by CI2 will provide ~11.18 million people with year-round 
access to reliable and safe water supply despite climate shocks and stresses and protected and 
strengthened ecosystems (core indicator 2). In addition, the coverage of ecosystems protected and 
strengthened in response to climate variability and change will be ~1.7 million Ha (supplementary 
indicator 4.1). With regards to mitigation benefits, CI2 will reduce or avoid ~4.96 million tCO2e per year 
for a total of ~44.65 million tCO2e over the implementation period (core indicator 1). CI2 will create 
additional opportunities for scalability and replicability beyond its projects, triggering the paradigm shift 
described in more detail in section D2. The specific climate vulnerabilities within the countries eligible for 
GCF funding through CI2 are described in Annex 7. 
 
FMO, the Dutch Development Bank, will act as Accredited Entity for this proposal and as an Executing 
Entity in respect of specific activities which will be elaborated in the FAA. Climate Fund Managers, a joint 
venture between FMO and Sanlam, will act as the Fund Manager of Climate Investor Two, and is the first 
Executing Entity. The DF2 and the CEF2, the two Funds within Climate Investor Two, will act as the 
second and third Executing Entities.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION 
B.1. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages) 
The climate crisis 
Globally, the seven hottest years in recorded history have been the six years from 2014 to 2020vi, driven 
by increasing concentrations of greenhouse gases in the atmosphere. Simultaneously atmospheric 
carbon reached a corresponding record high in 2020, driven primarily by anthropogenic use of fossil 
fuelsvii. As of 2020, global average temperatures are 1.14°C higher than pre-industrial temperatures, the 
effects of this warming are already being felt, often disproportionately in developing nationsviii. For every 
1°C increase in temperature, a developing nation can expect a decrease in the gross domestic product 
of 1.3%, whilst developed nations are far less affected by temperature variationsix. The increase in global 
temperatures, combined with the vulnerability of developing nations to these temperatures highlights the 
critical need for climate finance to be invested in projects and programs which both decrease greenhouse 
gas emissions and increase the resilience of host nations to adapt to these changes. To date, climate 
finance has primarily invested in climate mitigation projects, receiving an average of USD 537 billion in 
2017-18, compared to only USD 30 billion invested in climate adaptation projectsx.  
The importance of the water cycle in the climate crisis  
Water is an often-overlooked part of the global efforts to mitigate and respond to the climate crisis. Water 
is paramount to all life on earth, and any imbalance in its cycle or supply chain can quickly become 
detrimental to the global climate and the many livelihoods that depend upon it. Imbalances are created 
by a combination of growing economies and populations, changing weather patterns due to climate 
change, and a growing lack of resilient infrastructure. An example of how these imbalances have serious 
consequences for many is shown by the grim observation that more than 80% of illnesses in the 
developing world are linked to inadequate water and sanitation infrastructure.  
CI2 aims to develop and construct the infrastructure that’s required to future-proof parts of the water 
cycle. The investment thesis differentiates between three different sectors: (i) ‘the water sector’, (ii) ‘the 
sanitation sector, and the (iii) ‘the oceans and ecosystems sector’. The water sector is defined by projects 
that source, transport, and treat water to and from municipalities and sectors. The sanitation sector looks 
at treating waste and wastewater to avoid and sequester methane emissions while providing clean 
sanitation. The oceans and ecosystems sector aims to: (1) improve the ocean’s healthy by providing 
environmental services to ports and harbors and (2) protect, strengthen, and restore important 
ecosystems.  
The water sectors 
Water is fundamental to human life and economic development but is also the medium through which 
the effects of climate change will be felt on earth, with a warming climate causing increasingly variable 
and unpredictable rainfall. Changing climate patterns, together with growing economies and populations 
could reduce urban water availability in 2050 by two-thirds compared to 2015 levels, putting over USD 6 
trillion of developing nations’ GDP at riskxi. UNSESCO estimates that over 2 bn. people already live in 
areas subject to water stress. A study done by the WHO and UNICEF in 2017 shows that globally more 
than 785 mln people did not have access to at least basic water services and more than 884 mln. people 
do not have immediate access to safe water to drink. Women are often more negatively impacted as 
they are responsible for collecting the water within the household. These responsibilities reduce the 
likelihood of women receiving education. One of the objectives of CI2 in the water sector is to realize

=== PAGE 11 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 8 OF 63 
 
 
climate change adaptation impacts in countries undergoing, or expected to undergo water stress, through 
building the infrastructure which sources, transports, and treats the water necessary for both municipal 
and industrial users to be resilient to the localized impacts of climate change. 
The sanitation sectors 
Sanitation, defined as the sustainable collection, treatment, and disposal of waste and wastewater, is a 
major source of greenhouse gasses, environmental damage, and health hazards in developing countries. 
In developing countries, over 80% of all wastewaters are discharged into water sources without any 
treatment, resulting in severe and worsening pathogen pollution in one-third of all rivers. Pathogen 
pollution, caused by for instance seepage from landfills into water aquifers, affects surface water 
resources which supply 60% of the world’s water consumption. As a result, water quality has significantly 
worsened in Asia, Africa, and Latin America since 1990xii. More than 3 billion people worldwide lack 
adequate facilities to safely wash their hands at home, with stark concentrations of them living in Sub-
Saharan Africa and Southern Asiaxiii. Similarly, over two billion people in developing countries have no 
access to solid waste collection, and with solid waste volumes projected to increase by 1 billion tons 
annually to reach 2.6 billion tons by 2050, serious environmental pollution will continue to occur without 
investment in sanitation (and waste) infrastructure. In improving groundwater quality and reducing 
pollution through its investments in sanitation infrastructure, CI2 increases the resilience of communities 
to climate change by improving the quality and availability of different water sources including rivers and 
groundwater. In turn, as in many cases livelihoods are dependent on these polluted water resources for 
sustenance or work, CI2 increases the resilience of communities to climate change through the 
preservation and restoration of existing and alternative sources. The lack of sanitation facilities is even 
more impactful for young women, as they are more likely to miss out on education due to insufficient 
sanitation at schoolsxiv. The primary objective of CI2 in the sanitation sector is to deliver significant climate 
change mitigation impacts, through building the infrastructure which sustainably treats and manages 
solid wastes and wastewater, including, wherever possible, using waste and wastewater as a source for 
bio-energy generation by constructing waste to energy projects. 
The oceans and their ecosystems  
The oceans provide half of the Earth’s oxygen, feed three billion people, and have absorbed an estimated 
93% of the added heat arising from human-driven changes to the atmospherexv. In economic terms, the 
annual economic value-added output derived from the oceans is estimated at USD 2.6 trillion, making 
the oceans the world’s seventh-largest economy, after the United Kingdomxvi, showcasing its importance 
for many livelihoods.  The climate crisis is imminent for the oceans, with climate change, causing warmer 
waters already being observed at depths of 1000m. Atmospheric warming is causing the melting of inland 
glaciers and ice, causing rising sea levels with significant impacts on shorelines (coastal erosion, 
saltwater intrusion, habitat destruction). The IPCC projects the global sea level to increase by 0.4 to 0.8 
meters by the end of the century, putting shorelines, but also local water and sanitation infrastructure 
projects, further at riskxvii. Oceans are under further serious pressure by uncontrolled pollution, often 
caused by the lack of infrastructure to properly treat (waste)water that’s being dumped in the world’s 
rivers and shores, and resource overexploitation.  
In addition to reducing ocean pollution through its investments in pollution control through the sanitation 
sector, CI2 also aims to invest in infrastructure for ports, ships, and harbors that contributes to improved 
ocean health (by reducing greenhouse gas emissions). In addition, it will pursue investments and 
ecosystem-based approaches to protect, preserve and/or restore riverine, coastland, marine, and 
wetland ecosystems. Financing ecosystems has traditionally been seen as net-cost projects by

=== PAGE 12 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 9 OF 63 
 
 
governments and businesses because external costs associated with pollution of the environment and 
GHG emissions are often not accounted for correctly Through investments in ecosystem-based 
approaches, CI2 targets cross-cutting climate change mitigation outcomes through the natural ability of 
restored ecosystems to sequester carbon and adaptation outcomes, through ecosystem-based 
adaptation. CFM is a signatory to the United Nations Environment Program’s Sustainable Blue Economy 
Finance Initiative and the investments of CI2 in the oceans’ sector will be consistent with this framework.  
While climate change resilient water and sanitation infrastructure is a global challenge, CFM recognizes 
that Adaptation requires tailored and localized solutions to have a legacy that endures. As a result, CFM 
will develop and construct interventions that fit within the host’s country National Determined 
Contributions (“NDC’s”) and local context to maximize the impact they have on increasing resilience and 
reducing greenhouse gas emissions. Annex 7 of this Funding Proposal includes more detailed climate 
assessments about countries for which the AE has obtained, or is aiming to obtain, an NOL. 
B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram) 
Climate Investor Two aims to create a paradigm shift by making investments which result in the scaling 
and replication of water, sanitation, and oceans infrastructure that is aligned to the adaptation and 
mitigation needs of developing countries. If it succeeds in this, low-emission, climate-resilient sustainable 
development will be catalyzed in the programme’s host countries because successful climate 
investments in the target sectors, with a risk adjusted return, will be demonstrated to commercial

=== PAGE 13 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 10 OF 63 
 
 
investors. In turn, this will foster significant contributions to blue and green climate finance in emerging 
markets, bringing countries closer to meeting their INDCs, and the global targets of the SDGs 
To derive the above, CI2’s theory of change relies on a number of reasonable assumptions.  First, it is 
assumed that there will be sufficient expertise provided by project co-developers and co-investors for 
project development and implementation. This is an assumption further underpinned by CFM’s inhouse 
technical, legal and ESG expertise, which is applied to projects accordingly in order to complement 
those specialties of co-developers or co-investors. Second, it is assumed that offtake and/or financial 
service contracts enable the financial sustainability of projects. This condition is considered and 
assessed prior to and during the development phase of a CI2 project. Third, it is assumed that targeted 
ecosystems are not further degraded by external variables. While this assumption is reliant on external 
factors, CFMs ESG and technical teams scrutinize different aspects of a potential project to evaluate 
and consider its long-term feasibility. Fourth, it is assumed that the projects will generate a material 
demonstration effect, leading to catalyzation of capital from external sources to similar projects. As in 
the case of assumption number one, this is supported by CFMs own in house expertise, which will 
drive knowledge transfer and skill sharing when working with local developers. Fifth, it is assumed that 
PPP regulation clearly defines a process for private sector participation. Sixth, it is assumed that 
wastewater quality standards are effectively enforced in the beneficiary countries. As the regulatory 
environments for water and sanitation in the emerging markets continue to improve in response to the 
threats posed by climate change, this assumption is likely to develop in CI2’s favor over the 20-year 
implementation period. Similarly, the increased need to address infrastructure deficits support 
assumption seven, that country regulatory frameworks permit and facilitate private sector participation. 
Eighth, it is assumed there will be a stable political environment in operating geographies. While this 
too is dependent on externalities, CFM’s regional offices employ the necessary expertise to take 
measures to minimize associated risks. This can include  project structuring approaches, political risk 
insurance, and also the support of the CI2 investor community which contains a number of influential 
regional financial institutes. Finally it is assumed that institutional structures support commercial 
investment in CI2’s target sectors. This assumption, like assumptions six and seven, is bolstered by the 
necessity for commercial investment to address the scale of the existing deficits within CI2’s target 
sectors. 
Furthermore, in order to generate a paradigm shift, CI2’s theory of change identifies and aims to 
address or mitigate the following risks and barriers. 
With respect to climate risk, the CI2 theory of change identifies that climate change, increasing 
population, and growing economies will reduce the availability of water if resilient infrastructure is not 
built. This will be exacerbated as untreated waste and wastewater will further increase GHG emissions 
while causing environmental pollution and health hazards. Simultaneously, the exploitation of the 
oceans will reduce biodiversity, increase environmental pollution, and negatively influence livelihoods 
for those most dependent on the oceans and their ecosystems. While this continues, the issue is 
compounded by increasing sea levels and water temperatures, which will jeopardize the health of the 
oceans and their ecosystems further. Finally, deterioration of the natural asset base of riverine, coastal, 
wetland, and marine ecosystems will reduce nature’s ability to act as a carbon sink, reduce their 
regulatory abilities, threaten food stock, reduce biodiversity, and negatively influence local 
communities’ livelihoods. When considered together, these five individual factors represent a network 
of related and interdependent drivers of climate risk. 
Given CI2’s planned interventions and the climate risks it intends to address, it is possible to identify a 
number of barriers and risks to the accomplishment of the paradigm shift it will trigger.

=== PAGE 14 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 11 OF 63 
 
 
Barrier 1, is that the potential long-term benefits of investing in target sector projects may not outweigh 
the high perceived risks. This can be linked to Risk 1, that low volumes of private investments are 
mobilized due to a lack of demonstrating the commercial viability of investing in target sector projects. 
The water, sanitation, and ocean sectors have primarily been within the public domain. Traditional 
private investors, therefore, perceive the sectors as high-risk. These (perceived) risks can be reduced 
by both (i) providing private sector investors with a blended finance solution that reduces their risks and 
(ii) by demonstrating the bankability of projects in the water, sanitation, and oceans sectors. A lack of 
long-term private sector buy-in will further jeopardize global temperatures and the resilience that 
developing countries have against it. 
Barrier 2, is the limited know-how and capacity for the development, construction, and management of 
target sector projects in host countries. 
There’s often limited capacity for the development and construction of complicated and technology-
abundant infrastructure projects. Adequately addressing this will require cooperation between all the 
developers and third parties. 
Barrier 3, is the Increasingly limited public sector budgets and commitment for target sector projects in 
host countries. 
The budgets that developing countries have available to invest in the water, sanitation, and ocean 
sectors are insufficient for the task at hand. Adequately addressing the lack of the water infrastructure 
that would be needed to stay within the 1.5-degree pathway while ensuring resilient water supply will 
require the mobilization of private sector finance. 
Climate Investor Two will aim to address these barriers and mitigate these risks by: (i) establishing and 
managing Climate Investor Two (including the Development – and the Construction Equity Fund) and 
(ii) development and implementation of mitigation and adaptation projects in developing countries. 
The establishment and management of the two different funds, that together provide a whole-of-life 
solution to project financing, will help to showcase the commercial viability of water, sanitation, and 
oceans infrastructure projects by demonstrating the possibility of risk-adjusted returns to investors. This 
can help investors to see the long-term benefits of investing in such sectors and countries and mitigate 
some of the perceived high risks. Furthermore, it will showcase the effectiveness of blended finance 
vehicles such as Climate Investor Two in mobilizing (private sector) investors with different risk to 
return appetites. This will result in further mobilization of the private sector for the much-needed scaling 
and replication of water, sanitation, and ocean infrastructure projects in developing countries. 
Furthermore, showcasing the commercial viability of water, sanitation, and ocean infrastructure 
projects will contribute to providing know-how and capacity for the development and implementation of 
such projects in the host countries by providing a knowledge of transfer to local parties. CI2 aims to do 
this by including local partners and developers in both the development as well as the construction 
phases of its projects. Lastly, it will aim to include local financial intermediaries, including DAEs, to the 
refinancing of its projects. 
Climate Investor Two will develop mitigation and adaptation projects by providing development loans 
from its DF2 to do the required feasibility studies, ESIAs, gender assessments, stakeholder 
engagements, potential pilot projects and everything else that’s required before a project can go its 
construction phase. Once successfully developed, these projects graduate to the CEF2, which will 
implement (construct and operate) the projects by providing up to 100% of the equity (albeit it is

=== PAGE 15 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 12 OF 63 
 
 
expected that most equity stakes would be below 75%). Successfully developing and implementing 
mitigation and adaptation projects for the water, sanitation, and ocean sectors in developing countries 
will: (i) improve wastewater treatment capacity, (ii) improve access to water, (iii) improve access to 
sanitation, (iv) improve renewable energy capacity and generation, (v) improve access to fuel efficient 
green ports and shipping, and (vi) restore and/or protect riverine, wetland, coastland, and marine 
ecosystems. Improving wastewater treatment and capacity, renewable energy capacity and generation, 
access to fuel-efficient green ports and shipping and restoration and/or protection of riverine wetland, 
coastal, and marine ecosystems will then result in ameliorating GHG emissions in the water sanitation 
and oceans sectors of the Programme’s host countries. Improved access to water will and restoration 
and/or protection of riverine, wetland, coastland and marine ecosystems will result in beneficiaries with 
more climate-resilient water security in the Programme’s host countries. As a co-benefit, improved 
access to sanitation, access to water and wastewater treatment capacity will result in improved health 
and sanitation by strengthening the water, sanitation, and oceans sectors in the Programme’s host 
countries. 
Altogether the above implies that if CI2’s investments result in the scaling and replication of water, 
sanitation, and oceans infrastructure projects aligned to the adaptation and mitigation needs of 
developing countries, then low-emission, climate-resilient sustainable development will be catalyzed in 
the programme’s host countries because successful climate investments in the target sectors, with an 
attractive risk-adjusted return, will be demonstrated to commercial investors fostering significant 
contributions to blue and green climate finance in emerging markets.

.3. Project/Programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages) 
Introduction to Climate Investor Two 
Climate Investor Two is a blended finance facility mandated to develop and construct projects in the 
water, sanitation, and oceans sectors in developing countries to contribute to climate mitigation and 
adaptation benefits. CI2 duplicates the structure of the CI1 fund that launched in 2017 and received a 
GCF commitment in 2019 (FP099). CI2, like its predecessor CI1, was designed to address the lack of 
private financing options in developing countries by providing a whole-of-life project financing solution

=== PAGE 18 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 15 OF 63 
 
 
that caters to investors with different risk to return profiles (ranging from Donors to commercial 
investors). CI2 constitutes two independent but interlinked funds, being two different legal entities, that 
operate at an arms’ length: the Development Fund and the Construction Equity Fund.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Development Fund: The Development Fund (“DF” or “DF2”) has been set up to provide development 
loans, accompanied by development support/assistance provided by CFM and 3rd parties, to project 
companies. The purpose of this is to fast-track qualifying development projects to financial close by 
reducing the development timeline and improving the ultimate bankability of infrastructure projects. The 
DF2 will increase the implementation rate of projects – while demonstrating the commercial viability of 
industries that have traditionally been part of a public mandate. The DF2’s investor base mainly consists 
of Donors that want to get involved in the high-impact development phase. Once projects successfully 
complete their development phase by passing the assessments (including but not limited to gender 
assessment, ESIA’s, and stakeholder engagements) they ‘graduate’ to the CEF2. The CEF2 then 
reimburses the grant to the DF2 and the project moves on to the construction phase. CFM ensures the 
DF2 remains ‘revolvable’ by recycling its capital. As it is expected that not all projects successfully 
complete the development phase (CFM expects 2/3rd of the projects to graduate), the CEF2 reimburses 
the grant with a premium that compensates for losses made on unsuccessful projects. The premium paid 
remains subject to the default rate within the DF2 (but would equal 50% if the default rate does end up 
Equity 
Development 
funding 
Construction 
Equity Fund 
US$ 1 bn 
Development Fund 
US$ 100 mn 
Development and construction of infrastructure projects in the water, 
sanitation, and oceans sectors  
Tier 3 commercial investors US$ 400mn  
Tier 2 commercial investors US$ 400mn  
Tier 1 concessional capital US$ 200mn

=== PAGE 19 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 16 OF 63 
 
 
at 1/3rd.)  This recycling character of the DF2 enables CI2 to complete multiple project cycles within its 
20-year implementation period. CFM expects CI2 to complete two full project cycles (see D.6). 
Construction Equity Fund: The CEF2 has been set up to provide to kick-start projects from their 
development phase, after the ‘proof of concept’, by providing up to 100 percent of the required equity 
(and repaying the development loan, including a premium, to replenish the DF2). The equity stake can 
include different sorts of equity (ordinary, preferred, shareholder loans) and will not always constitute a 
majority stake. It aims to further decrease the construction time and costs by: (i) reducing the complexity 
and costs at the project level through equity only funding strategy, (ii) reducing the construction timeline 
and thereby reducing the (costly) exposure period to investors, (iii) reducing costs by eliminating certain 
debt specific costs, (iv) recycling capital from completed projects to new project to reduce the time any 
funding ‘stands still’ (something that happens often in fundraising). The CEF2 uses a blended finance 
approach to mobilize three different tiers of investors: (i) Donors in the most junior tranche (representing 
20% of the CEF2), (ii) DFI’s, and commercial/impact investors in the mezzanine tranche (representing 
40% of the CEF2), and (iii) commercial and institutional investors in the senior tranche (representing 
40% of the CEF2). The CEF2, like the DF2, recycles its capital over the 20-year implementation period 
(with the final deployment cycle occurring by year 15). The 20:40:40 ratios between the three different 
tiers are set and will always be maintained. 
CFM, which will be responsible for the sourcing for and execution of, projects based within the 
following sectors:

• 
Bulk water supply and storage. 
•

•

• 
Decentralized water treatment and distribution. 
 
Sanitation (waste and wastewater), including the following sub-sectors: 
• 
Wastewater collection and sewerage networks. 
• 
Wastewater treatment facilities, including wastewater to energy production and nutrient 
extraction and capture. 
• 
Wastewater recycling facilities. 
• 
Waste to value facilities, including waste sorting, recycling, composting, and waste to energy 
production; and 
• 
Methane capture and destruction for waste facilities. 
 
Oceans, including the following sub-sectors: 
• 
Sustainable management, protection, and/or restoration of wetland, riverine, coastal, and 
marine ecosystems. 
• 
Seaweed aquaculture. 
• 
Climate-resilient port infrastructure.

• 
Sustainable solutions for ports, including onshore power, waste, and wastewater treatment. 
•

•

• 
De-carbonisation of coastal and inland shipping.

ESMS; and 
•

These projects will be assessed extensively on their contribution to either/or climate resilience and or 
GHG/reductions. Alongside that, the projects are screened on their commercial viability, ESG 
consideration, and governmental support. In addition, the partners (developers) are also screened on 
their ESG approach, reputation & local network, and track record. 
Other investment restrictions / criteria for CI2 include: 
Subject to the restrictions outlined below, the Fund will invest in the Mandate Countries listed below. 
The Fund will invest no more than:  
 
•

such limit may be increased to a maximum of 20% of the Aggregate Fund Capital in a single 
Project Company / sub project  with the consent of the CI Two Funds Advisory Board, which 
shall not be unreasonably withheld or delayed;  
•

•

•

•

•

•

• 
25% of its Aggregate Fund Capital into the oceans sector (as defined above). 
Components, outcomes, and underlying (sub)activities Climate Investor Two. 
Component 1: Establishment and management of Climate Investor Two 
The first component aims at establishing and raising the funding for the two Funds that will form the 
basis for Climate Investor Two. Thereafter, it aims at managing the three legal entities (CFM, the 
‘Stichting’ CI2 Development Fund (or: DF2), and the CI2 Coöperatief Construction Equity Fund (or: 
CEF2)’. This includes putting in place and maintaining, all the legal and governance arrangements (as 
described in B.4).  
Activity 1.1: Fundraising, establishment, and management of the Development Fund. 
CFM will continue to raise Donor funding, in the form of reimbursable grants for the DF2. CFM aims to 
raise an amount of USD 100mln (USD 80 mln. for countries eligible for this proposal). It will do this by 
pro-actively reaching out to Donors, impact investors, and multilateral organizations that have a 
mandate that fits the Development Fund. Once the targeted amount has been reached, and the final

=== PAGE 21 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 18 OF 63 
 
 
close achieved, CFM will oversee and manage all affairs of the legal entity for the entire 
implementation period of the DF2.   
Activity 1.2: Fundraising, establishment, and management of the Construction Equity Fund 
CFM will continue to raise funding for the three different tiers of the Construction Equity Fund (or: 
CEF2) up to the targeted amount of USD 1 bln (USD 800 mln. for countries eligible for this proposal). 
This will include funding from commercial investors (pension funds, asset managers, and other 
institutional investors, as well as impact-oriented investors (i.e. DFIs) and Donors (such as the 
European Commission, the Dutch government, and the GCF). The funding will be raised within the pre-
determined ratio of 20:40:40 between the three different tiers. Once the targeted amount has been 
reached, and the final close achieved, CFM will oversee and manage all the affairs of the legal entity 
for the entire implementation period of the CEF2.   
Outcome 1: Private sector finance mobilized for the scaling and replication of water, sanitation, and 
ocean infrastructure projects. 
By successfully setting up the two different funds and raising USD 100 mln. Of funding for the DF2 (out 
of which USD 80 mln. is destined for GCF eligible countries) and USD 1 bln. For the CEF2 (out of which 
USD 800 mln. is destined for GCF eligible countries), the Programme will successfully engage the private 
sector in climate change adaptation and mitigation projects. It does this in three different manners: (i) it 
mobilizes private financing directly into CI2 by providing a blended finance solution that makes the return 
to risk profile acceptable for private investors and (ii) it demonstrates the potential that blended finance 
and whole-of-life all equity financing has for mobilizing private capital and project finance and (iii) it 
demonstrates the commercial viability/bankability of climate mitigation and adaptation infrastructure 
projects (linked to Activity 2.1 and Outcome 2, 3 and 4). 
Component 2: Development and implementation of mitigation and adaptation projects for the water, 
sanitation, and oceans sectors in developing countries  
Activity 2.1:  Development of mitigation and adaptation projects for the water, sanitation, and ocean 
sectors in developing countries. 
Activity 2.1.1: Identifying potential investment opportunities and initial screening by CFM IC: 
The CFM deal teams will do the initial project sourcing. Once an opportunity has been found, and 
enough data have been gathered, an initial approval and guidance are requested by presenting the 
case to CFM’s internal IC (“CFM IC”). The IC then decides whether: (i) the project fits within the 
investment mandate and (ii) whether additional information, restructuring, or due diligence is required 
before presenting it to the external IC. The CFM IC should be seen as a first ‘quality control’. 
Activity 2.1.2: (Re)structuring after CFM IC and initial due diligence 
The CFM IC will provide the deal team with tasks to be completed before the actual Climate Investor 
Two Development Fund Investment Committee (DF2 IC). After that, the deal team will work to get all 
the information required to present the impact and business case of the project to the DF2 IC.  
 
During this phase, the deal teams will gather all the information required to present the impact and 
business case towards the DF2 IC. The deal team will do initial due diligence on the project and the 
third party (co)-developers and sponsors.

=== PAGE 22 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 19 OF 63 
 
 
Activity 2.1.3: Project approval by DF2 IC: 
The deal team presents the case to the DF2 IC after doing the initial due diligence and completing the 
data information requested by the CFM IC. The DF2 IC will judge proposals, among other indicators, 
on the adaptation and mitigation impact they provide for the host countries. For adaptation, the deal 
teams will provide them with historic climate trends (trends (observed changes or variability in changes 
in the projects area), climate change projections in the area, and climate impact (an explanation on 
how these changes will hurt communities in the project area). Adaptation benefits will be further 
addressed by explaining how the project company’s products, services, or technology will help the 
community adapt to these climate impacts, an explanation of how the proposed intervention will not 
lead to maladaptation, an explanation of the Rio Marker score for climate adaptation (to be achieved by 
the company) and an overview of the impact indicators that will be monitored to validate the above 
(including an identification of the baseline and mid-term (year 10) and final (year 20) projections). To 
assess mitigation benefits, CFM deal teams will explain how the project companies’ products, services, 
or technologies will help reduce, avoid, or sequester GHG emissions, an explanation of the Rio Marker 
Score that’s to be achieved by the project company and a description of the GHG gas accounting 
methodology to be used to validate the climate mitigation impact realized by the company’s services, 
products, or technologies (including the baseline, mid-term, and final projections).   
 
During the first DF2 IC, the DF2 IC will also approve terms of reference for an independent detailed 
adaptation and/or mitigation assessment based on the criteria set out above. This detailed independent 
assessment will be carried out as part of the more detailed due diligence that’s conducted during the 
development phase (Activity 2.1.4.) put preceding the approval of the Construction Equity Fund 2 
Investment Committee (CEF2 IC) (Activity 2.2.1). This independent assessment will then form part of 
the materials that the CEF2 IC receives and bases its approval on. 
It is only after approval by the DF2 IC (including, for the avoidance of doubt, the satisfactory initial 
climate screening conducted by CFM) that a proposed investment would receive funding from the DF2 
IC to move into the next stage of detailed due diligence and project development.  GCF proceeds will 
only be used for projects in countries for which an NOL has been obtained and to projects which 
contribute to the envisioned climate impact. Likewise, the proceeds from divestments will run through 
the CEF2’s waterfall on per project basis, ensuring that the GCF proceeds are not comingled with 
those from projects in countries for which no NOL has been obtained.  
Activity 2.1.4: Project development and extensive due diligence (including but not limited to stakeholder 
engagement, gender assessment, and Environmental and Social Impact Assessment.) 
The actual development phase starts after the approval of the DF2 IC. During this phase, CFM will 
conduct, supported by independent parties, extensive due diligence. This due diligence will include, but 
won’t be limited to, stakeholder engagements, gender assessments, and Environmental and Social 
Impact Assessments (ESIA). The ESIA will then judge whether the projects have any resettlement risk 
(which is not expected to happen often) after which a Resettlement Action Plan (RAP) would be 
drafted. It’s during this phase that all legal and regulatory permits are obtained and that the contractors 
are selected. This phase needs to be completed, on all important aspects, before the construction 
phase can start.  
Activity 2.2:  Implementation (construction and operation) of mitigation and adaptation projects for water, 
sanitation, and ocean sectors in developing countries.

=== PAGE 23 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 20 OF 63 
 
 
Activity 2.2.1: Project approval by CEF2 IC 
Upon successful completion of the development case, the deal team re-proposes the project to the 
CEF2 IC. This proposal will include all the findings off the due diligence and evaluations done in 
Activity 2.1.4. It is only after approval of the CEF2 IC (including, for the avoidance of doubt, the 
satisfactory independent screening conducted by a third party and satisfactory extensive due diligence) 
that a proposed investment would receive funding from the CEF2 IC. GCF proceeds will only be used 
for projects in countries for which an NOL has been obtained and to projects which contribute to the 
envisioned climate impact. Likewise, the proceeds from divestments will run through the CEF2’s 
waterfall on per project basis, ensuring that the GCF proceeds are not comingled with those from 
projects in countries for which no NOL has been obtained.  
Activity 2.2.2: Project construction: 
The CEF2 will then repay the development loan from the DF2 (including a premium to ensure full 
revolvability of the DF2) and commit equity to start a project’s construction phase after the successful 
approval of the CEF2 IC. During the construction phase, CFM will actively manage and oversee the 
project, in example by nominating directors or representatives on the boards or sub-committee of each 
investee company, to ensure optimal efficiency, (ESG) compliance, and maximum value creation.   
Activity 2.2.3: Project refinancing exit and exit: 
After successful construction, the project moves to the operational phase. Once a project is stable in 
terms of operations, impact, and cash flows, a process will commence procuring debt at the project 
level and, a few years later, the project’s exit will be arranged following approval from the CEF2 IC 
These two events will recapitalize CEF2 and enable it to commit funding to further projects. 
Outcome 2: Ameliorated GHG emissions in the water, sanitation, and ocean sectors of the Programme’s 
host countries (outputs 2, 5, 6, and 7).  
Output 2: The Programme will avoid up to ~16.7 million tCO2e in emissions by developing and 
constructing waste and wastewater treatment facilities,   
Output 5: The Programme will develop and construct waste-to-energy projects that will produce ~314.3 
GWh/yr in renewable energy (with a power capacity installed of (~111.5MW) providing up to ~ 314.3 
thousand people with power. In addition, the Programme will develop and construct wastewater 
treatment, both municipal and industrial, that will produce 1,510.3 GWh/yr (with a power capacity 
installed of 147.0MW) contributing to the reduction of ~592.1 thousand tCO2e annually and provide 
~1,5 million people with power.  
Output 6: The Programme will avoid up to ~955.8 thousand tCO2e in emissions annually by developing 
and constructing energy-efficient infrastructure for green ports and/or shipping. Examples would be 
climate-resilient port infrastructure, onshore power, waste and wastewater treatment in ports, port 
infrastructure for off-shore wind farm assembly, load out and servicing, and de-carbonization of coastal 
and inland shipping.  
Output 7: The Programme will restore and/or protect ~1.8 million hectares of riverine, marine, wetland, 
and coastal ecosystems, contributing up to ~2.2 million tCO2e emissions avoided or sequestered 
annually.  Furthermore, an estimate of ~2400 beneficiaries will benefit from restored and/or improved 
ecosystems.

=== PAGE 24 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 21 OF 63 
 
 
In total, the Programme is expected to reduce up to ~ 44.65 million tCO2e of emissions over its 
lifetime, with an annual impact of ~ 4.96 million tCO2e in year 20.  
The outcome will also contribute to outcome 1, further private sector involvement, by expanding the 
track record of private sector investments in a previously primarily public sector. Successful 
development and construction of waste-to-energy projects and protection and/or restoration of 
ecosystems will further enable private sector investments by reducing the (perceived) risks investors 
associate with the sector. 
Outcome 3:  Beneficiaries with climate-resilient water security by enhancement of the water, sanitation, 
and ocean sectors in host countries (outputs 3, 4 and 7).  
Output 3: The Programme will provide up to ~ 11.18 million people with access to climate-resilient 
water infrastructure by developing and constructing infrastructure for bulk water supply and storage, 
ocean-water desalination, brackish groundwater desalination, groundwater abstractions, atmospheric 
water generation, water treatment facilities, water utilities, and concessions, pumping household water 
connections and decentralized water treatment.  
Output 4: The Programme will provide up to ~1.97 million people with climate-resilient sanitation by 
developing and constructing infrastructure to collect wastewater, building sewerage networks, and 
treating and recycling waste.  
Output 7: The Programme will improve the livelihoods of communities by constructing green 
infrastructure investments that restore and/or protect marine, riverine, coastal, or wetland ecosystems. 
Examples would be wetland restoration to improve water retention, flood control, and restoration of 
mangrove forests to provide coastal protection. The combined efforts will restore and/or strengthen up 
to ~1.8 million hectares of vulnerable ecosystems 
Co-benefit 1: The Programme will contribute to health and sanitation. (output 2, 3, and 4) 
Combined, outcomes 1, 2, and 3 are aimed at contributing to CI2’s goal statement: 
If Climate Investor 2 investments result in the scaling and replication of water, sanitation, and oceans 
infrastructure projects aligned to the adaptation and mitigation needs of developing countries, then low-
emission, climate-resilient sustainable development will be catalyzed in the programme’s host 
countries because successful climate investments in the target sectors, with an attractive risk-adjusted 
return, will be demonstrated to commercial investors fostering significant contributions to blue and green 
climate finance in emerging markets.  
B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams) 
Flow of funds 
Fund flows for investments 
• 
The GCF and FMO will contractually agree that the GCF will commit USD 145 mln. In 
reimbursable grants, from which USD 100 mln. Is subscribed to a junior equity position (being 
the Tier 1 tranche of the CEF2), on terms to be agreed in the Funding Activity Agreement. For 
the avoidance of doubt, all the GCF funds for the purpose of CI2 will be kept on a separate 
ledger on FMO’s GCF account. 
• 
FMO will then make a USD 145 mln capital commitment. In reimbursable grants to the 
Development Fund. Subsequently, the Development Fund will make a USD 100 mln capital

=== PAGE 25 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 22 OF 63 
 
 
commitment to the Tier 1 tranche of the CEF2 (which is its most junior capital tranche). The 
remaining USD 45 mln. Will remain within the DF2. 
• 
FMO will request the GCF funds on a bi-(annual) basis based on CFM’s estimates on the 
liquidity needs for the pipeline and portfolio. 
• 
Based on estimates for capital requirements for approved DF2 or CEF2 investments as well as 
for costs and fees, the Manager will issue drawdowns to FMO. 
• 
Upon receipt and after analyzing the compliance to its internal policies and procedures, FMO 
will remit the funds to the DF2 from its fiduciary GCF account. The DF2 can then remit the funds 
to the Construction Equity Fund, to the extent required.  
Fund flows for distribution  
• 
Upon partial or full divestment of a portfolio investment, a refinancing of equity by debt, or any 
other interim income received by the CCEF2, the funds will be remitted to the DF2 as per the 
distribution waterfall. At the end of the DF2 fund term and upon its dissolution, to the extent that 
funding remains, the DF2 will distribute funding received in accordance with the applicable law 
for Dutch trusts (“Stichting”) to its contributors including to FMO in respect of any GCF funding 
(see section B.6 for more details).  
Legal arrangements  
FMO, in its capacity as Accredited Entity, will ensure that the following agreements are entered into for 
the implementation of the Funded Activity (each a “Subsidiary Agreement” and together the “Subsidiary 
Agreements”: 
• 
A side letter between the Accredited Entity, the Manager, CEF and DF, respectively, to pass 
down to the DF and CEF all relevant obligations in accordance with the AMA and FAA (a “Side 
Letter”); the Side Letter shall also set out the mechanism pursuant to which the Manager shall 
seek the Accredited Entity’s consent before presenting relevant matters (which may require 
GCF’s approval) for approval to the relevant DF and CEF governing and decision-making 
bodies, and the Accredited Entity shall not provide such consent to the Manager unless it has 
sought the prior written approval of the GCF;  
• 
The donors’ agreement between the Manager, DF and donors to the DF, which agreement 
governs the rights and obligations of donors, as well as the governance and commercial 
arrangements between the Manager and DF (“Donors’ Agreement”);  
• 
For the flow of the GCF Proceeds from FMO to the DF a subscription agreement entered into 
by FMO in relation to the DF, whereby FMO commits and the Manager accepts the relevant 
portion of the GCF Proceeds (“DF Subscription Agreement”);  
FMO, in its capacity as Accredited Entity of the Fund, will ensure that the following agreements are 
entered into for the implementation of the Funded Activity (each a “Key Fund Document” and together 
with the Subsidiary Agreements, the “Key Fund Documents”): 
• 
Separate management agreements between CFM and the DF and CEF respectively pursuant 
to which CFM will agree to act as the manager of the DF and CEF (each a “Management 
Agreement”);    
• 
The Members’ Agreement, between the Manager, CEF, investors to the CEF, which governs 
the rights and obligations of investors, as well as the governance and commercial arrangements 
between the Manager and CEF (“Members’ Agreement”);

=== PAGE 26 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 23 OF 63 
 
 
• 
For the flow of the GCF Proceeds from the DF to Tier 1 of CEF a subscription agreement 
entered into by the DF in relation to the CEF, whereby the DF commits and CFM (as Manager 
of CEF) accepts the relevant portion of the GCF Proceeds (“CEF Subscription Agreement”);   
• 
A set of Articles of Association in respect of each of the DF and the CEF governing corporate 
legal matters (e.g. membership, representation, board composition, voluntary liquidation etc.) 
(“DF Articles of Association” and “CEF Articles of Association”, respectively). 
The terms of such Subsidiary agreements shall be consistent with the AMA and the Term Sheet (Annex 
14), in particular in respect to the provisions set out in Schedules 1 & 2 to the term sheet.   
Organizational structure & team experience  
CFM is organized along regional and functional lines which combines; (i) a strong investment and 
operational focus with industry and local expertise and (ii) solid middle and back-office skills in 
operations, finance, and strategy (iii) sound risk management, coordinated legal / tax structuring, and 
robust compliance functionalities.  
CFM will operate from its offices in the Hague, the Netherlands (head office), Cape Town, Singapore, 
and Bogota (regional offices) as follows: 
Head office  
CFM’s head office is located in The Hague, Netherlands, and was established in 2015. At present the 
office has 24 full-time employees, with Andrew Johnstone, acting as CEO. The responsibilities of the 
Hague Office include (i) strategy & central oversight, (ii) tax & valuations, (iii) fund management, (iv) 
investor relations & capital raising, and (v) central environmental, social & governance management. 
Regional Offices 
CFM’s regional offices are led by regional heads with strong networks and specific skills and experience 
in discrete geographies and sectors, thereby providing the regional teams with regional intelligence. 
Three regional offices have been established, in South Africa, Colombia, and Singapore.  
 
CFM’s regional office for Africa is located in Cape Town, South Africa. Established in 2017 the office has 
19 full-time employees, with Sebastian Surie, Head of African Investments, acting as office head. The 
responsibilities of the Cape Town office include all investment activity in Africa, CFM’s risk, compliance, 
and finance functions, as led by the CFO, and CFM’s operations & human resources led by Ms. Caron 
Aitken.  
 
CFM’s regional office for Asia is located in Singapore, where it operates as Climate Fund Advisers (CFA). 
Established in 2017 the office has 14 full-time employees, with Nathan Schmidt, Head of Asian 
Investments, acting as office head. The Singapore office is responsible for all CFM’s investment activity 
in Asia.

•

the construction phase of a project its Lifecycle with equity financing.

discharging specific activities as an Executing Entity as will be further elaborated in the FAA.   
Conflict of interest: 
The AE, and the EE, are aware of potential conflicts of interest between FMO’s investments in CFM 
and the T2. The AE therefore always tests such constructs with its conflict-of-interest sounding board 
that sits in the compliance department. The AE, which has done many of such sub-delegation on 
behalf of Donors (e.g. the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, the European Commission, and the Green 
Climate Fund), has, therefore, set up Chinese walls, where appropriate, between the departments that 
do the investments from its balance sheet (in this case the Private Equity department) and the 
department that manages the investment on behalf of the Donor (the “Partnerships for Impact” 
department). The AE commits to delegating any positions within the governance of CI2, belonging to 
the GCF investments, to independent individuals in FMO. Furthermore, the AE will appoint a separate 
deal team to negotiate the FAA and other potentially sensitive documents.  
Experience of the two parties: 
FMO is the Dutch development bank and has invested in the private sector in developing countries and 
developing markets for more than 50 years. FMO invests in sectors with the highest long-term impact: 
financial institutions, energy, agribusiness, and infrastructure. FMO is a financial institution owned 51% 
by the Dutch State, 42% by Dutch banks, and 7% by employers’ associations, trade unions, and 
individual investors. 
With an investment portfolio of ca. EUR 10 billion spanning over 85 countries, FMO is one of the larger 
bilateral private sector development banks in the world. FMO’s strategic goal is to be the preferred 
partner to invest in local prosperity. 
FMO currently has two projects under implementation with the Green Climate Fund: FP099 (or Climate 
Investor One) and FP164 (or: the Green Growth Equity Fund). Alongside GCF funding, FMO also 
manages funding on behalf of the UK government (BEIS), USAID, the Dutch Ministry of Foreign Affairs, 
and the European Commission. FMO has been accredited with the Green Climate Fund since 2016, 
has signed its AMA in 2018, and its first FAA in 2019.  
Climate Fund Managers is a B.V. incorporated under the laws of The Netherlands (hereafter “Climate 
Fund Managers” or “CFM”), incorporated on 30 October 2015, headquartered in the Hague, the 
Netherlands. CFM is a joint venture between the Nederlandse Financierings-Maatschappij voor 
Ontwikkelingslanden N.V. (“FMO”) and Sanlam InfraWorks. Climate Fund Managers has successfully 
set up Climate Investor One (or: FP099) with a final close of nearly USD 1 bln. In 2019. Climate Investor

B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages) 
Worldwide 780 million people still do not have access to an improved water source. Demand shortages 
are more pronounced in Africa and Asia. Furthermore, in developing countries around 3.2 million children 
still die each year because of unsafe drinking water and poor sanitation. Waste is the fourth largest sector 
of all greenhouse gas emissions. Water and sanitation infrastructure are arguably the most important 
target sectors for climate adaptation interventions. However, adaptation finance faces a severe funding 
gap. The Economist estimates that USD 140bn to USD 300bn in climate adaptation finance is needed 
annually by 2030xxiii. It is therefore of great importance to design water and sanitation infrastructure in a 
way that it i’s: (i) robust in the face of climate-driven shocks – thereby delivering climate adaptation 
benefits and (ii) reducing GHG emissions by creating (bio)energy from the waste and wastewater that 
they treat.   
These projects, whether designed by the public or the private sector, need to be implemented in a way 
that delivers on the additional climate requirements while keeping the costs of the services affordable to 
all those in society. This means that there’s a need for both: (i) mobilizing the private sector to bridge the 
finance gap and (ii) making the services accessible and affordable for all.   
 
CI2 has been designed to do the above by blending concessional funding with private funding in two 
structured funds. The CEF2 structure mobilizes private finance by reducing the risk with a junior-equity 
tranche (the T1) and reduces construction time of the required infrastructure by providing a life-cycle 
funding approach in conjunction with the DF2. The CEF2 has been modeled in a way that accommodates 
multiple investors’ risk-return preferences while minimizing concessionality as much as possible. The 
three tranches are structured as follows:  
 
• 
Tier 1 (T1) makes up [20%] of CEF2 and is comprised of junior equity.  
• 
Tier 2 (T2) makes up [40%] of CEF2 and is comprised of ordinary equity.  
• 
Tier 3 (T3) makes up [40%] of CEF2 and is comprised of senior equity.   
 
The GCF funding will play a key role in enabling CI2 by providing a significant investment into Tier 1 for 
which funding is the scarcest. The GCF investment will therefore immediately mobilize the private sector 
in T2 and T3 by taking away some of the risks withholding commercial investors.). T1 ameliorates the 
risk associated with investing in climate-related infrastructure in developing countries to catalyze 
commercial investors. An important part of CI2’s investment mandate is LCY denominated revenues. 
LCY risk is expected to be more profound in a larger part of CI2’s portfolio than it was for CI1. Commercial 
investors are not keen on taking LCY risks in developing countries. The T1 tranche is therefore important 
to absorb and buffer any (perceptions) of LCY risks to mobilize commercial investors into T2.   
  
In addition to the investments made by the GCF funding in water and sanitation infrastructure, there is 
also an urgent need for innovative financing solutions for the emerging sector of ecosystem-based 
approaches, or green infrastructure. In this regard, the GCF funding to the DF2 will enable CI2 to develop 
and finance ecosystem projects for which there is currently a limited track record, and practically no 
interest of the private sector, creating a demonstration effect that could open this sector for future private 
sector investment.

=== PAGE 30 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 27 OF 63 
 
 
A GCF commitment to CI2 would unlock 31=4x total investment at the project level (see Investment 
Leverage Ratio below) for climate change adaptation and mitigation. CFM is applying for a total GCF 
contribution to CI2 of USD 145mn. An investment of this size provides GCF the opportunity to become 
the largest donor in the CI2 structure, potentially generating the highest additionality (and impact) of the 
CI2’s members.  Through the CI2 intervention, CFM has identified the following channels of GCF 
additionality through a CI2 intervention:  
 
• 
Investment by GCF will support CI2’s investment focus to a subset of countries, for which NOLs 
have been obtained, that have a greater need for intervention that fits within their NDCs  
  
• 
A GCF intervention in the DF2 will catalyze a facility that offers scarce early-stage funding for 
project development including high-impact ecosystem restoration projects co-located to 
commercial projects. Access to development capital is one of the main unique selling points of 
CI2. By providing scale to the DF2 facility, GCF will enable CI2 to facilitate the development of a 
greater number of projects, all of which offer CEF2 a preferential financing opportunity.   
 
B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
Sustainability of the paradigm shift   
Through organic development of developing countries’ financial markets and with a CI2 demonstration 
effect it is expected that other developers and equity funds will adopt a similar approach. Under these 
presuppositions, they are expected to fund a greater share of construction costs through an all-equity 
strategy where this is preferred over a traditional project finance approach. This may reduce costs across 
the market, both through lower equity risk premiums and by introducing lower-cost debt for projects. GCF 
funding would be instrumental to this market development through its catalytic impact in CI2 and the 
project companies in which CI2 invests.  
 
The projects and companies financed through CI2 will focus on commercially viable assets with 
inherently long-lived technology and characteristics, in the range of 20-25 years. The commercial focus 
of CI2’s investments ensures a long-term interest by owners and investors in the projects. In contrast to 
grant financed projects, satisfactory financial returns will only be realized if the projects continue to 
provide strong operational performance and goods and services that are valued by their users. By 
contrast, failure to ensure this – for example through poor maintenance – will have contractual or legal 
ramifications.  
 
The sustainability of the paradigm shift will be further imprinted by involving local banks, potentially 
including Direct Access Entities (“DAE’s), in the re-financing of the Programme’s sub-projects after they 
have become operational. 
  
Figure 2 – CI2 channels of long-term sustainability

in receipt of Construction Equity Fund financing to report in accordance with defined key performance 
indicators. In addition, the performance measurement framework indicators aforementioned will be 
incorporated into fund-level and project-level monitoring and evaluation activities that will be managed 
by CFM.

(d) Other

(max. 250 words,

The below refers to the entire Climate Investor Two program. The GCF Funding 
Proposal however only partakes in 80% and ‘GCF’s CI2’ will therefore add up to 
USD 800 mln. for the CEF2 and USD 80 mln. for the DF2. 
CFM aims to raise up to 1,100 mln in funding for CI2. This consists of USD 100 
mln. in reimbursable grants for the DF2 and USD 1,000 mln. in funding for the 
CEF2. 
CI2, being the DF2 and the CEF2, had its first close, for a total of USD 675 mln, in 
November 202111. This included investments from the European Commission, the 
Dutch Fund for Climate and Development (a climate adaptation fund from the 
Dutch government that’s managed by FMO on behalf of CFM, WNF and SNV), 
IMAS Foundation, BNG bank, Sanlam, FMO, Nordic Development Fund, and KLP. 
FMO, the AE for this proposal, has invested USD 40 mln. directly into the T2. The 
DFCD and the European Commission investments, which have both invested in 
the DF2 and the T1, are also managed by FMO on behalf of either Donor. For the 
avoidance of doubt, all onboarded investors have successfully passed the AE’s 
and EE’s KYC policies.  
As explained in sections B.1, B.3, and B.5, the CEF2 is a structured fund with 
three different tiers with different investors that have different risk/return profiles. 
The first tier, or ‘Tier 1’, constitutes as soft–equity that has been provided by 
Donors and arguably the most additional. The second tier, or ‘Tier 2’, constitutes of 
impact investors and DFI’s. The third most senior tier, or ‘’Tier 3’, constitutes of 
institutional and private investors. The ratio between the three tiers is set at 
20/40/40. Whenever one or two out of the three tiers exceed that ratio that capital 
remains ‘locked’. The total ‘unlocked’ commitments equal the value of the 
maximum value for which the 20/40/40 can be preserved.   
The initial close primarily consisted of T2 (USD 151 mln.) and T3 (USD 400 mln.) 
investments. This implicates that only USD 315 mln. of the CEF2’s capital is 
currently ‘unlocked’ (as the ‘unlocked’ value is the maximum value for which 
20/40/40 can be preserved). GCF participation in the T1 will therefore mobilize a 
large portion of private finance without requiring additional T3 investors coming in 
at later closes.

C.3.2. Does GCF funding finance technology

In some cases, the CI2 Fund will invest in companies that operate and have developed technologies in 
developed (or developing) countries, and CI2’s investment proceeds will be used to deploy such 
technologies in the target countries. The technology and knowledge will be transferred by working 
together with local partners, developers, and third parties. Lastly, CFM will work together with local 
financial institutions, including DAEs, to refinance the projects after they have successfully graduated 
from the CEF2.  
In terms of capacity building, the GCF proceeds in the DF2 can be used for the likes of feasibility 
studies, gender assessments, ESIAPs and other important capacity-building activities.
