D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA  
This section refers to the performance of the project/Programme against the investment criteria as set out in the 
GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.  
D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
• 
GHG emissions avoided: The Programme will reduce or avoid ~4.96 million tCO2e per year, for a total 
of ~ 44.65 million tCO2e over the lifetime of CI2. This consists of avoiding GHG emissions by (i) 
utilizing waste and wastewater as a source of renewable energy and waste to energy (constructing up 
to 258.74MW in Power Capacity and producing ~1824.65 GWh/yr) in total, (ii) reducing GHG 
emissions with fuel-efficient infrastructure for ports and green shipping and by (iii) reducing and/or 
sequestering GHG emissions by protecting and/or restoring ecosystems. 
 
• 
Beneficiaries: The Programme will provide ~ 11.18 million people with year-round access to reliable 
and safe water supply despite climate shocks and stresses and restored and/or protected ecosystems. 
It will also provide ~1.97 million people with resilient infrastructure for sanitation. Lastly, it will provide 
~1.82 million people with power.  
 
• 
Hectares of ecosystems protected or restored: The Programme will protect and/or restore ~1.8 million 
Ha of ecosystems. This will improve the livelihoods of local communities and reduce GHG emissions 
by protecting or restoring nature’s ability to act as a carbon sink. 
D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
A paradigm shift for climate change occurs when there is a fundamental change in the way one, or many, 
perceive and respond to a climate change issue. 
 
Climate Investor Two has the potential to cause such a paradigm shift at three levels: (i) the global 
development finance sector level, (ii) the investee country level, and (iii) at the sectoral level.  
The first shift arises from the innovation of CI2’s whole-of-life blended finance structure, further proving a 
new catalytic tool in the climate finance and sustainable development toolbox. Its application to a new 
thematic area sets a precedent for similar approaches to be implemented by other Development Finance 
Institutions, impact – and commercial investors. CI2’s whole-of-life financing approach also eliminates a 
range of barriers to low carbon and climate-resilient development by ensuring that projects can progress 
smoothly and expeditiously from a concept or pilot project to a scaled, sustainable, and replicable operation 
with a clear function beyond CI2’s exit. In addition, by demonstrating a replicable and adaptable approach 
to catalyzing private sector financing to climate-focused infrastructure, local project developers and 
international equity funds may adopt a similar approach building on CI2’s precedent. CI2’s potential to 
catalyze impact in this manner is amplified by the scarcity of infrastructure-themed climate adaptation funds, 
which despite the growing urgency for adaptation finance, can falter for a lack of established precedent. 
Bringing CI2 to market will provide recognition that climate adaptation-focused funds work in practice at 
scale, contributing to a re-balancing of the primarily mitigation themed funding available at present. 
Furthermore, as with CI1, the CI2 fund could further prove the scalability of blended finance.  
At the investee country level, in some cases, CI2 will introduce the private sector into a space dominated by 
public funding and frequently facing shortfalls – in particular water supply & distribution, and ecosystem & 
oceans assets are typically publicly financed and/or operated. By being amongst the first to catalyze private 
climate finance to these sectors, CI2 will set a replicable precedent for the private sector across its thematic 
focus. In the long-term CI2 will also create opportunities for scalability and replicability beyond its

=== PAGE 36 ===
D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 33 OF 63 
 
 
 
 
investments, through the transfer of knowledge and technical skills to local partners, and by contributing to 
improved regulatory and policy frameworks in the countries where it operates. Successfully demonstrating 
the commercial viability of ecosystems projects, that are traditionally public financed, will contribute to the 
development of proper accounting techniques for the actual economic value of ecosystems (i.e., by 
accounting for their ability to act as a ‘carbon sink’). The lack of such accounting methodologies has been 
identified as one of the biggest potential paradigm shifts for ecosystems by the GCFxviii.  
Finally, at a sectoral level, CI2’s projects will introduce innovative technologies and circular business models 
from source to sea, which will promote the preservation of water, and the climate-proofing of water 
infrastructure across the water cycle. CFM will work together with local partners, developers, and third parties 
to make sure CI2 contributes to technology and knowledge. Through the funding of sanitation projects, CI2 
will also target reductions in ground and surface water pollution, removing a fundamental barrier to water 
reuse, and encouraging value creation and greenhouse gas (CH4) capture from non-conventional waste and 
wastewater resources. Through the combination of these factors, CI2 targets long-term, systematic, and 
self-sustaining change in the sectors and regions where it is active.   
Concluding, CI2 will provide a paradigm shift by fundamentally changing the way financial markets see 
adaptation-focused blended finance funds, demonstrating the commercial viability of sectors that were 
traditionally dominated by public funding and by transferring sectoral technology and knowledge to local 
communities.  
D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
CI2 has identified the following SDGs as primary goals: 
• Climate Action (SDG 13) 
• Affordable and Clean Energy (SDG 7) 
• Clean Water and Sanitation (SDG 6) 
• Partnerships for the Goals (SDG 17) 
By contributing to the above – CI2 sees potential co-benefits in the following SDGs: 
• Good health and well-being (SDG 3). 
• Gender Equality (SDG 5) 
• Decent Work and Economic Growth (SDG 8) 
• Industry, Innovation, and Infrastructure (SDG 9) 
• Life below water (SDG 14) 
Environmental co-benefits: 
• Decontaminating the earth’s water will increase biodiversity and improve the livelihoods and resilience 
of local communities.  
Social co-benefits including health co-benefit: 
• Safe access to water and sanitation will reduce the spread and severeness of diseases related to 
contaminated water and/or lack of sanitation. 
• Development, construction, and operation of the infrastructure projects will leverage existing local 
capabilities and knowledge. 
• Community Development programs planned and implemented alongside CI2 projects will tackle core 
issues identified by stakeholders in communities where CI2 is active.

=== PAGE 37 ===
D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 34 OF 63 
 
 
 
 
Economic co-benefits: 
• The construction and operation phases of all CI2’s projects, combined with the required ESG 
compliance, will create fair jobs for local communities.  
• Demonstration of the commercial viability of infrastructure projects will lead to a capital inflow/increase 
in GDP. 
• Investing in innovative technology will lead to technological advancements.  
Gender-sensitive development benefit: 
• Gender assessments will be done for every project to ensure active inclusion of women in the entire 
sphere of influence of the project 
• Access to water and sanitation will improve the livelihoods of women (as they are now often affected in 
an inequitable manner)  
• Commitments in the Gender Action Plan will increase formal employment possibilities, improve 
working conditions, and create greater gender parity for women. This will reduce dependence on 
individual livelihood activities and enhance financial resilience for women. 
D.4. Needs of the recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
The Programme targets 18 different countries, spread over Latin America, Asia, and Africa, that all have 
significantly different scales and intensities of climate vulnerability (including water-stressed areas) . The 
Programme will therefore assess the needs, and the intervention required, on a case-by-case and per 
country basis.. The 18 countries have been identified on their National Designated Contributions (NDC) and 
the fit these have with the product offered by CI2.  A detailed climate vulnerability assessment per country 
can be found in Annex 7. 
D.5. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
FMO and CFM acknowledge that the point of departure for selecting a target/focus country is whether 
Country Ownership is encapsulated, specifically through the following three elements:  
• Host nation climate plans, including Nationally Designated Contribution (NDC) commitments (and any 
subsequent updates) and the National Adaptation Plans, as well as sector plans from relevant 
ministries, including water, sanitation, energy, environment, and others as may be applicable. The 
countries selected by CFM recognize the importance of water, waste, and renewable energy, as 
highlighted in Annex 1. The initial short-listed countries cite specific targets to combat climate change 
(with a specific focus on adaptation) across various short, medium, and long-term periods. In addition, 
such short-listed countries align with CFM’s regional office’s investment opportunities, and therefore 
CI2 has identified potential projects that could qualify for funding. 
 
• Positive and ongoing stakeholder engagement: CFM to date has built a reputable relationship with 
many of the identified country NDAs and other relevant stakeholders on the back end of receiving 
NOLs from such countries for the Climate Investor One (CI1) Programme. CI2, therefore, allows the 
CFM team to continue such engagements, with the intent to align such countries’ climate change 
needs to the investment thesis of CI2.To further demonstrate Country Ownership at the project level, 
CFM is actively engaging with governments, local communities, and the private sector. This ongoing 
engagement process is fluid; hence it is conducted to attain broad continued support within these

D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 35 OF 63 
 
 
 
 
target jurisdictions of CI2’s investment activities. This process will be tailormade per country as per the 
requests of the relevant NDA. CFM already has such processes in place with respective NDAs for CI1.  
 
 
 
• No Objection Letter (NOL) support: In a similar mechanism to Climate Investor One, CFM and FMO 
will seek governmental support in the form of a No Objection Letters (NOL) and as per the Concept 
Note have approached twenty-five (25) countries to request NOLs. This process actively started in 
January 2021 and entails active engagement with the respective NDAs and other relevant 
stakeholders to ensure an alignment of interests. A summary overview of CI2 was shared with each 
NDA, along with the Concept Note. CFM has subsequently been in regular dialogue with NDAs and 
country representatives to address any clarifications that arise and will continue to do so in preparation 
for the GCF Board meeting, with the intent to try and secure all 25 NOLs. Should NOLs not be issued 
in time, additional countries might be added to the Programme after initial Board approval, provided 
that they submit an NOL and that such NOL is approved by the Board.  
 
It is also the Managers, and AE’s, ambition to include interested Directed Access Entities (“DAE’s”) to the 
pool of lenders that will constitute the Refinancing Fund after it has been set up. This will ensure further 
alignment of the Programme and the respective country ownership.  
The D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness (max`. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
The GCF’s investment has an expected mobilization ratio of 1:4 at the fund level (as every USD of T1 will 
mobilize 4 USD in the more senior tranches). A mobilization ratio of 1:4 is on the higher end of the 
benchmark and equal to the CI1 proposalxix.   
 
The below assumes an average 66% ownership of CI2 in the construction phase and two project life cycles 
during the implementation period. 
 
GCF Leverage Ratios 
Investment leverage ratio = value of investment (total project cost) divided by the total amount of GCF 
support relating to this investment. 
 
The investment leverage ratio (referring to the total investments at project level catalyzed by the GCF 
contribution) = A / B which equals 10x whereby: 
A = the DF2 (USD 100 million) + the CEF2 (USD 1 billion) + Partner Contributions (USD 363 million) 
B = the GCF contribution (USD 145 million). 
 
Including the ‘recycled’ funding from the CEF2, this equates to 20 whereby: 
A = the DF2 (USD 180 million) + the CEF2 (USD 2 billion) + Partner Contributions (USD 727 million)  
B = the GCF contribution (USD 145 million). 
 
Private sector finance leverage ratio = amount of private sector financing in the investment project divided 
by the amount of GCF support. The private loans/equity leverage ratio (referring to the total investments 
made by the private sector catalyzed by the GCF contribution) = A / B which equals 16x whereby: 
 
A = Private sector investors in CEF2 recycled (USD 800 million + USD 800 million) + private sector partners 
at project level recycled (USD 727mn). 
B = the GCF contribution (USD 145mn). 
CI2’s primary efficiency and effectiveness benefits are derived from its whole of life financing approach:

=== PAGE 39 ===
D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 36 OF 63 
 
 
 
 
• Funding made available by CI2 at all stages of the project including early-stage development and for 
pilot schemes, generates a pipeline of projects whilst building the capacity of local developers.  
 
• The recycling character of CI2 enables more projects to get developed and constructed during the 
implementation period of the fund (the capital is expected to be recycled twice). 
 
• The whole of life approach reduces the time project developers usually lose at fundraising at the 
different phases of project development. 
 
• Flexibility to use all-equity financing during the construction phase of the project. This eliminates cash 
flow and financial distress from the project during construction, whilst also ensuring the long-term 
interest of the owners and investors in the project.  
 
• Leaner capital expenditure of CI2 projects through exclusive technology partnership agreements with 
Original Equipment Manufacturers (OEMs), unlocking economies of scale for equipment sourcing, 
installation, and maintenance.

E.2. GCF Impact level: Paradigm shift potential (max 600 words, approximately 1-2 pages) 
Assessment 
Dimension 
Current state (baseline) 
Potential target scenario 
(Description) 
How the project/Programme will contribute 
(Description) 
Description 
Rating 
Scale (size of 
projects)  
The water, sanitation, and ocean sectors 
have traditionally been primarily financed 
within the public domain. There’s therefore 
often insufficient private finance available 
as private investors still perceive the 
sectors as too risky (i.e., because of a lack 
of projects that demonstrated bankability). 
This makes it difficult for projects, 
especially larger-scale projects, in the 
water, sanitation, and oceans sectors to 
receive enough funding. This is reflected in 
the average transaction size in the water 
and sanitation sector (an average of USD 
76 mln. versus USD 301 mln. for all 
blended finance). 
Medium 
The potential target scenario would be a 
situation in which projects within the water, 
oceans, and sanitation infrastructure sectors 
in developing countries can reach enough 
scale as investors become more comfortable 
with the bankability and commercial viability 
of the (larger) projects. 
The Programme will contribute to the potential 
target scenario by (i) mobilizing private 
finance at scale by offering a blended finance 
solution that caters to investors with different 
risk to return profiles and (ii) by reducing the 
perceived risks of investing in the water, 
sanitation, and oceans sectors by successfully 
developing and constructing bankable large- 
scale projects (demonstration effect) 
 
Replicability (of CI2 / 
blended finance 
within the Sectors) 
The water and sanitation sector has strong 
potential to strategically adopt innovative 
finance approaches like blended finance 
beyond CI2. Access to safe drinking water 
and sanitation is a basic human right and 
critical to ensuring human welfare and 
Medium 
The potential target scenario would be a 
situation in which a precedent is set whereby 
CI2 demonstrates the application of blended 
finance as a strategic option for the water and 
sanitation sector. In doing so, this could be 
further replicated by other entities, triggering 
the application of innovative financing 
The Programme will aim to help achieve the 
potential target scenario by replicating the 
successful, and replicable, structure of 
Climate Investor One, in the Water, 
Sanitation, and Oceans Sectors. This could 
take form in different ways: (i) by establishing 
a precedent for blended finance at scale in the 
water, sanitation, and oceans sectors, (ii) by

unlocking economic growth and 
productivity. 
Goal 6 (Clean Water & Sanitation) of the 
Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) 
aims to ensure access to safe water 
sources and sanitation for all; however, the 
World Bank estimates that 114 USD bln per 
year would be required to meet this target. 
Despite this financing shortfall, the water 
and sanitation sector has not been as quick 
as other sectors to adopt alternative 
financing sources, including blended 
finance approaches to mobilize commercial 
capital. 
solutions to address the 114 USD bln shortfall 
which currently exists. 
replicating the all-equity full lifecycle approach 
of the Construction Equity Fund and (iii) by 
creating opportunities for co-investing 
alongside CI2.  
 
Sustainability 
The water, sanitation, and oceans 
infrastructure in developing countries are 
currently insufficiently equipped to use their 
potential for avoiding GHG emissions while 
dealing with the consequences of climate 
change. 
 
Medium 
The target scenario is a situation in which the 
infrastructure developed and built remains 
operational and resilient even after the 
implementation period of the Programme. 
CI2 will focus on commercially viable assets 
with inherently long-lived technology and 
characteristics, in the range of 20-25 years. 
CI2 will ensure strong operational 
performance and goods and services that are 
valued and affordable by and for the users. 
Furthermore, the assets will cause a 
knowledge transfer and demonstration effect.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting, and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
 
Monitoring & Reporting 
CFM reports on the investments done and financial performance of CI2’s portfolio to all investors, including all Donors, on a quarterly basis. On an 
annual basis, a report will be provided that includes audited financial accounts and detailed information on investments, investment valuations, 
fees, and the impact indicators. In addition, the Accredited Entity, supported by CFM, will provide the GCF with an Annual Progress Report as 
mandated by the GCF.  
 
Financial reporting:

=== PAGE 51 ===
E 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 48 OF 63 
 
 
 
 
 
Within sixty (60) calendar days after the end of each calendar quarter ended 31 March, 30 June, and 30 September, CFM shall prepare and 
provide all Investors with a quarterly report consisting of, inter alia: 
• 
An unaudited profit and loss account, balance sheet, and cash flow statement of the Programme for the preceding quarter; 
• 
Explanatory notes; 
• 
An overview of the investment activity undertaken by the Programme within the reporting period; 
• 
An overview of each project company, including: 
1. 
A description of the Project Company's business; 
2. 
The economic interest of the Programme in the Project Company; 
3. 
The percentage of the Project Company owned by the Fund; 
4. 
A discussion of recent key events; 
5. 
Selected financial information; 
6. 
If the Investment has been repaid or realized, the realized liquidation or disposal proceeds; and, 
• 
Quarterly capital account statements.  
Within one hundred and twenty (120) calendar days after the end of each financial year the Manager shall prepare and provide all Investors with 
an annual report (the "Annual Report") consisting of, inter alia: 
• 
The annual accounts of the Programme, comprising at least a balance sheet, a profit, and loss account and explanatory notes thereto, as 
audited by the Auditor; 
• 
A report of the Auditor on its findings resulting from its audit of the annual accounts;  
• 
An overview of each Project Company acquired sold, or otherwise disposed of during the reporting period; 
• 
A valuation of each Investment; 
• 
A report on KPIs; 
• 
A report on impact indicators 
• 
A breakdown of fees received by CFM during the reporting period; 
• 
A breakdown of the Programme's operating costs during the reporting period; 
• 
A schedule of Drawdowns and distributions effectuated during the reporting period; 
• 
The total amount of the Undrawn Commitments as per the end of the reporting period; and, 
• 
A report detailing the activities of the Programme during the reporting period.

=== PAGE 52 ===
E 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 49 OF 63 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Environmental & Social Reporting 
An important aspect of CFM’s risk management process is the ongoing monitoring and review of each investment. Depending on which Fund that 
investment is drawing from (Development or Construction Equity), different monitoring requirements will be applied. Monitoring is commensurate 
with the risk categorization, with physical visits to the location(s) of higher-risk projects during construction.  
All project companies drawing from the CEF2 are also required to monitor the performance of any contractors employed at the project site(s) and to 
participate in a Management Review held by CFM periodically during the lifecycle of the Fund to review overall E&S performance.  
In addition, CFM will also ensure that any legal requirements for monitoring, for example, that may be included in permits and licenses for 
development, construction, and operation, are incorporated into the project monitoring and evaluation plan. CFM requires all projects in receipt of 
CEF2 are to report in accordance with defined key performance indicators (presented below). In addition, the performance measurement framework 
indicators presented in H1.1 and H1.2 will be incorporated into fund-level and project-level monitoring and evaluation activities that will be managed 
by CFM.  
Evaluations 
The Accredited Entity, together with CFM, will arrange for an interim and final evaluation to be outsourced to an independent evaluator after 10 and 
20 years, respectively. The interim evaluation will focus on the extent to which CI2 is achieving its goals regarding the number of – mention GCF 
impact indicators -. The final evaluation will constitute an ex-post effectiveness study. The evaluation will focus on the DAC criteria, which focus on 
relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, impact, and sustainability and the GCF’s evaluation criteria.  
• 
Relevance: the extent to which the investments are suited to the priorities and policies of CI2 
• 
Effectiveness: the extent to which the objectives of CI2 were achieved  
• 
Efficiency: the extent to which activities were cost-efficient and timely  
• 
Impact: determining the positive and negative changes produced by CI2, directly or indirectly, intended or unintended.  
• 
Sustainability: the extent to which the investments of CI2 are likely to continue operating after CI2 has exited.

Currency Risk 
The risk is that future cash receipts/payments will decrease/increase due to adverse movement in exchange rates. 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
• 
Most off-take agreements contracted by CI2 at the asset level are directly or indirectly denominated in USD leading to an 
influx of USD (or equivalent) amounts. 
• 
It is therefore estimated that most off-take agreements will be indexed to USD currencies. If deemed desirable/necessary, 
CI2 has the option to explore hedging opportunities made available by local markets or by TCX (the Currency Exchange, 
a hedging mechanism that enjoys wide support from the DFI community including FMO, EBRD and KfW) 
• 
In principle, CFM may only enter into futures, options, or other derivative transactions for the purpose of, and only to the 
extent required for, hedging currency positions on non-USD Investments. CFM is not obliged to do this, however 
• 
A hedging policy may be adopted in respect of non-USD currency commitments for imminent acquisitions or divestments, 
considered on a project-by-project basis or hedging of short-term distributions 
• 
Distributions and other cash flows from assets are not hedged

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS 
G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)  
Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) 
CFM, in cooperation with, and as approved by, the AE, has developed an Environmental and Social 
Management System (“ESMS”) to manage impacts and risks associated with the Funds under its 
management. The ESMS manual has been disclosed in accordance with the GCF’s ex-ante disclosure 
requirements for category A/I-1 projects/Programmes. The document, provided in Annex 6, describes the 
requirements, processes and arrangements required to comply with the requirements of the Responsible 
Investment Code (“RIC”) and to uphold the commitments set out in CFM’s Responsible Investment Policy 
(“RIP”). It describes how the arrangements are embedded into CFM’s investment lifecycle (from 
development to operational). It also describes the environmental and social (“E&S”) management 
measures that need to be adopted by the Project Companies. The ESMS has been designed in 
accordance with IFC Performance Standard 1 (“PS 1”) and other international standards including ISO 
14001 and ISO 45001 as well as the AE’s Sustainability Policy Universe.  
The structure the ESMS is presented in the figure below and summarized as follows:  
• 
Sections 1 and 2: Following an introductory section, section 2 of the ESMS outlines the context and 
scope, with a brief presentation of the CFM Managed Vehicles and the potential E&S impacts 
associated with them, a description of the key stakeholders with their needs and expectations and the 
scope of application of the ESMS.  
• 
Section 3 presents the senior accountability in place for oversight of the ESMS, the resources assigned 
for its implementation with their roles and responsibilities and how the right level of expertise and 
capacity is ensured. 
• 
Sections 4 and Chapter 5 describes CFM’s policies, principles, commitments, and E&S requirements 
that form the overall Responsible Investment Framework.  
• 
Chapter 6 describes how CFM operationalizes the RIP across the investment cycle through risk 
management and delivering positive impacts. 
• 
Chapter 7 presents the system in place for monitoring and periodically reviewing the effectiveness of the 
ESMS, through the definition of E&S performance indicators and impact indicators, audits, and 
management review.  
• 
Chapter 8 outlines the arrangements in place for communication with external stakeholders, including 
reporting to investors, public disclosure, and responsiveness to grievances.  
• 
The ESMS also includes a set of Annexures, including Annex 2 (List of Excluded Activities); Annex 4 
(Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework), and Annex 5 (Resettlement Planning Framework).

=== PAGE 60 ===
F 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 57 OF 63 
 
 
 
 
 
E&S Governance and Management 
FMO, as the AE, plays a central role in CFM’s governance. Specifically in relation to E&S governance and 
management CFM has committed to FMO as AE to: (i) implement and maintain an ESMS to the 
satisfaction of FMO, (ii) appoint or acquire services of ESG specialists, (iii) submit annual reports including 
E&S performance and (iv) comply with all relevant GCF policies and procedures.  
CFM, as the EE, has a designated Head of ESG that oversees and implements all E&S commitments and 
responsibilities. The Head of ESG directly reports to the MB and is part of the Executive Committee. 
Reporting to the Head of ESG is a broader team of ESG professionals. The team will be expanded as 
necessary to ensure adequate resourcing.  
In addition, CFM will: (i) hire 
external E&S advisors to support 
with due diligence, specialist studies 
and ongoing monitoring; and (ii) 
partner with organizations (such as 
CSOs, development organizations, 
research organization and training 
institutions) to deliver specialist 
programmes including those 
focused on delivering community 
development interventions. 
Furthermore, all CFM Investment 
Staff have a responsibility for 
ensuring that all projects are 
developed and implemented according to CFM’s requirements as set out in the ESMS.  
At the project level, CFM will require the project to manage E&S risks as set out in the ESMS. Each project 
will ensure they have a project level ESMS as well as adequate staffing and resources. CFM’s ESG team 
will work closely together with the project company to ensure compliance.

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 58 OF 63 
 
 
 
 
E&S Risk Management  
CFM’s approach to responsible investment is enshrined in the RIC which guides all investment decisions 
and management activities relating to the CFM Managed Vehicles. The principles and undertakings of the 
RIC are reinforced by a set of commitments which are contained in the RIP. These commitments are 
implemented through the arrangements described in the ESMS Manual and supporting documentation. 
The underpinning ethos is of managing risk and delivering positive benefits in line with legal requirements, 
and good international industry practices. This is presented schematically below.  
ESG considerations are fully integrated into the investment process, starting with providing inputs into the 
mandate fit stage of a potential investment, through screening and due diligence, to project development, 
construction, operation, and final exit. Each investment is assessed to confirm the potential for adverse 
impacts and risks, and this leads to a preliminary E&S risk categorization. As a general rule, Category A 
projects will not be pursued unless sufficient evidence is available to meet the conditions outlined in the 
ESMS manual. The risk category will inform the scope of the development activities that will be required, 
including the commissioning of an environmental and social impact assessment (“ESIA”) for all Category A 
and B projects. Mitigation, management, and monitoring measures will be defined during the development 
phase for adoption by the Project Company from the start of the construction phase. Section 6 of the 
ESMS manual provides a description of the way in which E&S impacts and risks are assessed and 
integrated into the investment and decision-making process. 
In addition to managing adverse impacts and risk, CFM is 
committed to maximizing opportunities for delivering 
environmental and social benefits, particularly to the local 
communities in the area of influence of its projects. These 
benefits are designed to be additional to the intrinsic 
positive impacts of the investments and are focused on 
local actions that can be taken to support progress 
towards achieving selected UN SDGs (refer to section 
D.3). Budget is committed to community development 
during the development, construction, and operational 
phases of an asset. A needs-based community 
development programme will be established during the 
development phase of the project, and the Project 
company will be required to implement it under the 
guidance of CFM. The programmes are typically 
delivered by development partners with the local network, 
local knowledge, and the expertise required to deliver 
successful outcomes. 
Human Rights 
CFM recognizes the centrality of human rights to sustainable development and ensuring fair distribution of 
development and opportunities and benefits. CFM is committed to operating in accordance with all legal 
requirements and agreements, including the ILO Core Conventions and the UN’s Guiding Principles on 
Business and Human Rights. These principles have been implemented within CFM, eliminating all forms of 
unfair discrimination in the recruitment and selection of staff, and within all project companies, suppliers

=== PAGE 62 ===
F 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 59 OF 63 
 
 
 
 
and other companies CFM has control or influence over.  Project companies will be required to provide 
information relating to their suppliers. 
Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment  
CFM’s gender policy, as described in G.1 and annexed to the FP, details the commitments and 
arrangements for achieving gender equality and women’s empowerment within the business and in 
relation to the projects. CFM recognizes that women have as much to gain from its projects (and 
particularly from increased access to energy and water and sanitation resources) as men but given their 
relative social position, will likely not profit as men from the access to energy, water, and sanitation 
resources. CFM considers women as employees, end-users and beneficiaries of its projects and requires 
risks and opportunities to be assessed and managed in accordance with a local gender action plan (see 
G.2) as well as in accordance with other E&S safeguards as described in the ESMS manual.  
Of particular note is the issue of gender-based violence and harassment to which members of the Project 
workforce and those in the local communities can be exposed. CFM therefore requires all projects to 
implement measures to safeguard female members of its workforce as well as in the broader communities 
it serves. This includes consideration of the risks associated with gender-based violence at all stages of 
the investment lifecycle, adoption of controls to avoid cases and measures to respond to and address 
reported or suspected cases. The grievance mechanism adopted by every project will make specific 
provisions for a survivor-centered approach as well as providing the necessary support to witnesses and 
others such as whistleblowers, with those involved in managing the case receiving appropriate training in 
line with international guidance. 
Indigenous Peoples 
CFM recognizes the critical importance of respecting the rights of Indigenous Peoples (“IP”) and of 
avoiding any adverse impacts on the IPs which may arise because of a Project. CFM will avoid proceeding 
with a Project beyond the Development Phase (activity 2.1.) where adverse impacts cannot be avoided. 
When avoidance is not feasible, CFM requires measures to be put in place to minimize and mitigate these 
impacts. Compensation shall only be considered as a last resort. Annex 4 of the ESMS manual describes 
CFM’s Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework (“IPPF”) which is designed to guide all projects in terms of 
the minimum requirements wherever IPs are identified in a project area of influence. The IPPF is informed 
by the requirements of IFC PS7 and the associated guidance note, and the Green Climate Fund IPP and 
associated Operational Guidelines. 
Corporate Grievance Mechanism 
CFM provides a fund-broad grievance mechanism for external stakeholders on its website. This grievance 
mechanism is intended for use by stakeholders such as NGOs and CSOs, government representatives, 
investors, and the wider public. Grievance mechanisms are also implemented by the Project Companies 
and are intended for use by the Project affected communities. All grievance mechanisms must be 
designed and operated in accordance with the Effectiveness Criteria detailed in Principle 31 of the United 
Nations Guiding Principles on Business and Human Rights. All grievance mechanisms will also consider 
gender-sensitivity and provide a survivor centered-approach for responding to reported cases of gender-
based violence and sexual harassment. 
G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

=== PAGE 63 ===
F 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 60 OF 63 
 
 
 
 
CFM recognizes that the projects can only be successful if they are gender sensitive. The use of the term 
‘gender’ refers to the social relations between women and men; thereby the need to work with women and 
men to ensure both equitably benefit. It is further recognized that women have as much to gain from its 
projects (and particularly from increased access to water and sanitation) as men but given their relative 
social position, will likely not profit as men from the access to energy, water, and sanitation resources. 
CFM, in cooperation with the AE, has developed a Gender Action Plan (“GAP”) to present an overview of 
relevant gender-related issues in the water, sanitation, and oceans sectors in the 25 targeted countries. 
The GAP has been developed based on country assessment and analysis, with close reference to the 
approach described in the GCF guidance document for mainstreaming gender in projects. The GAP will be 
supplemented by local analysis of gender issues and opportunities, and a local gender action plan that 
aims to address these challenges and opportunities. The Project company will be required to implement 
this action plan throughout the lifecycle of every asset (and will form part of the handover at the exit of an 
investment). 
The GAP is intended to support the Manager in fulfilling its commitments to responsible investment in 
accordance with the Responsible Investment Framework and CI2’s Responsible Investment Policy. In 
particular, the GAP is based on the Framework’s commitment to “Do No Harm” and “Do Good” and the 
policy’s commitments. These include “avoiding and otherwise minimizing and mitigating the impact of its 
investments on local communities” and “maximizing opportunities for positive social benefits to be 
realized”. In this sense, the GAP provides a gender lens to the arrangements that already exist for 
addressing the social dimensions of CI2 financing. The GAP will include an (estimated) budget The AE 
and the EE also confirm that the budget would be readjusted in case it would be deemed insufficient during 
the implementation of CI2. 
The gender assessments and the full Gender Action Plan are to be provided as Annex 8. 
G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
The financial management is done at two levels: (i) the AE (FMO) and (ii) the EE (CFM). On the AE’s level, 
the AE will enter into the Funding Activity Agreement (FAA) with the GCF. The AE will oversee the 
fiduciary management of the GCF’s funding in its GCF account and on a ledger specific to CI2. The AE will 
request the GCF funds, based on the funding needs as predicted by CFM, on a bi-annual basis. The AE 
will then disburse these funds to the DF2 whenever CFM issues a drawdown request. CFM will not 
manage any unused funds of the GCF. The drawdown requests and payments at FMO will follow FMO’s 
standard policies and procedures for PE fund investment.  
Once received by CFM, the funds will be administered by CFM including drawdowns from and distributions 
to investors, as well as all fund-related accounting will be instigated, overseen, and performed by CFM (as 
asset manager). CFM has an Authorization and Payments Policy in place, which details the process of 
authorizing and making payments to external parties, including the various levels of internal authorizations 
required in order to ensure a sound control environment. CFM has controlled access to the CIO Fund bank 
accounts and is responsible for initiating and releasing payments according to the Authorization and 
Payments Policy and generally in accordance with the CI2 Funds’ governing documentation. All investor 
reporting is controlled and managed by CFM. A monthly review of the trial balance is performed by the 
CFM Finance Manager and quarterly and annual reports are prepared by CFM. The CI2 Funds’ and CFM’s 
accounts will be audited annually by PwC. Accounts will be prepared according to the International 
Financial Reporting Standards (IFRS) and will consist of, among others, a balance sheet, profit and loss 
account, explanatory notes, auditor’s report, an overview of project turnover activity, breakdown of

=== PAGE 64 ===
F 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 61 OF 63 
 
 
 
 
management fees paid, breakdown of fund operating costs, etc. CFM will require the project companies to 
prepare accounts in accordance with appropriately recognized financial reporting standards and each 
project company is required to appoint an Auditor. CFM’s financial reports will be subject to FMO review 
and approval through FMO’s participation on CFM’s supervisory board & CFM’s Audit, Risk, and 
Compliance Committee. 
The AE's approved procurement policy will be applied, in addition to CFMs, throughout the programme 
including in relation to activities implemented by the EE. Please refer to Annex 10, Procurement Plan, for 
further details on procurement thresholds and requirements  
G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal  
 
☐ No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may 
be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence. 
☒ With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may 
not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited 
entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes: 
• 
full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with 
an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the 
accredited entity’s disclosure policy, and 
• 
redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.  
The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above if containing confidential 
information.

Remarks 
An Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) 
consistent with the requirement for a Category I-1 
programme is contained in the “Environmental and Social 
Management System (ESMS) Manual”.* Climate Fund 
Managers (CFM) is the fund manager for the Climate 
Investor Two (CI2) fund. As co-founder and initiator of CI2, 
FMO has also taken a significant ownership stake in CFM. 
Considering (i) the status of FMO being a 50 percent 
shareholder of CFM, as well as (ii) the fact that all previous 
E&S safeguards-related information on CI2 has been 
published on the CI2 and CFM websites, the present ESMS 
disclosure is being done via the CI2 and CFM websites 
instead of the FMO website. Links to the CI2 and CFM 
websites may be found on FMO’s CI2 web page. For further 
details on the ownership role of FMO in relation to CFM, as 
well as on the overall involvement of FMO in CI2 as an 
initiator and investor, please refer to the below link to the 
FMO website:  https://www.fmo.nl/governance-related-to-
cio-and-cfm

Large 
I. 
 Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel  
1.1 
   Impact potential   
 
 
  
        Scale: Medium 
1. 
Overview: Climate Investor Two (CI2) intends to develop and construct 
infrastructure projects in developing countries in the water, sanitation and ocean sectors that 
reduce the effects and consequences of climate change by mitigating greenhouse gas (GHG) 
emissions and by increasing resilience. The CI2 structure builds on and repurposes the multi-
tranche blended finance approach utilized by Climate Investor One (FP099) (approved by the 
Board at its twenty-first meeting (B.21)) while targeting new sectors. CI2 seeks to accomplish 
the programme’s objectives by mobilizing public investments and unlocking private capital 
across the following sectors: 
• 
Water: The water sector is defined by projects that source, transport and treat water 
from municipalities and other sectors. Climate adaptation benefits include building 
infrastructure that sources, transports and treats the water necessary for both 
municipal and industrial users in countries/subnational regions undergoing or 
expected to undergo water stress because of a changing climate; 
• 
Sanitation: The sanitation sector looks at treating waste and wastewater to avoid and 
sequester methane emissions and increase water availability in areas experiencing 
increasing water stress as a result of climate change;  
• 
Ocean: The oceans and ecosystems sector aims to (a) reduce GHG emissions from and 
enhance the resilience of ports and shipping; and (b) protect, strengthen and restore 
important ecosystems to safeguard their services in buffering droughts, floods and 
storm surges intensified by climate change. 
2. 
CI2 intends to deploy a whole-of-life financing approach utilizing two independent 
but operationally interlinked funds. The two funds focus on different stages of the project 
lifecycle: a Development Fund (DF) provides reimbursable grants to early-stage climate 
projects, whereas the Construction Equity Fund (CEF) provides follow-on finance during the 
construction phase. The DF has been set up to provide development loans (in the form of 
reimbursable grants), accompanied by technical assistance provided by Climate Fund 
Managers (CFM) and third parties, to project companies. The purpose of this is to fast-track 
qualifying development projects to financial close by reducing the development timeline and 
improving the ultimate bankability of infrastructure projects. Once projects successfully 
complete their development phase, CEF reimburses the grant to DF, and the project moves on 
to the construction phase. In this way, CEF ensures that DF remains capable of recycling its 
capital.  
3. 
CEF has been set up to finance the construction phase by providing up to 75 per 
cent of the required project equity. The CEF equity approach is designed to address a

=== PAGE 102 ===
GCF/B.33/02/Add.04 
Page 99 
 
 
structural barrier of many local banks and lending institutions being unwilling to assume 
construction risk. It further aims to decrease the construction time and costs by: (i) reducing 
the complexity and costs at the project level through an equity-only funding strategy; (ii) 
reducing the construction timeline and thereby reducing the exposure period to investors; 
(iii) reducing costs by eliminating certain debt-specific costs; and (iv) recycling capital from 
completed projects to new projects to reduce the time any funding ‘stands still’.  
4. 
CEF itself is structured as a blended finance vehicle to better mobilize commercial 
capital at scale into CEF. It is made up of a multi-tiered structure to accommodate multiple 
investors’ risk/return preferences. The three tranches are structured as follows:  
Tier 1 makes up 20 per cent of CEF and is comprised of junior equity; 
Tier 2 makes up 40 per cent of CEF and is comprised of ordinary equity; and  
Tier 3 makes up 40 per cent of CEF and is comprised of senior equity.  
5. 
The CI2 funding proposal requests a total GCF contribution of USD 145 million, of 
which USD 45 million would be directed to DF and USD 100 million to Tier 1 of CEF. The 
expected capital size of the prospective funds is estimated at USD 90 million for DF and USD 1 
billion for CEF. GCF funding would play a key catalytic role in enabling CI2 by providing 
junior Tier 1 capital at concessional rates (0 per cent + inflation Consumer Price Index (CPI)). 
GCF participation in Tier 1 is intended to reduce investment risks for the larger Tier 2 and 
Tier 3 tranches and thus mobilize private sector finance into the facility. For instance, the 
junior status of Tier 1 capital ameliorates risks associated with investing in climate-related 
infrastructure in developing countries such as local currency fluctuation risk. This facilitates 
CI2’s ability to invest in projects where revenues are denominated in local currency while at 
the same time mitigate real and perceived local currency risk on the part of commercial 
investors.  
6. 
This programme would be GCF’s first at-scale private sector programme in the 
water and sanitation sector. The programme aims to mobilize capital at scale. The USD 145 
million investment by GCF will potentially catalyse USD 945 million at the fund level, and 
with further leverage assumed at the project level, the total capital expected to be mobilized 
will be higher.  
7. 
The Dutch Entrepreneurial Development Bank (FMO) will act as the accredited 
entity (AE) for this proposal. CFM, a joint venture between FMO and Sanlam, will act as the 
fund manager of CI2 and is the first executing entity (EE). DF and CEF, the two funds within 
CI2, will act as the second and third EEs. 
8. 
Impacts: CI2 aims to make investments which result in the scaling and replication 
of water, sanitation and ocean infrastructure that is aligned with the adaptation and 
mitigation needs of developing countries. The investments themselves are expected to 
generate measurable climate impacts and are also intended to have a market demonstration 
effect. CI2 contends that the financial and impact success of low-emission, climate-resilient 
sustainable development in host countries, with a risk-adjusted return, will catalyse 
commercial investors through demonstration.  
9. 
The water cycle is a key component of the global climate crisis. The World Bank 
estimates that by 2050, water shortages caused by climate change could reduce the gross 
domestic product (GDP) of developing countries in Asia by USD 4.4 trillion and developing 
countries in Africa by USD 1.6 trillion, compounding existing health and development 
challenges. Water can also be a substantial carbon emitter when managed improperly, with 
methane emissions from untreated waste and wastewater accounting for 7 per cent of global 
GHG emissions. Riverine, wetland, marine and coastal ecosystems have the potential to act as 
‘carbon sinks’ that sequester and avoid carbon emissions, and restoring them can enhance 
their services in buffering against climate change-intensified disasters.

=== PAGE 103 ===
GCF/B.33/02/Add.04 
Page 100 
 
 
10. 
The financing of the water, sanitation and ocean sectors has primarily been within 
the public domain. However, the budgets that developing countries have available to invest in 
these sectors are insufficient for the task at hand. Adequately addressing the lack of low-
emission water infrastructure that would contribute to staying within the 1.5-degree 
pathway while ensuring resilient water supply in areas experiencing reduced water 
availability because of climate change will require the mobilization of private sector finance. 
Traditional private investors, however, perceive the sectors as high-risk, particularly in 
developing countries.  
11. 
CI2’s two-fund blended approach is intended to address these risks (real and 
perceived) by both (i) providing private sector investors with a blended finance solution that 
reduces their risks; and (ii) demonstrating the commercial viability of water, sanitation and 
ocean infrastructure projects by demonstrating the possibility of risk-adjusted returns to 
investors. This can help investors to see the long-term benefits of investing in such sectors 
and countries and mitigate some of the perceived high risks.  
12. 
Furthermore, highlighting the commercial viability of water, sanitation and ocean 
infrastructure projects will help provide know-how and capacity for the development and 
implementation of such projects in the host countries by providing a transfer of knowledge to 
local parties. CI2 aims to do this by including local partners and developers in both the 
development as well as the construction phases of its projects. Lastly, it will aim to include 
local financial intermediaries, including direct access entities, in the refinancing of its 
projects. 
13. 
Successfully developing and implementing mitigation and adaptation projects for 
the water, sanitation and ocean sectors in developing countries will involve: (i) improving 
wastewater treatment capacity; (ii) improving access to water; (iii) improving access to 
sanitation; (iv) improving renewable energy capacity and generation; (v) improving access to 
fuel-efficient green ports and shipping; and (vi) restoring and/or protecting riverine, 
wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems. In terms of mitigation potential, improving 
wastewater treatment and capacity, renewable energy capacity and generation, and access to 
fuel-efficient green ports and shipping, and restoring and/or protecting riverine, wetland, 
coastal and marine ecosystems will then result in reduced GHG emissions in the water, 
sanitation and oceans sectors of host countries. With regard to mitigation benefits, CI2 is 
expected to reduce or avoid approx. 6 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO2eq) 
per year for a total of approx. 56million tCO2eq over the implementation period. 
14. 
In terms of adaptation potential, developing and constructing additional water 
supply, treatment, distribution and wastewater treatment facilities, as well as enhancing 
ecological infrastructure, will enable CI2 to reduce the vulnerability of developing countries 
to climate hazards. CI2 aims to construct up to USD 3.7 billion in infrastructure projects in 
developing countries. The projects financed, developed and constructed by CI2 are expected 
to provide approx. 14 million people with year-round access to reliable and safe water 
supplies despite climate shocks and stresses. They will also protect and strengthen 
ecosystems covering approx. 900,000 hectares to help them adapt to climate variability and 
change.  
15. 
CI2 is also expected to contribute to technology transfer by proactively 
cooperating with local partners and developers during project development as well as during 
the the construction of the infrastructure assets. 
16. 
It is important that all investment projects in which GCF funds are used to take an 
equity stake in CI2 do ultimately deliver on the specific climate change adaptation and 
mitigation impacts sought in the funding proposal. All potential investment projects claiming 
climate change adaptation impacts must go through a robust climate adaptation assessment 
at the level of investee, activity and/or site prior to any decision on investment through DF

=== PAGE 104 ===
GCF/B.33/02/Add.04 
Page 101 
 
 
and CEF. With regard to the broad way in which the funding proposal discusses interwoven 
development and climate challenges, the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) 
identified a risk that a shortage of viable deals could lead to low standards being applied by 
CFM in the selection of investment projects aimed at adaptation, with CI2 ultimately funding 
business-as-usual water and sanitation investment projects that lack any clearly motivated, 
specifically contextualized climate adaptation logic. In response to questions from the 
independent TAP on this, the AE highlighted the screening process the Climate Investor Two 
Development Fund Investment Committee will use to judge proposals with intended 
adaptation benefits: “The deal teams will provide [the Climate Investor Two Development 
Fund Investment Committee] with historic climate trends (observed changes or variability in 
changes in the project areas), climate change projections in the areas, and climate impact (an 
explanation on how these changes will hurt communities in the project area). Adaptation 
benefits will be further addressed by explaining how the project company’s products, 
services, or technology will help the community adapt to these climate impacts, an 
explanation of how the proposed intervention will not lead to maladaptation, an explanation 
of the Rio Marker score for climate adaptation (to be achieved by the company) and an 
overview of the impact indicators that will be monitored to validate the above (including an 
identification of the baseline and mid-term (year 10) and final (year 20) projections).” 
17. 
Overall impact potential is assessed as Medium. The Medium rating is conservative 
and reflects pipeline risk (Do these project opportunities turn into concrete investments?) 
and execution risk. CFM demonstrated its capacity to manage a similar vehicle in Climate 
Investor One, but migrating to new and arguably more challenging sectors will require new 
capacity and expertise, leading us to hedge our impact rating somewhat.  
1.2 
Paradigm shift potential  
 
 
    Scale: Medium 
18. 
The focus of the funding proposal is an innovative investment scheme enabling 
new investors with no or minimal track record in developing markets to enter new sectors 
previously considered as high risk (water, sanitation, oceans), accelerating the development 
and implementation of projects and reducing costs across the entire process. CI2 provides 
different types of technical support to the private sector at the development stage as well as 
ready investment for the construction and operational stages, all of which are fundamental 
elements of the investment scheme and might be considered innovative for the targeted host 
countries.  
19. 
CI2 aims to create a paradigm shift by making investments which result in the 
scaling and replication of water, sanitation and ocean infrastructure that is aligned with the 
adaptation and mitigation needs of developing countries. These are sectors that are perceived 
as risky or better suited for public finance, especially in developing countries. CI2 has the 
paradigm shifting potential of introducing the private sector into a space dominated by public 
funding, although the success of these efforts will depend on participating countries’ policy 
and regulatory environments. Additionally, CI2 has the potential to demonstrate the actual 
versus perceived risk of climate-beneficial investments in the water sector and blue 
infrastructure generally. Data and learning from CI2 has the potential to change the way 
financial markets see these sectors by demonstrating the commercial viability and 
transferring sectoral technology and knowledge to local communities. 
20. 
Moreover, CI2’s whole-of-life financing approach itself is paradigm shifting by 
eliminating a series of barriers to low-carbon and climate-resilient development by ensuring 
that projects can progress smoothly and expeditiously from a concept or pilot project to a 
scaled, sustainable and replicable operation. CI2’s structure is a useful catalytic tool, as 
demonstrated by Climate Investor One’s success and now by the willingness and appetite of 
CFM and its investors to shift into more commercially difficult sectors with CI2. Its

=== PAGE 105 ===
GCF/B.33/02/Add.04 
Page 102 
 
 
application to a new thematic area sets a precedent for similar approaches to be implemented 
by other institutions and in other sectors. By demonstrating a replicable and adaptable 
approach to catalysing private sector financing of climate-focused infrastructure, local project 
developers and international equity funds may adopt a similar approach, building on CI2’s 
precedent.  
21. 
CI2’s potential to catalyse impact in this manner is amplified by the scarcity of 
infrastructure-themed climate adaptation funds, which, despite the growing urgency for 
adaptation finance, can falter for a lack of established precedent. Bringing CI2 to market will 
provide recognition that climate adaptation-focused funds can work in practice at scale, 
contributing to a rebalancing of the primarily mitigation-themed funding available at present.  
22. 
In the longer term, CI2 will also create opportunities for scalability and 
replicability beyond its investments, through the transfer of knowledge and technical skills to 
local partners.  
23. 
Overall paradigm shift potential is assessed as Medium. Replicability of the 
investment scheme will depend on its success and results.  
1.3 
Sustainable development potential  
 
Scale: High 
24. 
Alignment with Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): CI2 contributes to the 
achievement of several SDGs, primarily Climate Action (SDG 13), Affordable and Clean Energy 
(SDG 7), Clean Water and Sanitation (SDG 6), and Partnerships for the Goals (SDG 17). By 
contributing to the above, CI2 can provide potential co-benefits relating to the following 
SDGs: Good Health and Well-Being (SDG 3), Gender Equality (SDG 5), Decent Work and 
Economic Growth (SDG 8), Industry, Innovation, and Infrastructure (SDG 9) and Life Below 
Water (SDG 14). 
25. 
Environmental co-benefits: CI2 can improve water quality and in turn increase 
biodiversity and improve the livelihoods and resilience of local communities. Ecosystems and 
ecosystem management will also protect communities from floods and increase biodiversity 
and food stock. 
26. 
Social and health benefits: Changing climate patterns, together with growing 
economies and populations, could reduce urban water availability in 2050 by two-thirds 
compared to 2015 levels, putting over USD 6 trillion of developing nations’ GDP at risk. A 
study done by the World Health Organization in 2017 shows that globally, more than 785 
million people did not have access to at least basic water services, and more than 884 million 
people do not have immediate access to safe drinking water. One of the objectives of CI2 in 
the water sector is to realize climate change adaptation impacts in countries undergoing, or 
expected to undergo, water stress, by building the infrastructure which sources, transports 
and treats the water necessary for both municipal and industrial users to be resilient to the 
localized impacts of climate change. 
27. 
Gender-sensitive development impact: The lack of sanitation facilities is even more 
impactful for young women, as they are more likely to miss out on education due to 
insufficient sanitation at schools.1 Additionally, women are often more negatively impacted as 
they are frequently responsible for collecting the water for use in the household. This and 
other household responsibilities reduce the likelihood of women receiving education. By 
 
1 UNICEF. 2020. Progress on drinking water, sanitation, and hygiene in schools. Available at 
<https://washdata.org/sites/default/files/2020-08/jmp-2020-wash-schools.pdf>.

=== PAGE 106 ===
GCF/B.33/02/Add.04 
Page 103 
 
 
providing and improving sanitation infrastructures, CI2 will enhance the livelihoods of 
women.  
28. 
Economic co-benefits: In economic terms, the annual economic value-added output 
derived from the oceans is estimated at USD 2.6 trillion, making the oceans the world’s 
seventh-largest economy (after the United Kingdom),2 showcasing the ocean’s importance for 
many livelihoods. Regarding the water and wastewater treatment sectors, equipment market 
size was valued at USD 61.6 billion in 2020. The market value is expected to reach USD 84 
billion by 2028, growing at a Compound Annual Growth Rate of four per cent between 2021 
and 2028.3 This growth will be fuelled by a rising demand for clean water amid shrinking 
water access worldwide.  
29. 
The independent TAP assesses this programme as having High sustainable 
development potential with significant social, economic and health co-benefits. 
 
 
 
1.4 
Needs of the recipients   
 
Scale: Medium–High 
30. 
The United Nations Educational, Scientific and Cultural Organization estimates that 
over 2 billion people currently live in areas subject to water stress, with water availability in 
many areas worsening because of changes in precipitation patterns. Additionally, as noted 
earlier, up to 80 per cent of illnesses in the developing world are linked to inadequate water 
and sanitation, as well as pollution combined with climate change-induced rising sea levels, 
which contaminates trusted water sources globally. These factors can detrimentally impact 
local health, safety and economic opportunity. CI2 will provide much needed infrastructure 
and nature-based solutions for local communities of targeted host countries.  
31. 
The CI2 programme targets 25 different countries, spread over Latin America, Asia 
and Africa, that all have significantly different scales and intensities of climate vulnerability 
(including water-stressed areas). CI2 is designed to assess the needs and determine the 
intervention required on a case-by-case and per country basis. The 25 countries have been 
selected based on their nationally determined contributions (NDCs) and whether CI2 
products are suitably aligned.   
32. 
To attract capital into host countries, CI2 requires investment from GCF as catalytic 
capital to help countries attract capital from private investors who have not been active in the 
water, sanitation and ocean sectors. 
33. 
The independent TAP assesses this programme as Medium–High in terms of needs of 
recipients. 
1.5 
Country ownership 
 
 
 
Scale: Medium–High  
34. 
CFM has a demonstrated track record from Climate Investor One in building a positive 
relationship with many of the identified country national designated authorities (NDAs) and 
in procuring no-objection letters (NOLs) from such countries. CFM and the AE have already 
sought NOLs from 25 countries for CI2. A summary overview of CI2 was shared with each 
 
2 Sumaila, U.R., Walsh, M., Hoareau, K. et al. Financing a sustainable ocean economy. Available at 
<https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-021-23168-y#citeas>. 
3 Statista 2022. Available at <https://www.statista.com/statistics/1258838/water-and-wastewater-treatment-
equipment-market-size/>.

=== PAGE 107 ===
GCF/B.33/02/Add.04 
Page 104 
 
 
NDA, along with the CI2 concept note. As of May 2022, CI2 has received 14 NOLs and were 
expecting approximately 18–20 NOLs to be in place by B.32.  
35. 
CFM maintains regional offices in the relevant CI2 regions. CFM’s regional office for 
Africa is in Cape Town, South Africa. Established in 2017, the office has 19 full-time 
employees. CFM’s regional office for Asia is in Singapore. Also established in 2017, the office 
has 14 full-time employees. CFM’s regional office for the LAC region was established towards 
the end of 2021, and it is finalizing arrangements for its office in Bogotá, Colombia. The team 
consists of four full-time employees. To strengthen this local presence, the AE (FMO) also has 
headquarters in Kenya, Singapore and South Africa.  
36. 
The country selection for CI2 was based on national climate plans, including NDCs and 
national adaptation plans, as well as sector plans from relevant ministries, including water, 
sanitation, energy and environment, and other ministries as may be applicable. The countries 
selected recognize the importance of water, wastewaster management and renewable 
energy.  
37. 
We assess the country ownership of the funding proposal as Medium–High.  
1.6 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
 
 
Scale: Medium–High 
38. 
The GCF’s investment in CI2 is expected to mobilize a ratio of 1:4 at the fund level (as 
every USD 1 of Tier 1 will mobilize USD 4 in the more senior tranches). The total leverage 
amount of private sector financing resulting from GCF funding is estimated at 1:16 (fund-
level plus project-level mobilization). The impacts of the programme are dependent on the 
ability of FMO and CFM to raise additional capital of USD 945 million from investors. CI2 has 
already achieved its first close with total commitments of USD 675 million. 
39. 
CI2’s primary efficiency and effectiveness benefits are derived from its whole-of-life 
financing approach. Funding made available by CI2 at all stages of the project, including early-
stage development and for pilot schemes, generates a pipeline of projects while building the 
capacity of local developers. The recycling character of CI2 enables the development and 
construction of more projects during the implementation period of the fund (the capital is 
expected to be recycled twice). 
40. 
The whole-of-life approach also reduces the time project developers usually lose to 
fundraising at the different phases of project development. CI2 also provides financing 
flexibility by preferring equity financing during the construction phase of the project. This 
eliminates cash flow and financial distress from projects during construction, while also 
ensuring the long-term interest of the owners and investors in the project.

41. 
We note that although the CFM is experienced in fund management and in making 
equity investments in climate-focused sectors and projects, it does not have investment 
experience/a track record in the water sector and ocean-related investments. Hence, the 
success of this CI2 hinges upon the ability of CFM to establish a team of investment and 
climate professionals who have sufficient experience and a track record in investing in water, 
sanitation and ocean-related investments.  
42. 
This includes the need for expertise in the relatively untested and challenging area of 
“green infrastructure” investments (grouped under the Oceans theme on the grounds of a

=== PAGE 108 ===
GCF/B.33/02/Add.04 
Page 105 
 
 
“catchment-to-coast” approach), for example wetland restoration for water retention or flood 
control, catchment restoration for water infiltration and erosion prevention, or mangrove 
restoration for coastal protection. The origination of such investment projects may rely on 
complex deals involving voluntary carbon market funding or other “payments for ecosystem 
services” such as income streams, and the establishment of innovative public-private 
partnerships. The existence of DF and the expertise of CFM in a range of adaptation 
strategies, including ecosystem-based adaptation, give the independent TAP some confidence 
that these challenges can be met. Although ecological infrastructure investments are likely to 
form only a small portion of the overall CI2 portfolio, this kind of innovation would be 
welcome. 
43. 
The funding proposal is assessed with a rating of Medium–High in terms of efficiency 
and effectiveness. 
II.     Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel  
44. 
Based on the assessment above, the independent TAP endorses this funding proposal.  
45. 
We are pleased to see earlier GCF-approved blended finance models being expanded 
and applied to harder-to-solve financial barriers: mobilizing private finance for mitigation 
and adaptation in the water sectors.  
46. 
The one area that gave us some pause was the level of concessionality requested (0 
per cent return + CPI for GCF funding), albeit the request was in line with the level previously 
provided to Climate Investor One. In our view, the appropriate level of concessionality 
required to leverage private sector finance is an evolving target, as more firms become 
experienced in climate investments and are engaged in developing countries generally. What 
might have been the appropriate amount of concessionality needed to mobilize private 
funding in 2017 may be greater than what is needed in 2022.  
47. 
However, the information provided in the funding proposal and discussions with the 
AE made clear that the perceived risks associated with the water, sanitation and ocean 
sectors, and the lack of familiarity of private sector investors with this area, supported the 
requested levels of concessionality. Ultimately, we were persuaded that the risk of over-
subsidization is low in this case.  
48. 
Nevertheless, we would prefer to see future blended finance instruments with flexible 
structures that allow the GCF to make a return if the expected risks and barriers to fund 
mobilization and investment return prove to be overly conservative. We want to make sure 
that GCF concessionality strives to be priced (i) at the lowest necessary level to achieve the 
desired mobilization and climate impacts; (ii) to avoid crowding out commercial initiatives 
that do not have access to concessionality; and (iii) to support the financial sustainability of 
GCF itself. 
49. 
We also note approvingly that CFM is providing a General Partner contribution of 1 
per cent (up to USD 10 million) into CI2. Investors in private sector funds typically prefer to 
see managers allocate some of their fees into the fund itself or otherwise contribute. This is a 
sign of confidence in the expected returns and aligns investor and manager interests. We 
would prefer to see this become more commonplace in fund vehicles where GCF concessional 
funds are being requested. Additionally, we take note that CFM’s shareholders (which include 
the AE) are investing a combined total of USD 70 million into CI2.  
50. 
Finally, we observe that true paradigm shift will be achieved only if the CI2 funds are 
able to build a track record of performing impactful and successful investments, which 
increases the confidence of asset owners and encourages new investment managers to

=== PAGE 109 ===
GCF/B.33/02/Add.04 
Page 106 
 
 
structure new vehicles in these sectors. For that reason, it is important that CI2’s underlying 
investments ultimately deliver on the specific climate change adaptation and mitigation 
impacts sought in the funding proposal. All potential investment projects claiming climate 
change adaptation impacts are dependent upon a robust climate adaptation assessment being 
conducted at investee/activity/site-level prior to making a decision on investment. Owing to 
the programmatic nature of the proposal, such assessments would need to be carried out 
before project-specific investment decisions are made by CI2. This introduces a level of 
uncertainty as to the exact projects that will ultimately be financed. This is the nature of fund 
models where we must rely to a great extent on the capacity of AEs and their selection 
processes. In this case, we were ultimately able to become comfortable with the approach to 
be taken by CI2, including the use of third-party assessments to validate scoring at least 2 or 
higher in the Rio markers for climate mitigation and/or adaptation, as well as robust 
measurement, reporting and verification processes.

(FMO) 
 
Impact potential 
The AE agrees with the iTAP’s observation that Climate Investor Two has a challenging mandate 
for a private sector Programme for the reasons mentioned. However, the AE believes the 
mandate to be highly impactful, with a significant part of its mandate focusing on much-needed 
adaptation and resilience projects, and that pipeline risks are being mitigated as much as 
possible. The AE would therefore argue that the Impact Potential would warrant a high score. 
   
Paradigm shift potential 
The AE would like to highlight that the investment scheme has already been successfully piloted 
by Climate Investor One (FP099) since its first close in 2017.  
 
Sustainable development potential 
This is well noted 
 
Needs of the recipient 
This is well noted 
 
Country ownership 
This is well noted 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
The AE agrees that the Ecosystem part of the proposal has high impact potential but remains 
somewhat untested and challenging due to the lack of clear revenue streams. The AE does 
however already see a promising pipeline and is confident in its capabilities to source and 
execute deals for an anticipated 10% of the GCF’s proceeds.  
Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel: 
 
The AE agrees with most of the iTAP remarks. However, the AE would note that:

=== PAGE 111 ===
Page 108 
 
• 
It is of the opinion that the + CPI return for the Tier 1 providers is warranted due to the 
perceived investment risks of the water, sanitation, and oceans sectors (i.e., because of 
the lack of ‘clear’ revenue streams). Mobilizing private sector finance in these sectors 
still requires such levels of concessionality (which is also reflected by the status of the 
Tier 2 fundraising) 
• 
The Climate Adaptation Assessment, and more detailed climate studies, are typically 
done during the Development Phase (pre-approval of the Construction Equity Fund)

Executive Summary 
Overview of Scope and Methodology 
This Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan forms part of Climate Fund Managers (CFM) funding 
proposal to the Green Climate Fund (GCF) for financial support to the Climate Investor 2 (CI2) to invest in 
infrastructure for water and sanitation services, the use and protection of wetlands, coastal and marine 
ecosystems as well as climate resilient ports in up to 25 countries.  
 
The Gender Assessment explores how, in the context of climate change women and men are affected by 
problems in three main areas: i) access to and management of water and sanitation (WASH) services; ii) the 
protection and management of wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems; and iii) the development of 
climate resilient port infrastructure.  
 
The preparation of the Gender Assessment comprised four main components (Figure 1): 
▪ 
A global evidence review, of 85 studies in the areas of WASH, wetlands, coastal and marine 
protection and port infrastructure; 
▪ 
Analysis of national WASH, climate change and gender policies in 11 priority countries, 
Bangladesh, Botswana, Brazil, Djibouti, Indonesia, Ivory Coast, Kenya, Morocco, Namibia, 
Nigeria, Uganda;  
▪ 
Analysis of selected SDG related quantitative datasets available for access to WASH, the 
protection of wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems and girls’ education. 
▪ 
Stakeholder analysis in 8 out of the 11 priority countries to map actors in the WASH sector 
and engage stakeholders in the Gender Assessment process. 
Figure 1: Methodology for the Gender Assessment and Action Plan 
 
 
Findings from each of these components were used to develop a problem tree analysis for each of the three 
areas covered by the Gender Assessment and identify opportunities for promoting gender equality through 
CI2 investments.
