The opportunities identified in the Gender Assessment are expanded upon in the linked Gender Action Plan 
which provides further details on how CFM and its partners will take advantage of the opportunities 
available to promote gender equality internally within Project Companies and in communities where CI2 
supports investment. The Gender Action Plan includes a performance management framework with which 
CFM can be held accountable. 
Overview of Global Evidence Review 
WASH 
WASH infrastructure and services for households, in health care facilities and in schools are inadequate in 
most developing country contexts. Their supply is further undermined by climate change, which affects 
weather patterns, reducing water availability and damaging WASH infrastructure.  
 
Women and girls are disproportionately affected by inadequate WASH infrastructure and services. Their 
domestic and care-giving roles mean they are responsible for collecting water, for WASH practices within 
the household and for caring for family members when they are sick, sickness which often is a result of low-
quality WASH services available to them. Women and girls can often travel long distances to collect water, 
limiting the time they can spend on other, more enriching activities such as attending school, or engaging 
in productive activities.  It exposes them to the risk of violence whilst travelling to and from water points, 
and to a heightened risk of disease through greater exposure to poor quality WASH. 
 
Women and girls’ physiology means they have greater need for WASH facilities, for example, during 
menstruation or pregnancy, needs which often cannot be met. Cultural norms sometimes prevent women 
from sharing latrines with men, especially during menstruation, thereby limiting women and girl’s access to 
WASH and forcing them into situations where they are exposed to the threat of violence. Girls often miss 
school when menstruating as toilet facilities are inadequate.  As primary users of health services, women 
and girls face increased risk of infection and disease as a result of poor WASH facilities in health centres.  
 
Although most affected by limitations in the WASH system, women and girls have limited influence over 
the planning, design and management of WASH infrastructure and services. Social norms often exclude 
women from public consultation spaces or silence their voices when they are invited in and so their needs 
and priorities do not inform the development of the WASH system.  Female representation in the workforce 
is a major challenge in most countries in both government and NGO WASH services. Where women are 
found in the WASH sector workforce, they are more likely to be in junior or unpaid roles, although 
pioneering work in Asia is starting to break down traditional barriers.    
Wetlands, Coastal and Marine Ecosystems  
Relevant evidence identified through literature searches for the gender assessment speaks to: i) women 
and men’s use of and interaction with wetland, coastal and marine areas; ii) how climate change is affecting 
these different uses and interactions, and the impact on the populations concerned; and, lastly, iii) the 
different ways in which men and women are, are not and could be involved in the protection and 
management of wetland, coastal and marine areas.  
 
Key findings include that, as regards their participation in oceans-based industries (of which most of the 
relevant literature relates to fishing) women are disproportionately represented in informal and 
unregulated economies and concentrated in less profitable parts of value chains. Women’s participation is 
curtailed by gendered norms which restrict their mobility, constrain their access to finance and discourage 
other community members from viewing them as “real fishers”. Women’s participation in the fishery 
industry is often overlooked and undervalued, resulting in the marginalisation of women who do participate 
in the management of wetland, coastal, and marine areas. 
Page 112

=== PAGE 116 ===
Draft Report 
Page v of 143 
 
At the same time, and whilst the evidence base on the gendered impacts of unsustainable fishing practices 
is underdeveloped, it is women who are likely to be disproportionately affected by rising sea temperatures, 
levels and acidity.  
 
As regards gender mainstreaming in sustainability and management initiatives, women’s participation is 
currently very limited – a hindrance for the improvement of, for example, fishing sustainability because 
women tend to be more willing to engage in progressive sustainability practices. Policy and decision making 
remains mostly gender blind, with data collected by governments tending to focus on formal economies 
and evidence on gender-differentiated effects of innovation and technological advancements lacking. 
Unsurprisingly, then, existing women’s empowerment interventions, tend to take a segregated, ‘gap filling’ 
approach which fails to address underlying structural barriers, perpetuating discriminatory norms and 
gendered inequalities.  
Ports 
The evidence base on the intersect between gender, climate change and port infrastructure is particularly 
limited, primarily focusing on i) the different ways in which men and women use and benefit from available 
port infrastructure, as well as ii) those in which men and women are, are not and could be involved in the 
development of climate resilient port infrastructure. 
 
Women are not able to fully reap the benefits of economic growth from ports development due to their 
exclusion from related employment and, particularly, from operational and managerial positions. At the 
same time, there is a lack of evidence on the gender-differentiated effects of ports’ negative impacts on 
ecosystems.  
 
The development and expansion of new climate-resilient ports creates jobs and therefore opens up 
opportunities to train and hire women. Women’s recruitment, integration and progression is starting to be 
supported through training, mentorship and initiatives to improve working environments. More broadly, 
women have an important role to play in promoting climate resilient port development for the good of 
wider communities – but continue to be marginalized in discussions around new approaches. In the same 
vein, data on gender-differentiated benefits of optimised infrastructure and energy efficiency remains 
scant. 
Context Analysis in Focal Countries1 
As part of this gender assessment, 11 in-depth country analyses were completed to understand the national 
policy context for WASH in programming countries, as well as to identify relevant issues, opportunities and 
challenges at the national level to drive more equal outcomes. These analyses present a top-line picture of 
access to WASH facilities and look at the WASH context to understand how gender is addressed throughout. 
Summaries of these analyses can be found in Section 6 of this report, and the full country analyses are 
included in Annex 5, including a stakeholder mapping of the WASH sector.  
 
Overall, most countries have access to improved drinking water for around 90% of their population, with a 
few outliers. Drawing on WHO/UNICEF’s classification for WASH services, analysis suggests that the rate of 
access to different quality levels of service (e.g. either safely managed or basic drinking water services) 
varies more significantly. In terms of sanitation, access to improved facilities is more varied between 
countries, with some countries having eradicated open defecation while others are still working to get half 
of their population access to improved sanitation facilities. 
 
 
1 This section is based on detailed gender and energy profiles for each programming country. Key insights as they relate to the renewable energy 
sector are included in [Annex A].  
Page 113

=== PAGE 117 ===
Draft Report 
Page vi of 143 
 
The policy frameworks across different focal countries address a range of aspects related to water, 
sanitation and hygiene. All of them place a strong emphasis on the impact of climate change in exacerbating 
already existing issues in water availability, health problems, and inequality. For instance, it is widely 
recognised that climate change exacerbates the already challenging WASH landscape for women. At the 
same time, the degree to which gender is mainstreamed throughout policy and practice varies significantly. 
For instance, while all policies in Kenya concretely identify opportunities for women’s participation and job 
opportunities at different levels; Ivory Coast’s are relatively gender blind and do not propose how to create 
gender-inclusive environments. Many countries, such as Namibia, often reference gender as a cross-cutting 
issues in water and sanitation but fail to explore in much detail what role they can play or what 
opportunities exist for them.  
 
In terms of policy priorities, policies commonly identify a need to create more equal participation and 
outcomes for women. The notion of inclusion is echoed in national development plans, as well as more 
specific gender, water, health, and climate change plans. At the same time, many countries do not identify 
concrete actions to achieve these objectives. Nevertheless, this policy analysis identifies a number of 
common entry points across focal countries to improve the position for women, including greater 
participation of women in the workforce (including the WASH sector), education and training opportunities 
in specific areas (such as Science, Technology, Engineering and Mathematics (STEM)) and ensuring that 
women and young people are consulted and have sufficient voice in policy and investment decisions.  
Overview of Gender Action Areas for CI2 
This Gender Assessment brings together analysis of the position, opportunities and challenges faced by 
women and men across CI2 sectors (drawing on the review of literature and the deep-dives into the analysis 
of country contexts). Building on this assessment, the Gender Action Plan annexed to this Assessment in 
Annex 6 (and summarised in Section 8) identifies action areas and entry points CFM and its Project 
Companies can leverage to enhance participation and equal outcomes for women in CI2 investments in 
these sectors. 
Improving women and girls’ access  
CFM and its Project Companies will use its investment projects and community development projects to 
address the physical, financial, attitudinal and safety barriers that may limit women’s access to WASH and 
Oceans Infrastructure.  
Using project development processes (such as Environment and Social Impact Assessment, community 
needs assessment and  targeted Gender Assessments), CFM will explicitly consider the barriers which affect 
women and girls’ access, specifically focusing on indigenous households and women and girls with 
disabilities. It will incorporate, where possible, appropriate actions to address these barriers into the design 
of investment projects (and will monitor progress in this area). CFM will seek to use community 
development projects to increase the direct benefits for women and girls resulting from investment by 
addressing identified barriers which cannot be addressed through the main investment. Examples of 
potential projects include working with families and communities to build their understanding of and 
support for good WASH practices and modifying water points to ensure access. Additional projects are 
outlined in the Gender Action Plan. 
Giving women voice in the planning, design and management of WASH facilities 
As a priority beneficiary group for CI2 investments, it is essential that stakeholder engagement and 
consultation processes service the needs and experiences of women and girls. This is to ensure that 
investment projects and community development projects can be responsive, as far as possible, to the 
WASH needs of women and girls. This is particularly pertinent given that social norms in CI2 focal countries 
often devalue and silence women’s voices, even when public consultation mechanisms are in place. 
Page 114

=== PAGE 118 ===
Draft Report 
Page vii of 143 
CFM and its Project Companies will adopt an  inclusive stakeholder engagement approach that provides 
conducive spaces for women and girls to voices their needs and experiences, including identifying women 
and girls , as well as other identified marginalized social groups in the targeted countries, as a distinct 
stakeholder group with whom to directly engage, identifying women’s leaders to work with to convene 
appropriate fora to hear a range of women’s and girls’ voices and working with community and men’s 
leaders to help them understand the rationale for engaging women in the planning of WASH facilities. 
In some projects, community-led WASH management committees may play a role in the management of 
WASH facilities. Where this is the case, CFM and its Project Companies will support committees to operate 
in a way that provides space for women’s voices to be heard, including by identifying and recruiting to the 
committee local women who are perceived (by men and other women) to have some legitimacy in speaking 
out on behalf of the community. Planned actions to provide a safe space for women’s voices in the planning, 
design and management of WASH facilities will be set out in the investment’s stakeholder engagement plan. 
Additional action points to ensure women and girls have an equal voice are outlined in the Gender Action 
Plan. 
Providing women with formal employment opportunities in the WASH sector 
Low levels of secondary education completion amongst girls in several of the CI2 focal countries combined 
with gender norms around acceptable productive roles for women and men can make the formal 
employment of women in the WASH sector challenging.  
CFM Project Companies will be proactive in hiring a gender-balanced team, making adjustments to working 
practices where this could level the playing field between male and female candidates. It is, however, 
recognised that in several focal countries, there are systemic issues beyond CFM’s control which are likely 
to limit the Companies’ ability to recruit senior female project staff. In such contexts, CFM will scope out 
opportunities to encourage more women into target sectors, including through scholarships for women 
embarking on tertiary education in relevant Science, Technology, Engineering and Mathematics (STEM) 
fields. 
At the project level, Project Companies will identify employment opportunities for women in WASH 
enterprises and put in place appropriate mechanisms to recruit, train and support women in these roles. 
Opportunities might include roles as sanitation sale agents, in meter reading, water tank construction or 
cleaning, among others. Additional action points are outlined in the Gender Action Plan. 
Creating an enabling environment for gender mainstreaming in the CI2 Fund and the Project 
Companies  
CFM recognises the need to draw on specialist skills and advice to guide the company’s leadership on how 
to operationalise its gender commitments across the business and within the investments it manages. This 
is expected to be achieved through a network of gender and inclusion specialists who can be hired on an 
as-needed basis to provide fund and project specific support. 
CFM will support Project Companies to put in place essential internal systems which are gender-responsive 
and gender-transformative, and capacities that will facilitate gender mainstreaming through the project 
and its operations, thereby operationalising this CI2 Fund Gender Action Plan. Arrangements will include, 
developing a local (Project-specific) gender action plan, agreeing and implementing a Project-specific 
Gender Equality Policy which sets out the company’s commitments to gender equality and Project 
Companies also take steps to require their principal contractors to give due attention to gender issues in 
their own operations (such as developing an operational Gender Equality Policy and recruiting a gender-
balanced team). Further commitments are set out in the Gender Action Plan.

Glossary of Selected Gender Terms 
Empowerment 
The process through which people take control and action in order to overcome 
obstacles of structural inequality which have previously put them in a 
disadvantaged position. 
Gender 
Sex refers to the biological differences between male and female bodies. Gender, 
on the other hand, refers to the socially constructed attitudes, values, roles and 
responsibilities of women and men, in a given culture and location. These attitudes, 
values and roles are influenced by perceptions and expectations arising from 
cultural, political, economic, social and religious factors, as well as from custom, 
law, class, ethnicity and individual or institutional bias. Gender attitudes and 
behaviors are learnt and change over time. 
Gender analysis 
The close examination of a problem or situation in order to identify to differences 
in the gender roles, activities, needs, and available opportunities of men and 
women. Gender analysis of a development program involves identifying the gender 
issues within the problem which is being addressed and in the obstacles to 
progress, so that these issues can be addressed in all aspects of the program - in 
project objectives, in the choice of intervention strategy and in the methods of 
program implementation. 
Gender-aware policy 
A policy which takes into account the social relationships of women and men as 
well as the differences in their needs, as opposed to a policy that is gender neutral 
and implicitly assumes that women and men have the same needs. 
Gender blindness 
The failure to recognize that the needs of men and women are different. A gender-
blind approach assumes that gender is not an influencing factor in projects, 
programs or policies. 
Gender division of 
labour 
Is an overall societal pattern where women are allotted one set of gender roles and 
men another. An unequal gender division of labor refers to a division of labor in 
which there is an unequal gender division of reward. Discrimination against women 
in this sense occurs when women get most of the burden of labor, and most of the 
unpaid labor, but men collect most of the income and rewards resulting from labor. 
In many countries, the most obvious pattern in the gender division of labor is that 
women are mostly confined to unpaid domestic work and unpaid food production, 
whereas men dominate in cash crop production and waged employment. 
Gender equality 
Means that there is no discrimination on grounds of a person’s sex in the allocation 
of resources or benefits, or in the access to services. Equality exists when both men 
and women are attributed equal social value, equal rights and equal 
responsibilities, and have equal access to the means (resources, opportunities) to 
exercise them. Gender equality may be measured in terms of whether there is 
equality of opportunity, or equality of results. 
Gender equity 
Means fairness and justice in the distribution of benefits and responsibilities. 
Gender equity is the process of being fair to women and men. To ensure fairness, 
measures must often be available to compensate for historical and social 
disadvantages that prevent men and women from otherwise operating on a level 
playing field. Equity leads to equality. 
Gender gap 
A gender gap is an observable and sometimes measurable gap between men and 
women in terms of socioeconomic indicators, such as ownership of land, 
attendance at school or participation in the labor force, which is understood to be 
unjust and provides evidence of a gender issue to be addressed. 
Gender 
mainstreaming 
Is the systematic process of ensuring that women and men have equal access and 
control over resources, development benefits and decision-making at all stages of 
the development process through ensuring that the respective needs, interests and 
priorities of women and men are integrated into in all policies, programs and 
Page 118

=== PAGE 122 ===
Page 11 of 143 
 
projects. It requires: (a) continuous assessment of the implications and effects of 
energy-related projects and policies on women and men; (b) designing and 
implementing strategies and actions that aim to better address the needs of 
women and men, improve their well-being, and facilitate their participation in the 
development process; and (c) a mechanism for ensuring this is done and for 
reporting to managers. 
Gender neutral and 
gender blind 
 
Gender-neutral policies are not specifically aimed at either men or women and are 
assumed to affect both sexes equally. However, they may actually be gender-blind. 
Gender-blindness means ignoring the different roles, responsibilities, capabilities, 
needs and priorities of women and men. Gender-blind policies are based on 
information derived from men’s activities and/or assume those affected by the 
policy have the same (male) needs and interests. 
Gender relations  
 
The social relationships and power distribution between men and women in both 
the private (personal) and public spheres. 
Gender roles 
 
Gender roles are sets of behaviours assigned to men and women respectively, 
according to their cultural norms and traditions that determine which activities, 
tasks and responsibilities are perceived as male and which female. Gender roles are 
affected by age, class, race, ethnicity and religion, and by the geographical, 
economic and political environment. These roles shape identity, determining how 
we are perceived by others, how we are expected to think and act as women and 
men. Changes in gender roles often occur in response to changing economic, 
natural or political circumstances, including development efforts. 
 
 
Page 119

=== PAGE 123 ===
Page 12 of 143 
 
 
Background to the Assessment 
Climate Fund Managers (CFM) is preparing a funding proposal for the Green Climate Fund (GCF) to be 
presented to the GCF Board in June 2022. If successful, CFM will use its financing facility Climate 
Investor 2 (CI2) to invest in infrastructure for water and sanitation services, the use and protection of 
wetlands, coastal and marine ecosystems as well as climate resilient ports in up to 25 countries.  
 
Both GCF and CFM are committed to promoting gender equality and the empowerment of women 
through their investments. By adopting a gender mainstreaming approach in investments, GCF and 
CFM expect not only to mitigate the risks for women and men associated with funded projects, but 
also to benefit women and men equally, reducing any gender differentials associated with 
vulnerabilities exacerbated by climate change and promoting sustainable and equitable development.   
 
This assessment has been commissioned to inform CFM’s funding proposal to GCF. In line with the 
assignment’s Terms of Reference (ToR), it aims to understand, within the context of climate action, 
how women and men are affected by problems in three main areas: i) access to and management of 
water and sanitation (WASH) services;2 ii) the protection and management of wetland, coastal and 
marine ecosystems; and iii) the development of climate resilient port infrastructure. The specific issues 
covered under each area are summarised in Table 1 below.

Findings from the assessment have informed the development of a Gender Action Plan, which sets 
out how CFM will mainstream gender and inclusive approaches in order to promote gender equality 
through the CI2 investment and supported projects. 
 
This report presents a gender assessment of the three areas of investment interest. It has been 
prepared over two iterations. The first iteration provided a high-level assessment of gender issues in 
the water and sanitation sectors, as well as country level context analysis for 8 priority countries: 
Bangladesh, Djibouti, Ivory Coast, Kenya, Morocco, Namibia Nigeria and Uganda. Responding to GCF 
feedback on the first iteration, this second iteration has an expanded scope. It now includes a high-
level global gender assessment of wetlands, coastal and marine ecosystems and port infrastructure, 
stakeholder mapping and engagement for the original 8 priority countries, as well as country level 
context analysis for an additional 3 priority countries: Botswana, Brazil and Indonesia. 3  
1.1 This report 
The findings from the gender assessment are presented in this report in the following eight sections.  
We start by briefly outlining the methodology used in preparing the assessment (Section 2). 
In Section 3, we describe, at a global level, the main gender issues identified in the water and 
sanitation sectors, and how these gender issues are affected by climate change, drawing on evidence 
from academic papers and practitioner literature. 
In Sections 4 and 5, we conduct a similar exercise in the areas of wetlands, coastal and marine 
ecosystems and climate resilient port infrastructure. 
In Section 6 we analyse the policy context and data in 11 focal countries in which we describe the 
policy direction in relation to WASH, gender and climate change, and identify entry points at the 
national level to improve women’s participation and equal outcomes. For eight of these countries, 
those designated priority countries 1 and 2, the context analysis also includes a mapping of key 
stakeholders in the WASH sector from national to community levels. 
In Section 7 we draw together the literature reviews and country-level analyses into problem tree 
analyses which unpack the causes and effects of inadequate infrastructure and services for women 
and girls across the CI2 sectors. 
In Section 8, we use the problem tree analyses and the evidence reviewed to identify four 
opportunities for CI2 to promote gender equality through large scale investment in WASH, wetland, 
coastal and marine ecosystem protection and climate resilient port infrastructure development. 
In the final section (Section 9), we provide an overview of key indicators to monitor the results and 
impact of gender mainstreaming activities.

Gender Assessment methodology 
The methodology for this Gender Assessment and the subsequent Gender Action Plan has been guided 
by GCF’s approach to gender mainstreaming (Figure 2). As part of it, we have conducted gender 
analysis of global evidence on gender issues in WASH, the protection of wetlands, coastal and marine 
ecosystems and port infrastructure, national policies for priority countries and available quantitative 
datasets; mapped country level stakeholders; and used this evidence to prepare the narrative and 
problem tree analysis that are presented in this report. Subsequently, the Gender Assessment has 
been used to inform a Gender Action Plan, which will guide CFM’s gender mainstreaming efforts in 
CI2. This Section describes in more detail the methodology used in preparing the Gender Assessment.   
Figure 2: GCF’s gender mainstreaming approach4  
 
 
This is a second draft of the Gender Assessment and Action Plan, which, following feedback from GCF, 
has been developed further. The second iteration responds to this feedback, especially focusing on 
the inclusion of Oceans Infrastructure in addition to the water and sanitation sector and reflect on 
findings from stakeholder analysis and engagement and will include a performance measurement 
framework and be relevant to a wider set of countries where CI2 will provide support.   
2.1 Information and Data Gathering   
The information/data gathering element of the Gender Assessment focused on: 
1. A review of global evidence in WASH, the protection of wetland, coastal and marine 
ecosystems, and the development of port infrastructure in developing country contexts. 
This included men’s and women’s, girls’ and boys’ access to and use of available resources, 
and associated constraints, including gender-based violence; their participation in the 
 
4 Source: guidelines-gcf-toolkit-mainstreaming-gender_0.pdf (greenclimate.fund) 
Page 122

=== PAGE 126 ===
Page 15 of 143 
 
planning and design of initiatives to improve facilities in the three thematic areas, as well as 
employment opportunities. 
2. National policies in WASH, gender and climate change in 11 focal countries: Bangladesh, 
Botswana, Brazil, Djibouti, Indonesia, Ivory Coast, Kenya, Morocco, Namibia, Nigeria and 
Uganda.5 
3. Quantitative datasets available for access to WASH, the protection and management of 
wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems and girls’ education, data primarily used for reporting 
against relevant SDGs. 
Review of global evidence in WASH, the protection of wetland, coastal and marine 
ecosystems and port infrastructure 
For the review of global evidence, we identified relevant search terms/keywords for the three 
thematic areas. These included: “gender”, “women”, “boys”, “girls”, “water”, “sanitation”, “hygiene”, 
“menstrual hygiene”, “handwashing”, “WASH”, “blue economy”, “ocean economy”, “sustainable 
ports”, “climate resilient ports” and “climate change”. These terms were used to manually search 
(through trialling different combinations of search terms) search engines and the websites of the 
organisations known to have a role in gender, WASH, oceans, port infrastructure and climate change. 
Through this, we identified relevant online resources from academic and practice-focused sources, 
which we then used to snowball additional sources by examining reference lists within the literature 
found. 
A total of 85 studies were identified and reviewed, including 71 focused on WASH, nine on wetland, 
coastal and marine ecosystems and eight (two being used for both) on port infrastructure (See Annex 
1 for bibliography). A selection of academic papers was included as well as multilateral agency reports 
and selected practitioner literature. To narrow down the extensive literature on WASH, the review 
focused primarily on what the Joint Programme Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene (JPWSSH) refer 
to as higher levels of service, in line with CI2’s focus on infrastructure.  Although hygiene is not 
specified in the assignment ToR, improved water and sanitation infrastructure will facilitate better 
hygiene, and so sources on hygiene were also reviewed.  
The global evidence review found that, with one notable exception (the Water for Women Fund), 
research with a comprehensive and combined focus on gender, WASH and climate, is relatively sparse. 
More studies were identified on the effects of climate change on WASH infrastructure than on its 
gendered impacts on WASH services. The exceptions here are the Water for Women studies which 
focused on rural settings and community managed WASH systems in the East Asia and Pacific region. 
In WASH overall, there is limited qualitative research and a lack of longitudinal research assessing the 
effects over time. For urban settings, utility level reports, complaint registers and WASH programme 
evaluations could provide additional sources of information.  
 
The global evidence review also found that most literature on wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems 
is largely gender blind. Most reports primarily examined benefits and impacts from an economic and 
biodiversity perspective, with limited discussion of benefits and impacts for people. When people 
were discussed, this was mostly associated with economic benefits, such as those relating to tourism 
and fishing, with little consideration of social impacts. 
 
5 The context analysis concentrated on WASH only, and did not include wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems, or port infrastructure, 
areas that were covered through global level analysis only.

=== PAGE 127 ===

Policy Analysis in focal countries 
This analysis focused on national policies, strategies and action plans in the 11 focal countries in the 
following areas: overarching national development, water, sanitation, hygiene, gender, climate 
change, health and education. Our review covered a total of 65 policies currently operational, 
including seven for Bangladesh, five for Botswana, nine for Brazil, six for Djibouti, five for Indonesia, 
four for Ivory Coast, seven for Kenya, five for Morocco, five for Namibia, six for Nigeria and six for 
Uganda (see full set of policies reviewed in Annex 2).  All of them were sourced online. Most were in 
English, although those for Djibouti, Ivory Coast and Morocco were largely in French and those for 
Brazil were largely in Portugese. 
Once collated, key data on core areas of interest (Box 1) was extracted from the polices into a 
spreadsheet and then analysed drawing out the overarching WASH policy direction, the extent to 
which gender issues are considered and tackled in WASH policy, and how climate change is affecting 
all of this.   
 
Quantitative data analysis 
Quantitative data on population access to WASH facilities and access to WASH facilities in schools and 
health centres has largely been drawn from the WHO and the UNICEF JPWSSH.6 This data has been 
complemented with data from UNICEF’s Multiple Indicator Cluster Surveys (MICS)7, where available, 
and, for data on women’s education levels, from UNESCO’s Institute of Statistics.8 The latter is to 
understand the extent to which women may be equipped to access formal employment within the 
WASH sector. For the protection of wetlands, coastal and marine protection, we drew on SDG14 
reporting, but very little of it tracked men’s and women’s engagement.

of whom responded and were interviewed (Table 3).

2.3 Gender Assessment Narrative and Problem Tree Analysis  
Findings from the global evidence review of WASH and protection of wetlands, coastal and marine 
ecosystems and port infrastructure, as well as the deep dive analysis of focal countries in the WASH 
sector, are presented separately in this Gender Assessment, in Sections 3, 4 and 5 respectively. We 
have used these findings to prepare problem tree analyses for each of the three thematic areas, all of 
which can be found in section 7.  
The opportunities for promoting gender equality through CI2 investments presented in section 8 of 
this report draw on the full dataset collated through the global literature review, the stakeholder 
mapping, the national policy review in 11 countries and problem tree analysis in the three areas.  The 
 
9 It was not possible to conduct stakeholder interviews with stakeholders in the remaining three countries as No Objection Letters were 
received too late in the process. 
Page 125

=== PAGE 129 ===
Page 18 of 143 
analysis process to identify these opportunities involved two steps.  Firstly, as part of the first iteration 
of this Gender Assessment, we used the findings from the literature review of gender issues in WASH 
to identify potential opportunities for promoting gender equality through WASH.  We then validated 
these using the country analysis in an initial set of 8 focal countries. Overall, there was a good 
alignment between the two. Secondly, in the second iteration of this Gender Assessment, we used 
findings from the literature review of gender issues in the protection and management of wetland, 
coastal and marine ecosystems, and in the development of port infrastructure, to identify 
opportunities for promoting gender equality in these areas. The opportunities aligned well with those 
already identified for WASH and the two have been combined.  This integrated set of opportunities 
form the basis of the material presented in section 8 of the report. 
2.4 Limitations 
Availability of global evidence on the protection of wetlands, coastal and marine 
ecosystems, and climate resilient port infrastructure 
The existing evidence base on gender as it relates to oceans and, particularly, port infrastructure is 
extremely small. Many qualitative reports reviewed were gender-blind and did not explore how 
benefits and impacts may differ for women and men. No reports explored differentiated impacts for 
boys and girls, with children in general referred to only in relation to food security.  
More broadly, most reports primarily examined benefits and impacts from an economic and 
biodiversity perspective with limited discussion of benefits and impacts for humans. When people 
were discussed, this was mostly associated with economic benefits, such as those relating to tourism 
and fishing, with little consideration of social impacts. With the majority of ocean-related reports 
authored by INGOs and other issue-focused organisations who collaborate through groups such as 
Ocean Panel and WorldFISH, many reports are sector specific, particularly fisheries and, to a lesser 
extent, tourism.  
The literature on good practices for involving women in, and maximising benefits for them from, the 
protection and management of wetland, coastal and marine areas or the development of climate 
resilient port infrastructure is also scant.  
Quantitative data is similarly scarce. Most data identified focused on developed countries or regions 
(e.g., the EU). Available data also tends to focus on the formal sector with little or no 
consideration of impacts in the informal sector, where women tend to be concentrated. The most 
recent unpublished statistics for SDG 14 are from 2014 and, as above, focus on physical indicators 
such as marine and coastal protected areas and fish stocks, rather than related human benefits or 
impacts. 
Availability of national policy documents 
The research team has encountered some challenges in accessing required data. For example, it has 
not been possible to include in the review policies that are not accessible online. This has led to some 
significant omissions, for example, we have not been able to access Water and Sanitation policies for 
Nigeria and Uganda, and we have only been able to review three policies for Ivory Coast. Similarly, 
there are gaps in the quantitative data available which are noted in the country summaries in Section 
6. Furthermore, the gender analysis in policies can be quite scant, suggesting that a full consideration
of the gender issues in WASH has not been done at the policy level.
Page 126

=== PAGE 130 ===
Page 19 of 143 
 
Time pressures for conducting the stakeholder mapping 
With only three weeks to prepare the stakeholder mapping, the scope for engaging stakeholders has 
been extremely tight. It is positive that 29 stakeholders have been engaged but there are gaps: we 
have not been able to engage any stakeholders in Morocco, nor have we been able to conduct a 
stakeholder mapping for the three countries added in the second iteration of the Gender Assessment, 
namely Brazil, Botswana and Indonesia, for which No Objection Letters came through too late in the 
process.

=== PAGE 131 ===

Global Evidence Review: WASH & Climate Change 
In this Section, we present findings from the global evidence review exploring the main gender issues 
in WASH.  It considers the following questions, identified as the more pertinent issues based on our 
review of the relevant literature: 
• 
Women and men’s, girls’ and boys’ access to and use of WASH services, the barriers they 
encounter, including gender-based violence, and how climate change is affecting these; 
• 
How women and men, girls and boys are affected by challenges in accessing quality WASH; 
• 
The roles that men and women, boys and girls play in the WASH sector, including formal 
employment opportunities; 
• 
Women and girls’ participation and leadership in the design, planning and management of WASH 
infrastructure and services. 
3.1 Differences in WASH access and consequences  
Women and girls are largely responsible for household water collection and latrine cleaning, yet there 
are often differences in their ability to access and use WASH facilities. In 2017, WaterAid estimated 
that WASH-related diseases were the fifth biggest killer of women worldwide, causing more deaths 
than AIDS, diabetes or breast cancer. This section outlines the differences in access to and use of water 
supply, sanitation and hygiene in households, health facilities and schools. Some of the key barriers 
which affect access to WASH facilities are summarised in Table 3 below. 
Access to water in household settings 
Fetching water is a physically challenging task and is typically carried out by women and children. It 
has musculoskeletal health risks and other potential injuries, including spinal damage, hernia and 
prolapse, as well as an increased risk of miscarriage10. Distance to reaching water and sanitation 
facilities makes people vulnerable to physical or verbal abuse, rape and assault11. Other risks include 
rough terrain (particularly when carrying water) and attack from dangerous animals12. Water 
collection can affect people’s quality of life in a number of ways: time devoted to water-related tasks 
limits women’s time for other household chores, rest and leisure and income-generating activities13. 
 
Increasing access to sufficient quantities of water can also contribute to better hygiene (such as 
handwashing with soap or face washing) and thus improve health (in terms of diarrhoea and 
respiratory infections). Women have an increased need for water for hydration, sanitation and 
hygiene during menstruation, pregnancy, the postnatal period and caring for sick family members or 
young children14. Limited supplies of water, as well as competing water needs in the household, can 
trigger stress and violence between spouses and parents and children15. For this reason, women might 
put partner/family water needs above their own to avoid abuse. There is some evidence that 
household piped water helps reduce intra-household conflict16. Examples of sextortion (the practice 
 
10 Fisher (2008) 
11 Geere et al. (2018) 
12 Caruso et al. (2021) 
13 Sorenson et al. (2011) 
14 Kayser (2019) 
15 House et al. (2014) 
16 Devoto et al. (2012) 
Page 128

=== PAGE 132 ===
Page 21 of 143 
 
of extorting sexual favours) for public water supply in low-income settlements have been documented 
in Kenya17. 
Access to sanitation facilities in household settings 
Many communities have strict cultural norms around men and women sharing household or public 
toilets. Where norms prevent women from sharing latrines with men during menstruation, this often 
compels open defecation. Social expectations of modesty mean that women should not be seen 
urinating and defecating. In order to avoid using the toilet during the day, women report reduced 
intake of food/drink or taking anti-diarrhoea tablets18. Poor sanitation practices (such as open 
defecation) and WASH-related diseases can induce stress during pregnancy and may also contribute 
to miscarriage and still birth19. Pay-per-use latrines are often inequitable for women as there are often 
fewer cubicles for women (leading to longer queues), and women use toilets more frequently than 
men, making it more expensive20. Women report becoming practised at going for several hours 
without urinating due to the lack of facilities in a variety of settings (including in the workplace, in 
public buildings and spaces or when travelling). Those needing more frequent toilet use (e.g. pregnant 
women) or incontinence (older women) are limited in their ability to travel far from home21. 
Dehydration from not drinking due to poor latrine access (especially for women and girls) can affect 
personal hygiene and enhances the risk of reproductive and urinary tract infections. 
 
Men tend not always to use a toilet for defecation, either when they are at home or away. A range of 
reasons are cited for this, including: to slow the rate of the pit filling, men having more time available 
for open defecation (OD), not having a toilet where they work, the greater mobility of men and 
reverting to OD when household toilets are in high demand22. Open defecation is considered less risky 
for men regarding security/safety concerns and perhaps, in some cases, less shame about OD. Male 
open urination is a commonly accepted practice, although it can offend public decency, and is often 
prohibited through by-laws and punishable with fines23. 
 
Table 3: Examples of barriers inhibiting use 
 
Physical 
• 
Women have more limited land rights, which constrains their ability to decide 
whether or not to construct a household latrine or improved water point.  
• 
Use may also be limited by rough terrain (particularly when carrying water).  
• 
Men migrating for paid work means less support for physical labour. Women 
may need to pay men for construction of infrastructure24 
• 
Dependence on others for water assistance or visits to the latrine could result in 
stress and anxiety for people with disabilities, sick or older relatives and others.  
Financial  
• 
Paying for water has gender implications: women earn lower wages, give up paid 
work to collect water and have less control over assets within the household.

women’s access to preferred water sources, desired water quantity and water

• 
Wealthy women report spending money to access water sources with shorter 
wait times or higher quality, or to compensate male neighbours, laborers or 
drivers for water collection.  
• 
Using public pay-per-use toilets mean women incur higher costs than men due 
to more frequent need.  
Safety  
• 
General safety concerns include feeling unsafe collecting water or feeling unsafe 
where they bathe and wash clothes.  
• 
Long absences from home for water collection could lead to conflicts because of 
husbands suspecting wives of infidelity.  
• 
Inadequate access to sanitation (when toilets lack doors, locks, lighting or are far 
away) is also associated with a heightened vulnerability to various forms of 
violence by men25. This includes taunting, teasing, name calling and throwing 
things at women or girls when going to latrines and open defecation sites, 
especially after dark. 
• 
Women frequently accompanied one another or asked men to accompany them 
for safety when open defecating.
