avoid using public toilets27.  
• 
Quarrels over contributions toward maintenance of shared toilets affects ability 
to use such facilities.  
Attitudinal 
• 
Women are often unable to use sanitation facilities or touch water when 
menstruating due to perceptions that menstruation is polluting. Taboos around 
menstruation can also affect participation in community life28. Menstrual 
hygiene is linked to emotional and psychosocial stress, resulting in feelings of 
shame, fear, anxiety and distraction.  
• 
Participation in a water project can lead to intrahousehold conflict and violence 
or shunning, for instance, if women spoke in meetings. 
• 
Husbands became angry or physically abusive when women took too long to 
collect water, when there was not enough water available for bathing and other 
purposes or when they were asked to assist with water collection.  
• 
Women and girls report shame related to sanitation and menstruation 
behaviours and experiences.  
 
Access to hygiene in household settings   
Globally, men are reportedly less likely to consistently wash their hands with soap at critical times. In 
households, women are typically the ones targeted by hand hygiene promotion through WASH 
programmes (often in their role as mothers). Women play a key role in supporting the hand hygiene 
of family members, for instance, by ensuring soap and water is available for hand hygiene or reminding 
family members to wash their hands before eating or after using the toilet. Women, in their role as 
 
25 Heller, 2014; House et al., (2014) 
26 Kulkarni et al. (2017) 
27 Lennon (2011) 
28 Megaw, Kohlitz, Gero and Chong (2020) 
Page 130

=== PAGE 134 ===
Page 23 of 143 
 
community health promoters, also play a role in supporting this wider practice within the community. 
The Global Scaling Up Handwashing Project29 in Senegal included men in handwashing promotion 
discussions after recognition that: (1) men control their household’s access and provide funds for 
soap, (2) men can follow up the outreach session to ensure that household members wash their hands 
with soap and (3) men can enable, reinforce and sustain behavioural change by washing their hands 
with soap while encouraging others to do the same.  
 
Adequate menstrual hygiene (MH) refers to using a clean menstrual management material to absorb 
or collect menstrual blood.  It can be changed in privacy as often as necessary for the duration of a 
menstrual period, using soap and water for washing the body as required. It also encompasses having 
access to safe and convenient facilities to dispose of used menstrual management materials (Joint 
Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene, 2020). Clean water is a fundamental aspect of 
safe and dignified menstruation. Water is needed for (1) personal washing during menstruation and 
(2) washing of reusable menstrual products like menstrual cloths. MH is limited by inadequate access 
to water and sanitation infrastructure, limited availability of menstrual products (absorbents), lack of 
a private place to wash or change products, poor hygiene awareness and problems arising from stigma 
and taboo. 
Access to WASH in health facilities and schools  
Health facilities 
According to the JPWSSH (2020), a quarter of health care facilities (HCF) lacked basic water services in 
2019. HCF may rely on handpumps on their premises for their water supply. If the handpump stops 
working, staff have to collect water from nearby communities. Smaller facilities may not have the 
systems needed to provide buffer capacity by storing water on-site, meaning that where there is no 
caretaker, medical staff must collect water daily (or more often) for use in the HCF. In times of 
shortage, family members, likely to be women, may be expected to bring drinking water for patients 
as well as water for bathing. Water quality may also not be sufficient, and handpumps may not always 
be chlorinated (or the quality of water tested) on a regular basis so health care facilities may not 
always have the quality of water necessary for use.  
 
Integrating handwashing/basic hygiene is fundamental to primary health care and the prevention of 
Hospital Acquired Infections. Although clinic staff may have knowledge of the importance of 
handwashing, HCFs do not always have functional handwashing stations and soap. In 2019, one in 
three HCFs did not have hand hygiene facilities at the point of care. During the COVID-19 pandemic, 
handwashing stations have been increasingly deployed at the entrance of HCFs to ensure patients and 
staff, including all visitors, adhere to COVID-19 measures. Health facilities may lack sufficient numbers 
of functional sanitation facilities, separate for staff and patients: in 2019, 10% of health care facilities 
had no sanitation service. Female friendly public toilets would: be safe and private, cater for hygiene 
requirements, be accessible for users, be affordable and available when needed, be well maintained 
and managed, and meet requirements of caregivers and parents. There would also be sufficient 
numbers of cubicles for men and women to reduce queues30. 
 
Increasingly, development partners are supporting improved WASH in HCF with improved WASH 
infrastructure and training on proper handwashing and handwashing stations. In particular, WASH can 
also support dignity in childbirth: a survey by the White Ribbon Alliance (2019) found that women 
 
29 WSP (2010) 
30 WaterAid et al. (2019) 
Page 131

=== PAGE 135 ===
Page 24 of 143 
 
want WASH in health care facilities for a dignified birth experience: expectations include running 
water, indoor toilets, clean beds and sheets. 
Schools  
Globally, 69% of schools had a basic drinking water service in 2019 while 63% had a basic sanitation 
service31. Whilst there are global and national standard ratios for pupils’ latrines, many schools are 
unable to provide enough toilets for pupils, or their limited budget means that materials are not 
always available to regularly clean toilets or to ensure anal cleansing materials and soap are always 
available. Soap should be included in the school budgets, but parents may also be encouraged to 
contribute funds. 
 
WASH in schools is intended to have a positive impact on child health, thereby reducing absences from 
school. WASH services in schools can be an incentive for parents to send their children to school, 
particularly girls in the transition from primary to secondary schools32. Adequate consumption of 
water at school can also lead to better hydration, which has a number of health benefits as well as 
benefits for learning (Hunter et al., 2015). 
 
A lack of WASH at home can impact girls’ education, for instance, when they are enlisted to help with 
water or sanitation-related tasks at home. Where there is no caretaker, students and teachers have a 
role to ensure the WASH facilities are maintained and that clean water is available. When girls attend 
school, they may also be asked to collect water for the school or clean toilets instead of being in class. 
There is some evidence that adolescent boys are impacted by poor sanitation in secondary schools; 
however, they are less impacted than girls. Overcrowding in schools has been associated with both 
boys and girls avoiding toilets. Students avoid using the toilet due to the anxiety of using a filthy latrine, 
queuing during the break or a lack of privacy when toilets do not have doors or locks.  
 
Globally, 57% of schools had a basic hand hygiene service in 201933. Handwashing in schools is 
promoted through a number of WASH in-school programmes and by school Health Clubs that promote 
WASH among the school community. Female teachers are often the main champions of handwashing 
with soap in the school environment.  
 
Self-reporting by girls suggests that inadequate toilet facilities in schools (lack of toilets, filthy toilets, 
insufficient water and limited access to menstrual materials) are a contributory factor for truancy, 
failing classes, absenteeism and drop out. Similarly, the lack of facilities for female teachers is a factor 
in staff retention/turnover. WASH programmes are increasingly tackling taboos and stigmas about 
menstruation through education and increasing as well as availability of WASH facilities at school by, 
for example, increasing girl’s access to sanitary absorbent materials.  
3.2 Effects of climate change on access to and use of WASH services 
At its most basic level, changing weather patterns affect WASH facilities by reducing water availability 
and damaging WASH infrastructure (Figure 3). Low-income households are more likely to be situated 
in areas affected by water shortages, floods, landslides and sewage overflow during floods. They are 
also more likely to use WASH facilities that can be easily destroyed or disrupted by climate hazards. 
 
31 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene (2020) 
32 Chard et al., (2019) 
33 JMP, (2022) 
Page 132

=== PAGE 136 ===
Page 25 of 143 
 
The poorest typically have the least capacity to cope with and adapt to shocks34. This section reviews 
the general impacts of climate change on access to and use of WASH services, before reviewing the 
specific effects on water, sanitation and hygiene services.  
 
Figure 3: How WASH issues caused by climate change are exacerbated by other urban challenges, WUSP, 2021 
 
 
The literature review suggests that climate change affects access to and use of WASH facilities in five 
main ways: 
 
• 
Nuisance and disruption: Disruption to residents is an immediate implication of climate-
related damage to WASH infrastructure. For instance, flooding could lead to sewage back-up 
in homes connected to the sewerage network, overflowing pit latrines, as well as flooding of 
homes. As household managers or home-based workers, women are more likely to be 
affected by the nuisance caused by the maintenance and repair of WASH systems and 
disruption to WASH services caused by climate change, for instance, in ensuring the 
cleanliness of the home or dealing with nuisance such as odours. Clean-up activities are likely 
to cause temporary closure of markets, shops and roads leading to disruption for women in 
particular. 
• 
WASH health outcomes: Waterborne diseases are predicted to become more prevalent due 
to an increase in climate-related hazards35. In addition, exposure of residents to untreated 
faecal sludge or wastewater poses an immediate threat to public health, including faecal-oral 
disease and vector-borne diseases. Increased faecal sludge contamination is associated with 
health risks meaning that temporary evacuations of homes may be required. Women are 
more likely to be caring for sick with WASH-related diseases and elderly relatives within 
households.  
 
34 Mills, Willetts, Evans, Carrard and Kohlitz (2020) 
35 Ibid 
Page 133

=== PAGE 137 ===
Page 26 of 143 
 
• 
Elevated levels of depression and other mental health outcomes: Water insecurity and 
scarcity, as well as open defecation, are considered significant stressors for individuals36. 
Water insecure communities experience higher levels of anxiety and depression, and this is 
particularly the case for older women37. High stress levels are a result of perceived unfairness 
in water distribution38. Loss of WASH services to residents is also highly likely to cause stress 
and reduced mental health for residents, and this may be due to a decrease in the 
functionality of water-based toilets, toilets not flushing, unusable taps or a lack water for 
bathing etc. Water stressed communities are more prone to arguments, disagreements and 
violence39. 
• 
Increased WASH costs: Inadequate WASH can also increase financial strains40. Where men are 
the decision makers over household’s budgets, this can increase stress for women. Climate 
related damage to WASH infrastructure will require increased maintenance, repair and 
running costs. For those with networked services, increased preventive maintenance and 
water quality management costs are likely to be passed on to the household through tariffs. 
A rise in water fees or the cost of buying water from private providers can make safe water 
unaffordable for poorer households. 
• 
Loss of productive time/income: Homes, schools, HCFs and businesses may incur clean-up 
costs and a loss of income due to climate related events. Repair works are likely to cause 
temporary closure of businesses. Businesses that require water for operations e.g. catering, 
tea stalls, brewing, hairdressing or laundry, often run by women, are more likely to be affected 
by loss of income.   
Access to water in household settings 
Following extreme weather events, water scarcity can result from resource limitations (i.e. physical 
hydrological constraints) or accessibility/affordability constraints.  
 
Damage to piped water networks can mean that water is supplied through mobile tankers instead. 
Women and girls might have to queue to collect the water which is then stored in the household and 
its use carefully rationed. Women typically have higher water needs for hygiene and are thus impacted 
more. This brings a stress on families as a result of this water scarcity. Disputes are reported when 
men use too much water for bathing or when children spill water41. Households without many storage 
containers must make more trips to collect water instead. Warmer temperatures lead to increased 
demand for drinking water, with the poorest city residents particularly at risk due to the phenomenon 
of urban heat island42. On extremely hot days, water-carrying women and girls (especially elderly 
women) can be affected by heat stress/exhaustion. Women with limited physical abilities (e.g., 
pregnant women or the elderly) are more likely to struggle to access waterpoints and toilets when 
land conditions become waterlogged, muddy or slippery.  
 
The poorest people in cities, already reliant on intermittent services, are most affected by water 
shortages43. Augmentation strategies include the use of multiple water sources to meet household 
 
36 Young et al. (2021) 
37 Simiyu et al. (2021) 
38 Brewis et al. (2021) 
39 Adams et al. (2021) 
40 Javidi and Pierce (2018) 
41 Caruso et al. (2021) 
42 WSUP (2021) 
43 Ibid 
Page 134

=== PAGE 138 ===
Page 27 of 143 
 
water needs, and this has implications for water quality, cost and the time required to collect water. 
Residents might have to pay private water providers for additional water deliveries or find other 
private water solutions such as kiosks and bottled/sachet water during periods of water scarcity. To 
save costs, the poorest may use unsafe sources such as surface water or hand-dug/shallow wells. 
Increasing rainwater storage capacity could help to address water shortages, but poor households are 
less likely to have resources to store water (e.g., safe storage tanks). Water borrowing from 
neighbours is a strategy to overcome access constraints, particularly for those with longer round-trips 
to fetch water44, but this is associated with feelings of shame, fear and indebtedness45. 
Access to sanitation facilities in household settings 
Climate change will accentuate existing weaknesses in sanitation services. In many cities, little of a 
household’s sewage reaches the treatment plant due to poor design or poor management, or as a 
result of broken sewer pipes, pump failures or power supply outages or a non-functional treatment 
plant. Instead, faecal sludge is dumped or discharged into open drains, bodies of water and the 
surrounding environment. The public health risk this poses will increase in the context of climate 
change e.g., increased faecal contamination of water sources and residential areas. Bad practices in 
rainy season include taking advantage of flooding to flush out pit latrine contents (instead of paying 
for pit emptying). Men often provide pit latrine emptying services and experience increased health 
risks from working with inadequate sanitation services without properly health and safety equipment.  
 
Where toilets are no longer usable because they’ve been destroyed or damaged by climate hazards, 
poor households are more likely to struggle to repair toilets and may be more likely to be driven to 
revert to open defecation. During periods of water scarcity in a peri-urban community in Botswana, 
residents with toilets connected to a sewer reverted to using pit latrines46. The poorest live on land at 
risk from flooding and human-driven deforestation, which can create flash floods and landslides. 
Rebuilding damaged facilities or purchasing new devices after extreme weather events can be difficult, 
particularly for those living in remote areas and who may be cut off from access to markets that 
provide products and services. Gendered norms, such as men being responsible for decision making 
on household purchases or re-constructing damaged facilities, can result in delays rebuilding if male 
figures are absent (e.g. because of out-migration for work) or if men consider sanitation a low 
household priority47. Continual damage or destruction of a facility has an impact on WASH behaviour, 
and it can result in fatigue/apathy such that households must be motivated to re-build a toilet and 
handwashing facility, or it may result in reversion to open defecation (permanently or on a seasonal 
basis). Women and girls are likely to face issues with privacy or harassment while defecating or 
managing menstrual hygiene.  
 
Adaptations for climate change include the scheduled emptying of latrines in advance of flood 
seasons, low water-use toilets and improved construction quality to reduce the infiltration of water 
into septic tanks or sewers48. 
Access to hygiene in household settings 
Hygiene improvements create demand for water. Where water supplies are limited, households 
typically prioritize consumption over hygiene, and this has implications for women and girls in 
 
44 Rosinger et al. (2020) 
45 Wutich et al. (2008) 
46 McGill et al. (2019) 
47 Kohlitz et al. (2020) 
48 Oates et al. (2014) 
Page 135

=== PAGE 139 ===
Page 28 of 143 
 
particular. This section reviews hand and menstrual hygiene in the context of increased high-intensity 
rainfall, decreased rainfall, different types of flooding and tropical cyclones and other weather events.  
 
Extreme weather events (such as heavy rain, landslides, floods and cyclones) can destroy or damage 
hand hygiene facilities, disrupt the functionality of the facility, or make it difficult for people with 
physical limitations to access facilities. For example, where households have their handwashing 
station outdoors or next to their toilet, storms could cause damage to the station. Handmade devices 
are more prone to break down; however, variable rainfall also undermines the operations of 
handwashing facilities served by a piped water supply. Climate change also affects people’s journeys 
to wash their hands. This can be a particular problem for children, people with disabilities or older 
people49. 
 
Handwashing with unsafe water may increase the risk of diarrhoea50. For instance, handwashing with 
water contaminated with high levels of E. coli is likely to transfer more E. coli onto hands than it 
removes through washing51. Intensive handwashing with contaminated water can cause skin lesions 
that can lead to infections entering the bloodstream52. 
 
Climate hazards can mean that access to urban centres or other places to purchase soap or 
commercial menstrual products may not be accessible all year-round. Climate-related events can 
disrupt transport networks, affecting the final link of a supply chain i.e. preventing products available 
in the district town from reaching the village shop or household in sparsely populated rural areas.  
 
Water scarcity can force choices about how water is used at the household level. With menstrual 
hygiene already insufficiently prioritized in many cases, this challenge could easily become even more 
problematic for women and girls in the face of climate-induced water shortages. 
 
Water shortages at home and at school make personal hygiene more difficult to maintain during 
menstruation. Hotter days, more rain and higher humidity can increase the risk of reproductive tract 
infection, making personal hygiene that prevents disease even more critical. Washing intimate areas 
with unsafe water may plausibly increase the risk of infections. Saline intrusion in coastal areas can 
make water unsuitable for bathing or washing menstrual cloths e.g. causing fabrics to become rough 
and chaffing skin, which necessitates the usage of single-use, disposable products instead. Hand 
washing after changing a sanitary product or handling menstrual waste is often promoted as a critical 
time for hand hygiene, again highlighting the importance of access to water.  
 
Prolonged shortage of water affects how reusable menstrual product are used, the frequency and 
manner in which they are washed and whether fuel is available to make it possible to sterilise them 
with boiling water. It also affects whether women and girls are able to wash their menstrual cloths 
effectively. Prolonged rainfall or very windy weather can make it impossible to dry reusable cloths in 
direct sunlight as recommended for hygiene reasons since drying and storing cloths in a dark or shady 
place (or using damp cloths) can lead to infections. 
 
Climate change may make conventional disposal options for single-use products irrelevant, both those 
that are safe and hygienic (disposal in solid waste systems, incineration, composting and deep burial) 
 
49 Kohlitz et al. (2020) 
50 McCormick et al. (2016) 
51 Verbyla et al. (2019) 
52 Lemery et al. (2015) 
Page 136

=== PAGE 140 ===
Page 29 of 143 
 
or improvised (flushing products down the toilet, shallow burying of menstrual waste and throwing 
used products into drainage channels or open spaces). High-water table, heavy rainfall and 
overflowing sewers and flooding mean burial is not a viable option. Incorrect disposal of single-use 
menstrual products can exacerbate climate change if they block sewers or drainage systems, cause 
flooding and pollute water. Inadequate disposal options can result in the further stigmatisation and 
marginalisation of menstruating women and girls. During climate related disasters, social norms and 
stigma that impact MH often become more entrenched. This could make it difficult to discuss 
menstruation, hygiene and more sustainable ways for women to manage their menstruation.  
Access to WASH in health facilities and schools  
Staff, students and patients in schools and health care facilities require sufficient WASH facilities. 
Increasingly deep boreholes are being installed with overhead tanks to provide a higher-level of 
climate resilience to services in schools and health care facilities53. The use of renewable energy 
sources (such as solar energy) for pumping systems can further optimize their resilience. Storage 
containers provide water supply in drought and can provide a gravity-fed water supply to 
handwashing/drinking water tap stations. Simple adaptations to the water pipe system include water-
efficient fixtures and leakage management.  
 
Moreover, the greater rooftop area and storage capacity can mean that institutions provide greater 
efficiency in rainwater collection. The water collected can then be used for toilet flushing, cleaning, 
washing hands and also drinking, with adequate filtration and proper treatment. In coastal areas 
affected by salinity, water storage facilities based on the rainwater harvesting can provide continuity 
of safe water to communities. Greywater can also be captured and recycled for non-potable uses such 
as irrigation of trees.  
 
Repair works are likely to cause temporary closure of schools. Additionally, increased domestic WASH 
work leaves less time for education, and this is likely to affect girls in particular. During weather events, 
schools are often used as shelters for temporarily displaced people, and this can place strain on WASH 
services. When schools re-open for students, latrines may need to be emptied and cleaned and 
supplies of water treatment products and soap restocked. If it is too dangerous to go to school or if 
schools are closed, girls might not be able to collect their entitlement of sanitary pads (if the 
government has a pad distribution programme). All of this has consequences for girls and women’s 
ability to access their preferred menstrual product, or to change the product as often as needed. 
3.3 Agency in accessing and managing WASH facilities 
 
Women, men, boys and girls have different roles in WASH services in household, community and 
schools and health care facilities. Where women have relatively lower social and legal status and hold 
less political power within a community, this limits their influence on decisions about how families 
and communities maintain WASH in accessing, using, developing and managing services against 
climate change impacts54. 
Household 
Women are very often given responsibility for household WASH, for example, fetching water for the 
household, storing water safely, washing plates and cleaning toilets. Women and girls are usually 
 
53 Charles et al. (2009) 
54 Kohlitz et al. (2020) 
Page 137

=== PAGE 141 ===
Page 30 of 143 
 
tasked with filtering or boiling water before drinking55. Women also take care of the toileting needs of 
children and older people. They also spend considerable time caring for family members ill from 
water-related disease. Despite being primary managers of water and charged with the WASH related 
care for their families and households, women are not always consulted in decisions on household 
WASH services. Routray et al. (2017) found that in rural Odisha (India), the majority of construction of 
household level sanitation facilities was done exclusively by the male head, attributed to women’s 
lack of education and limited exposure to the world beyond their home and inability to influence the 
household's financial decisions.  
 
Care and the domestic WASH-related work burden falls disproportionately on women in all cultures, 
but the degree of this inequality varies. A shift in gender roles, with men taking some responsibility 
for cleaning the house, toilets, and the environment can help women and girls take on other roles and 
options within and outside the home, including work and schooling. Men more often take on 
household roles related to both building and financing WASH facilities. 
Community  
Women’s perspectives or voices are not always reflected in WASH decision making. Particularly in 
indigenous communities, women often hold important traditional knowledge about how best to 
manage and use both water and land resources, making them valuable experts56. Community WASH 
committees may have a requirement for female representation, for instance, the Government of 
Nepal’s 2011 Sanitation Master Plan requires 33% of WASH committee members to be women; 
however, women might not be empowered to contribute fully to WASH decision making. Women’s 
groups are rarely identified as stakeholders in the process of developing City Sanitation Plans, and so 
have limited input to guide strategic planning and investment decisions. Unless men are engaged in 
facilitating opportunities for women’s decision-making in WASH processes, a WASH programme can 
inadvertently run the risk of increasing burdens for women. Time spent by women on community 
WASH activities can be a source of conflict within the household, particularly if this is viewed as 
impacting on women’s time for household and family responsibilities57. 
 
When the household is invited to attend village meetings, typically the male head-of-household will 
go and women will stay in the home to take care of domestic responsibilities58. Facilitating men’s 
allyship to support women’s active participation in WASH decision-making is an emerging area in the 
WASH sector.  
Workforce  
UN Global Analysis and Assessment of Sanitation and Drinking Water (GLAAS) data indicates that a 
large majority of countries lack sufficient human resources to implement national WASH plans. There 
are insufficient workers with the appropriate competencies in the right positions, including in rural or 
hard-to-reach areas or communities.  
 
Men hold the majority of professional positions in the WASH sector. Studies in Vietnam, Indonesia 
and Timor and elsewhere indicate that men hold the majority of professional positions in water and 
sanitation companies and utilities59. 
 
55 Megaw, Kohlitz, Gero and Chong (2020) 
56 USAID (2020) 
57 IWDA (2015) 
58 Megaw, Kohlitz and Chong (2020) 
59 Leahy et al., 2017; Grant et al. (2017) 
Page 138

=== PAGE 142 ===
Page 31 of 143 
 
 
Female representation in the workforce is a major challenge in most countries in both government 
and non-governmental WASH services. While men take on more paid roles, women are more likely to 
be offered unpaid roles as community health volunteers or health promoters. In many countries, 
women are missing in technical fields or management roles in the WASH sector. For instance, the HR 
Capacity assessment in Bangladesh found that the HR composition of government and WASH sector 
agencies is dominated by engineers and technicians, most of whom are men. The number of women 
employed in the sector is very low, and the majority tend to work as health extension workers and on 
community level hygiene promotion activities. Data from the IBNET database60 reveals that 
Bangladesh had around 6% female staff in utilities (1% female engineers) in 2017. In Sri Lanka61, the 
number of female engineers in the WASH sector has grown steadily over the past three decades. Sri 
Lanka’s free education policy has had enormous impacts on the sector, improving the ratio of women 
on engineering and technical water and sanitation courses in higher education. In Sri Lanka, 
international agencies prefer female candidates for WASH activities, and there are numerous rural 
water supply schemes run by women-led organisations.  
 
SACI Waters pioneering work in 2010 documented the experience of female water professionals in 
the region and shared their personal stories62. Since then, participation of women in WASH enterprises 
is increasing63. In Nepal64, having female sanitation sale agents is found to drive higher overall latrine 
use, and additionally, women are more effective at selling water and sanitation products/services to 
marginalized households65. WaterShed (2019) has worked in rural Cambodia to support women’s 
engagement in private WASH markets, including latrine sales and WASH product retail. Women’s 
ability to succeed often depends on their having self-belief, technical knowledge and safe workplaces 
free of harassment. It is also important they have the ability to work from home and simultaneously 
complete housework or supervise children as well as having family buy-in and permission to be away 
from home. Elsewhere, women have engaged in water vending, selling water from kiosks, meter 
reading, water tank cleaning, water filter selling and rainwater harvesting tank construction. Women 
also work in water-dependent, income-generating activities like catering, brewing and hairdressing. 
Additionally, women can work in sanitation related occupations such as toilet pan selling, toilet 
cleaning, latrine construction, serving as toilet attendants, doing manual scavenging work/pit 
emptying, managing public toilets or running faecal sludge management enterprises. In India, it is 
common for Dalit woman sanitation worker to clean and remove faecal waste from dry toilets, without 
the proper safety gear, risking infections66: ’A woman sanitation worker, working as a sweeper on the 
road, has no facilities such as toilets, or a resting shed to take a break, especially when working under 
the hot sun …. These women do not raise their voice against it, because they feel powerless and 
replaceable.’ Improvement in household water and sanitation services have freed up time for other 
income-generating activities.  
Schools and health care facilities  
Gendered power issues are at play, not only in how WASH is managed within households, but also in 
community interventions. For instance, latrine cleaning in schools often remains a female teacher or 
 
60 https://database.ib-net.org/DefaultNew.aspx  
61 IWA (2013) 
62 SaciWaters (2010) 
63 Indarti et al., 2018; Indarti et al. (2019) 
64 USAID and IDE (2020) 
65 Ibid 
66 Participatory Research in Asia (2019) 
Page 139

=== PAGE 143 ===
Page 32 of 143 
 
girls’ role. School-based WASH behaviour change programmes focus on the practice of WASH 
behaviours in all students. Integrating household WASH issues, gender and climate awareness within 
‘life skills’ curricula could support change. Play-based WASH activities have been developed to help 
girls and boys learn WASH behaviour, but have also been used to teach boys about the importance of 
taking on WASH related work in the home and thereby challenge their preconceptions of which tasks 
are ‘appropriate’ for their gender.  
3.4 Effects of climate change on agency  
In some cases, climate change is entrenching existing gender norms and roles, but in other cases, it is 
contributing to a (temporary) shift in norms and expectations around WASH related work in the 
household, community and workforce67. 
 
In extreme weather events, due to the dangerous conditions, men and women make more decisions 
together about when and how to do WASH related tasks, as opposed to in normal conditions when 
women mostly make these decisions. Men and boys may take over water collection (usually women’s 
duty) due to the dangerous conditions that can arise during extreme rain and wind, for example, 
slippery surfaces and falling trees. Men may assist women and girls more with washing clothes due to 
the heavy loads and security issues associated with walking longer distances to water sources. Clothes 
are heavy, and therefore, they will help with this heavy work68. Men might also support in the bathing 
of children. However, when this happens it appears more on a temporary basis during a crisis and is 
done to ‘help’ women rather than signifying a more permanent shift in gender roles. Men and boys 
may still be embarrassed to be seen collecting water and are more likely to use bicycles, wheelbarrows 
or motorcycles to collect water. Anecdotally, some husbands help their wives in collecting water from 
far away (with donkey carts). They may also help when wives are sick or pregnant or when they are in 
the postnatal period69.  
 
At the community level, men may take on more responsibility and work load for public clean up and 
for repairing damage to infrastructure after a climate related weather event such as strong winds and 
heavy rain70. Dangerous conditions may also lead to men fixing water systems and building toilets 
alone rather than with help from boys71. 
 
Men often have better access to education and information about climate change and water 
management than women (such as access to climate forecasts, early warning information and 
emergency and post-disaster services), and this can reinforce their role as WASH decision-makers72. 
Men often take more responsibility making decisions for restoring WASH access following a weather 
event73. Men may attend more community activities and meetings than women in dangerous 
conditions.  
 
Women’s work inside the home generally increases with water scarcity due to gender roles and norms 
that govern house work and decision-making. Women often take on responsibility for managing scarce 
 
67 Dery, Bisung, Dickin and Dyer (2020) 
68 Kohlitz et al. (2020) 
69 Assefa et al. (2021) 
70 Leahy, Kohlitz and Chong (2020) 
71 Ibid 
72 Megaw, Kohlitz, Gero and Chong (2020) 
73 Ibid 
Page 140

=== PAGE 144 ===
Page 33 of 143 
 
water resources in the household74. Climate change can also lead to the migration of men to find 
alternative jobs outside of the village, which increases the burden on women in terms of caring for 
their household, including taking sole responsibility for their household’s WASH needs. Water sources 
become more time consuming to access due to climate-related shortages75; accessing them requires 
waking up early to queue for water, walking long distances and includes long waiting times. It also 
leads to quarrels and conflicts at water points and the need to identify multiple water sources to 
diversify risk76, all of which are stressful for women77. Women may also expect that men will assist 
more with collecting water if they have to travel longer distances to reach it, which suggests some 
potential for conflict78. Young girls and boys may also be asked to help mothers with increased 
workloads. Deteriorating water quality (or use of surface water instead) means that women and girls 
will usually be tasked with treating contaminated drinking water to make it safe to drink79. Constraints 
on women’s time can limit their ability to access the fuel needed to treat or boil water. When people 
with disabilities or older people in the household are no longer able to access sanitation facilities, 
either because they cannot reach the facilities or the facilities are not functional, they will depend on 
others (usually women) to help them take care of their toileting and hygiene needs80. Women may be 
dependent on men to generate and spend income for WASH climate change adaptations such as 
storm-resistant toilets or water storage tanks for the dry season81. 
 
Access to information influences how people are impacted by, and respond to, climate change. 
Women are less likely to have access to information about climate events (e.g., early warning 
systems), and therefore are likely to be less able to prepare (e.g. to store clean water in their home 
before a storm hits). Women often acquire information from other women about water issues and 
are often excluded from decision-making processes on the maintenance of water resources and assets 
that could make services more resilient82. Megaw, Kohlitz and Chong (2020) report how this inequality 
in decision-making is linked to the perception of the man’s role in physical response, such as building 
back infrastructure, as being more vital to decisions than the woman’s role in caring for the house, 
family possessions and the wellbeing of household members. WASH clubs in schools can help raise 
awareness and foster individual behaviour change around climate and WASH at the household level.

Global Evidence Review: Gender & wetland, coastal and marine 
ecosystems 
In this Section, we present the findings of the global evidence review in the protection of wetland, 
coastal and marine ecosystems. It considers the following areas of discussion, identified as the more 
pertinent issues based on our review of the relevant literature: 
▪ 
Women and men’s use of, and interaction with, wetland, coastal and marine areas 
▪ 
How climate change is affecting these different uses and interactions, and the impact on 
the populations concerned 
▪ 
The different ways in which men and women are, are not, and could be involved in the 
protection and management of wetland, coastal and marine areas.  
4.1 Different interactions with wetland, coastal and marine areas 
Gendered roles in the fishery industry 
Fishing at sea with specialised equipment is usually carried out by men, while women generally fish 
on shallow reefs, tidal flats, and coastal estuaries. Formal and paid fishing activities are usually 
dominated by men, with women only representing 2% of maritime workers.83 In most places, women 
only have access to the less profitable parts of the fish for selling, and have limited trade connections, 
as well as less access to boats, fishing gear, and limited financial capital due to barriers to taking out 
loans and accessing credit84. This has contributed to a gender pay gap, with women earning 64% of 
men’s wages in the aquaculture industry for carrying out the same work85. Within value chains, women 
predominate in the post-harvest processing, marketing, and trading of fish, while most fishery 
agencies focus on the production aspect; 15% of harvesting jobs and 90% of fish processing jobs are 
carried out by women globally.86 Moreover, women are disproportionately employed in the informal 
and unregulated fish economies, including shellfish gathering and food processing. This can lead to 
exploitation, as there is little oversight and labour trafficking and sanitary issues can be easily 
masked.87   
Gender norms as a barrier to women’s participation in formal activities 
Women’s participation in the fishery industry is often overlooked and undervalued, resulting in the 
marginalisation of women who do participate in the management of wetland, coastal, and marine 
areas. Studies show that fishing is seen as a “man’s job”, and women are not expected to fish due to 
the arduous labour and long hours that keep them outside of the home for long periods of time.88 For 
example, gender norms in the Philippines prevent women from straying far from home to go fishing.89 
In Zambia, fishing gear is often only owned by men.90As mentioned before, in many countries, women 
 
83 Stuchtey et al, 2020 & Österblom et al, 2020. 
84 Österblom et al, 2020.  
85 Friends of Ocean Action, 2020.  
86 OECD 2020.  
87 Stuchtey et al, 2020.  
88 Stuchtey et al, 2020 
89 Österblom et al, 2020 
90 Ibid. 
Page 142

=== PAGE 146 ===
Page 35 of 143 
 
are denied bank loans or do not have access to credit, which prevents them from buying large fishing 
boats and other equipment needed to engage in formal fishing. Women may not be viewed as 
constituting part of the market, and tend to lack access to bank accounts or their own personal 
financial capital.91  
Maternal health concerns related to consuming large amounts of fish  
High reliance on marine organisms for nutrition leads to higher exposure to methylmercury and 
organic pollutants. Pregnant women are impacted by high levels of mercury from consuming seafood 
and must avoid certain fish species.92 Infectious diseases from fishing can impact food security, and 
industrial activity near oceans can lead to contaminants ending up in food webs and human food 
supply. No further gender specific impacts pertaining to these health concerns have been found in the 
literature.  
Lack of evidence on the gender-differentiated effects of unsustainable fishing practices  
Food insecurity leads to reliance on seafood, which causes overexploitation of fish stock. This causes 
fishers to spend more resources locating and catching stock, as well as moving to shallower waters 
which leads to more overfishing.93 Tourism, mining, and energy industries disrupt natural habitats, 
displace marine species, and the spread of contaminants to food webs and human food sources.94. 
Gender-specific impacts of overexploitation are not mentioned in the literature reviewed. 
Seafood makes up for 16% of all animal protein consumed globally. Fishing from oceans provides jobs, 
nourishment and livelihoods for coastal communities. Apart from fishing, sources of income include 
ports, shipping, tourism, and energy. The fishing sector continues to grow, with fishery productions 
expected to expand by 20% over the next ten years worldwide (OECD 2016). However, we have not 
yet seen any wholesale shift from extractive fishing practices to biologically sustainable production 
practices; this is likely to prove particularly difficult given the great (and increasing) pressure on the 
fishing industry to provide livelihoods to entire communities and societies. The literature does not 
explore gender-specific impacts in relation to the supply of food or employment.   
4.2 Effects of  climate change on different uses and interactions 
Rising sea temperatures are likely to disproportionately affect women’s livelihoods and 
food security 
Rising sea temperatures due to climate change causes changes in the behaviour of fish stock, 
permanent changes to ecosystems, and extinction of some species. The elimination of important 
ocean habitats will have serious effects on the food security of vulnerable communities. Whilst not 
explicit in the oceans literature, we know that women tend to bear the responsibility for managing 
crises within households and often reduce their own food consumption relative to that of other family 
members in contexts of food insecurity. Job opportunities will decrease in a context where there are 
already limited opportunities for employment, especially for women who are excluded from much of 
the fishery industry. Climate change disproportionately affects coastal communities who do not have 
 
91 Ibid. 
92 UN Environment, 2019.  
93 High level panel for a sustainable ocean economy, 2020 & UN Environment, 2019. 
94 UN Environment, 2019 
Page 143

=== PAGE 147 ===
Page 36 of 143 
 
alternatives to certain fish supplies and do not have the resources to respond to changing fish 
behaviours.95Because women are often barred from taking out loans or accessing credit, they are less 
likely to be able to access resources that would enable them to adapt to these changing behaviours.  
Rising sea levels are likely to disproportionately affect women’s livelihoods and food 
security 
Heat-induced expansions of water lead to a rise in sea levels which increases the risk of storm surges 
for islands and coastal communities. Exposed infrastructure is also at risk.96Rising sea levels leads to 
submerged lands, salinised soil and groundwater, and erosion of coastlines.97Tropical storms put 
coastal regions at risk of significant economic damage, especially due to damage to ports and 
harbours. Women and children are especially vulnerable in these instances as it is more difficult for 
the former to relocate and find work outside their communities. 
Rising acidity in oceans are likely to disproportionately affect women’s livelihoods and food 
security 
Greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions have increased acidity in oceans by 26% since the industrial 
revolution.98This harms shell-forming animals such as corals, phytoplankton, bivalves, and 
zooplankton, who make up the basis of many marine food chains. Poor water quality and rising acidity 
causes shifts in fish stock behaviour and movements, which leads to food insecurity for many coastal 
and island communities (OECD 2016). Food supplies will shift locations, and coral reefs that serve as 
tourist attractions will die. Vulnerable communities, especially women and young workers, who 
already face difficulty in finding jobs, are unlikely to have the resources necessary (or for women, 
sufficient mobility) to follow the movement of fish stocks. 
4.3 Involvement in the protection and management of wetland, coastal and 
marine areas  
Women’s (potential) roles in community-based management  
Provision of clear boundaries for access and rights to fisheries are associated with a community 
commitment to usage rights. When this occurs, management is often more sophisticated with better 
registration records for boats and fishers.99Managing specific areas that communities understand as 
“theirs”, leads to the elimination of outsiders skimming off the benefits of good management 
behaviours.  
The role of women in sustainability and management initiatives for the ocean economy is very limited. 
Because women are rarely viewed as members of the formal fishing sector (or as “real” fishers), they 
are often excluded from discussion around the rights and needs of communities. They may even face 
negative social repercussions for participating in harvesting processes, despite the nutritional needs 
of their families.100As a result, recorded engagement of women in new management initiatives is very 
 
95 Stuchtey et al, 2020. 
96 UN environment, 2019.  
97 Stuchtey et al, 2020.  
98 Stuchtey et al, 2020.  
99 Österblom et al, 2020.  
100 Österblom et al, 2020.  
Page 144

=== PAGE 148 ===
Page 37 of 143 
 
low,101causing their ideas to go unheard in community-based management and protection of marine 
and coastal life.  
Women’s exclusion is a hindrance for the improvement of fishing sustainability because they are often 
more cognisant of the need to make conditions better for future generations, and are more willing to 
engage in progressive sustainability practices.102For example, when First Nation Heiltsuk women in 
British Columbia took on leadership roles in the management of wetlands, they helped transform the 
governance of herring in their coastal region (Ibid). 
However, as developments in the ocean economy progress, there have been more pushes to include 
local voices and developing communities in decision making processes – with some attempts at 
inclusion involving women103 (Österblom et al, 2020). With this new call for bottom-up processes, 
women are more likely to be included in discussions, but empowerment of civil society and localised 
movements (still new developments in the ocean economy) is necessary for women to exercise agency 
and decision making power (UN Environment, 2019).  
Gender-blind policy and decision making  
Institutions need to be agile, adaptable and follow ‘top-down, bottom-up’ practices that are informed 
by science. Women face barriers to accessing governing institutions, with their voices often left out of 
decision making practices. In Senegal, for example, women constitute only 5% of fishing management 
bodies, causing decisions to be made that only benefit some groups (Österblom et al, 2020), making 
it difficult to understand how women are affected by industry developments.  
Data collection by governments often only includes information on men’s engagement in the paid 
harvesting sector of the fish production chain and excludes women’s engagement (Adam et al, 2021). 
Such conventional gender-blind approaches to research and policy development for the fishery 
industry which only take into account formal and paid fishing activities mean that women’s activities 
in the sector are largely unaccounted for, with the resultant policies, including those concerning 
livelihoods, not designed with women in mind.104 For example, spatial policies that designate certain 
areas as ‘no-take’ lands, disproportionately impact women who do not have the resources to fish 
elsewhere. In these cases, women have had to resort to fishing illegally, which removes them still 
further from the formal fishing industry.105  
Limited evidence on the gender-differentiated effects of technological advancements  
Innovations in marine resource management have often been assumed to be “gender neutral”, 
meaning that approaches “leave gender out of it”.106In reality, such initiatives’ “gender neutrality” has 
contributed to male bias in their design.107 
Climate Smart Aquaculture (CSA) is a new technology that is used to ensure sustainable fishing and 
the continuity of the fish supply chain. Limited research has been conducted to see how women 
 
101 Adam et al, 2021. 
102 Österblom et al, 2020.  
103 Österblom et al, 2020. 
104 Österblom et al, 2020.  
105  Adam et al, 2021.  
106  Adam et al, 2021 
107 Ibid.  
Page 145

=== PAGE 149 ===
Page 38 of 143 
 
engage with and benefit from this new technology, despite the fact that climate change affects rural, 
low-income, communities of women particularly adversely.108Innovation in feed and seed breeding 
technologies are also lacking in gender-related analysis, resulting in a lack of evidence as to how new 
technological advancements are affecting women.109  
However, there have been some studies on gender differences in CSA initiatives. When a study was 
conducted in Bangladesh to investigate the potential role of women in CSA, several conclusions were 
made about women’s empowerment as a result of training, including over 75% of women reporting 
that they had experienced positive changes in their decision-making power.110In Bangladesh and India, 
women were found to relate fish trait preferences to household food and nutrition security.111 
Prevalence of gender-segregated interventions which fail to address underlying barriers 
Capacity development for local communities to engage in good fishing practices aims to maximise 
profits whilst reducing overfishing.112 Effective fishing practices that include women would not only 
generate wealth but also allow for better distribution of resources, and an ocean economy where all 
voices have decision making roles.113 Building institutions that support women’s employment in the 
fishing industry will protect against inequity, and prevent inefficiencies and unidimensional decision 
making. Including women in all segments of the fishery industry requires transparency, and 
monitoring of the supply chain for those who participate in illegal activity and exploitative practices 
such as labour trafficking.114 
However, existing training for women in the fishing sector largely follows a “gap filling” approach 
which seeks to accommodate (but, critically, not address) the normative barriers that women 
face.115Training tends to be held close to women's homes and often the technology or strategies 
introduced relate to jobs that can be performed from home. These interventions target women 
separately from men, and have limited impact as they fail to address the underlying power dynamics 
and barriers that perpetuate gendered inequities at the foundation of the fishing industry. These 
interventions often do not recognise that women are not viewed as “true” fishers, themselves treating 
women separately and as different from men.  
In select instances, gender transformative measures which address these underlying normative issues 
have been introduced in natural resource management, as seen in a study on the implementation of 
women-targeted training in Bangladesh.116  However, implementation in developing countries 
remains difficult. Barriers to gender-transformative approaches include low political and social will, 
poor messaging about gender norms and a lack of assistance from governments and NGOs.117 
 
108 Adam et al, 2021.  
109 Ibid. 
110 Ibid.

114 Ibid.

Global Evidence Review: Gender & climate resilient port 
infrastructure 
In this section, we present the findings from the global evidence review on climate resilient port 
infrastructure. It considers the following areas of discussion, identified as the more pertinent issues 
based on our review of the relevant literature: 
• 
The different ways in which men and women use and benefit from available port 
infrastructure; and  
• 
The different ways in which men and women are, are not, and could be, involved in and can 
benefit from the development of climate resilient port infrastructure. 
5.1 Different uses and benefits from available port infrastructure 
Women do not benefit equally from the employment benefits of port infrastructure  
Ports are a major hub for trade, and are vital for national economies as they provide job creation and 
employment opportunities. In Eastern and Southern Africa, seaborne trade accounted for 80% of total 
trade volume in 2016.118Seaports have contributed to the expansion of urban populations and growth 
of local markets.119 Women, however, are not able to fully reap the benefits of this economic growth 
due to their exclusion from operational and managerial positions.120 
Lack of evidence on the gender-differentiated effects of ports’ negative impacts on 
ecosystems  
Activities related to port infrastructures can damage city port, delta and river ecosystems, through 
land reclamation, dredging and construction. As ports grow in number, it is difficult to find areas that 
have the space for sustainable expansion.121 There is a need for port infrastructure development that 
respects the environmental constraints of a particular area, and can adapt to climate and technological 
change122. Ports were originally built with the prioritisation of trade and economic growth in mind, but 
little consideration of environmental and health impacts. No gender-disaggregated impact analysis 
could be found in this specific literature.  
5.2 Different involvement and benefits from  the development of climate 
resilient port infrastructure 
Women’s (potential) role in employment and leadership roles in climate resilient ports 
The expansion and development of new climate-resilient ports creates jobs and therefore opens up 
opportunities to train and hire women. However, according to reports from seafaring institutions, 
female trainees only made up 6% of training participants in 2021.123 
 
118 Humphreys et al, 2019.  
119 Ibid.  
120 Patrick Verhoeven, 2020.  
121 Shipper et al., 2015.  
122 Ibid.  
123 BIMCO & International Chamber of Shipping, 2021.  
Page 147

=== PAGE 151 ===
Page 40 of 143 
 
Women’s recruitment, integration and progression is starting to be supported through training, 
mentorship and working environment initiatives. Training institutions for maritime trade management 
are looking to include women in their training programmes. One step institutions have taken is to offer 
a 10% discount to female students.124 
Mentorship programmes have been implemented to connect women to those with experience in 
senior level roles, including roles that work with digital technology and autonomous vehicle 
operations.125These initiatives focus on the challenges women encounter in the maritime industry, 
finding ways of advancing and retaining female talent. With women rarely involved in the operational 
aspects of ports, initiatives may have a particular focus on teaching women how to use certain vehicles 
and technologies to build operational knowledge, skills and confidence.  
Through family-friendly human resources (HR) policies and adequate sanitation facilities for women 
(e.g. separate toilets), the development of new climate-resilient ports can encourage women to work 
in the port environment.126 
Women’s (potential) roles in promoting the importance of ecosystems  
More broadly, women have an important role to play in promoting climate resilient port development 
for the benefit of wider communities. Through the utilisation of ecosystem-based management 
approaches, the health, function and resilience of ecosystems can be restored and protected. No-
impact port development involves the development of ports which have no negative impact on the 
environment, and recognises the complexity of ecological systems, as well as the intricate ways in 
which elements of oceans and coasts interact with each other. One aspect of this is reducing noise 
pollution in order to reduce physiological stress and change on the environment. To limit disturbances 
to current ecosystems, no-impact ports are preferably created in natural locations that do not require 
dredging.127 Women are often excluded from discussions around new approaches, but tend, as noted 
above, to be more willing to engage in sustainable or environmentally friendly practices, and should 
be enabled to participate meaningfully in consultation and planning processes. 
Lack of evidence on gender-differentiated benefits of optimised infrastructure and energy 
efficiency 
Increased trade capacity and employment opportunities can stimulate local economies. The expansion 
of ports will create many jobs in the areas of operations and building, as well as increase business to 
the ports. This has the potential to grow under-developed economies in an environmentally friendly 
way. Moreover, better ports will lead to a decrease in the cost of sea and inland transport and 
operational expenses, increasing profits for post-operators and decreasing prices for port-users.128The 
optimisation of infrastructure and the use of digital technologies to combat climate change can benefit 
port users while also minimising the impact of port activity on the environment. However, the 
literature does not explore whether or how the optimisation of port infrastructure can benefit men and 
women differently. 
 
124 Ibid.  
125 Patrick Verhoeven, 2020.  
126 Patrick Verhoeven, 2020.  
127 Schipper et al. 2015.  
128 WWF & Deltares 2015.  
Page 148

=== PAGE 152 ===
Page 41 of 143 
 
Through the production and sourcing of local renewable energy, encouragement of clean energy 
initiatives and increasing energy-efficient port equipment, negative environmental externalities of 
ports can be reduced. These energy initiatives also improve the health and safety awareness of 
employees and local communities. However, the literature does not explore whether or how these 
changes benefit women and men differently.  
GHG emissions from ships can be reduced through onshore power supplying, incentivising best-
performing vehicles and investing in energy efficient infrastructure.129 Operations can be made more 
energy efficient through innovative processes, production technologies and the implementation of 
clean and renewable energy. An incentive scheme can help to promote these actions, such as an 
Environmental Ship Index that incentivises best-performing vessel initiatives. Concession agreements 
and lease clauses could be used to encourage clean energy initiatives from third parties.130 However, 
as it stands, the introduction of new technologies is likely to further marginalise women, who are often 
excluded from technical training and leadership positions. In order to limit inequities in port 
infrastructure development, it could be beneficial to engage in mentorship and training programmes 
for women first, before enacting any technical improvements.  
 
 
 
129 Patrick Verhoeven, 2020.  
130 Patrick Verhoeven, 2020.

National WASH policy and context in focal countries 
In this Section, our focus shifts to the country level to present a summary of national WASH policy and 
context in the 11 focal countries: Bangladesh, Botswana, Brazil, Djibouti, Indonesia, Ivory Coast, Kenya, 
Namibia, Nigeria, Morocco and Uganda. For each of these countries, we present a summary of country 
analyses that were completed and which can be found in full in Annex 5. These country analyses 
provide a top-line picture of access to WASH facilities, drawing on data from the WHO/UNICEF Joint 
Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene (JPWSSH)131 and making reference to JPWSSH’s 
ladders for drinking water and sanitation service (Table 4) as a means of differentiating the level of 
water and sanitation services the population has access to. Each country summary also appraises the 
WASH policy context to understand the extent to which, and how, current policy frameworks identify 
and seek to address gender issues in WASH, whilst building climate resilience. In 8 out of the 11 
country summaries, we also include a stakeholder mapping.132
