6.1 Bangladesh 
Key Messages 
Bangladesh generally has high levels of access to improved drinking water sources and relatively high 
levels of access to some form of sanitation facility. Improving water, sanitation and hygiene is a key 
part of Bangladesh’s national policy framework, with the Bangladesh Climate Change and Action Plan 
(BCCSAP) (2008) identifying connections between WASH and negative impacts caused by increased 
weather extremities. The plan identifies the need to take specific approaches for vulnerable people, 
to mainstream gender in improving hygiene promotion, including by shifting organisational culture 
and ways of thinking and by developing procedures relating to gender responsibility, accountability, 
coordination and personnel policy. While most WASH-related policies do not explicitly consider 
advancing women’s formal roles in the WASH sector, the National Women Development Policy, 
identifies a need to advance career development opportunities for women employees and equal 
participation of women in decision-making and governance at all levels. In general, women are limited 
in their careers prospects within the WASH sector by low rates of secondary education and policies 
which focus on opportunities in low-skilled jobs.  
Headline data on access to WASH services 
Access to Drinking Water: 98.9% of the population has access to an improved source for drinking 
water, of which the majority, 58.5%, is safely managed drinking water and women are the main 
collectors of water133. 
 
Access to Sanitation: 78% of the population has access to an improved facility, of which 38.7% has 
access to safely managed sanitation services. None of the population is reported to practice open 
defecation.  
Policy framework 
Improving water, sanitation and hygiene is a key part of Bangladesh’s national policy framework, as 
identified in their overall sustainable development strategy, as well as further, more specific water 
strategies. The policies recognise the increasing impacts of climate change, rapid urbanisation and 
water contamination, and inequitable access, identifying these as challenges for future development 
in water, sanitation and hygiene.  
 
Bangladesh’s location makes it highly susceptible to the adverse effects of climate change, hindering 
its development, including access to safe and economic opportunities for women and other 
vulnerable groups. The Bangladesh Climate Change and Action Plan (BCCSAP) of 2008 looks further 
into the negative impacts caused by increased weather extremities. Strategic objectives to combat 
identified challenges include ensuring safe drinking water, undertaking specific approaches for 
vulnerable people, gender mainstreaming, improving hygiene promotion, adapting to climate change 
and undertaking integrated and accountable development approaches. These objectives seek to 
include gender mainstreaming in all the service planning, implementation and monitoring by shifting 
organisational culture and ways of thinking and by developing procedures relating to gender 
responsibility, accountability, coordination and personnel policy and the consideration of women’s 
role in hygiene promotion, both as a token role and also as an empowered advocate.   
 
 
133 MICS (2019) 
Page 151

=== PAGE 155 ===
Page 44 of 143 
 
Most of the policies reviewed did not explicitly consider advancing women’s formal roles in the 
WASH sector, but they still discussed the need for community involvement in the planning, 
implementation, operation and management of water and sanitation facilities. Some policies present 
opportunities to consider for the advancement of women's involvement in, for example, 
entrepreneurial activities. Within the National Women Development Policy, there is a broader 
emphasis, within all sectors, on career development opportunities for women employees and equal 
participation of women in decision-making and governance at all levels. Additionally, due to only 
21.1% of women completing upper secondary schooling134 and 19.8% enrolment in tertiary 
education,135 women are limited in the careers prospects within the WASH sector, and a focus on 
policies is placed on opportunities in low-skilled jobs.  
Stakeholder Mapping 
In Bangladesh, the Policy Support branch of the Ministry of Local Government, Rural Development 
and Cooperatives has primary responsibility for Water and Sanitation Supply and Strategy. Other 
national Ministries with some WASH responsibility include the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, 
for hygiene, and Ministry of Water Resources (MoWR), covering irrigation, flooding, and climate-
related water protection.  
6.2 Botswana 
Key Messages 
Botswana has high levels of access to at least basic sources of drinking water and basic sanitation 
services. In the policy environment, key challenges identified in the WASH sphere include water 
shortages and droughts in the water sector, and the inadequate capacity of sanitation infrastructure, 
and pressure on sanitation and health services because of increasing levels of urbanisation, facilitate 
access to water of suitable quality adopting ‘smart agriculture’ as an opportunity and working at the 
interplay between climate change, the water sector and public health. No specific policies related to 
women and WASH are identified, but the National Development Plan calls for equal participation and 
representation of all women and men in all development processes. Key challenges and opportunities 
identified in the policy landscape include the currently low participation of females in STEM subjects, 
a need for gender-sensitive policies for climate change and including gender and climate change into 
academic curriculum.  
Headline data on access to WASH services 
Access to drinking water: 92.2% of the population has access to a basic level of drinking water and 
4.7% of the population has access to a limited level of drinking water.  1.7% of the population relies 
on unimproved drinking water whilst 1.3% of the population has access only to surface drinking water. 
Th 
 
Access to sanitation: 80% of the population has access to basic sanitation services and 5.6% of the 
population has access to a limited level of sanitation services. 9.8% of the population practices open 
defecation in sanitation services.  
 
134 UNIS (2019) 
135 UNIS (2020) 
Page 152

=== PAGE 156 ===
Page 45 of 143 
 
Policy framework 
Botswana’s WASH policy landscape is focused around its 11th National Development Plan, including 
Botswana’s Vision 2036. One of the key development challenges facing Botswana’s population 
includes water resources, and related sections on migration, population distribution and urbanisation. 
Challenges identified here, and further elaborated on in the National Water Policy include water 
shortages and droughts in the water sector, and the inadequate capacity of sanitation infrastructure, 
and pressure on sanitation and health services because of increasing levels of urbanisation, as well 
as further WASH-related health consequences. 
 
Future objectives is set around a framework that will facilitate access to water of suitable quality and 
standards for the citizenry and provide the foundations for sustainable development of water 
resources in support of economic growth, diversification and poverty eradication. Climate is a key 
consideration throughout all documents, including the Climate change policy. They identify water 
shortages and droughts and water supply for agriculture and industry as key issues, and expanding on 
adopting ‘smart agriculture’ as an opportunity. Between documents, interplay between climate 
change, the water sector and public health is pointed out. The National Health Policy elaborates on 
the impact of water shortages and their potential for causing malnutrition, and also identifies the 
negative public health consequences of a lack of access to adequate sanitation and safe drinking 
water.  
 
The National Development Plan largely ignores specific gender considerations in the water and 
sanitation sectors. However, it does state that the government recognizes the need for equal 
participation and representation of all women and men in all development processes. In further 
policies, an opportunity is identified in working towards gender and social equity in accessing water 
resources, in particular, women shall be empowered to participate fully in issues and decisions relating 
to sustainable development and management of water resources. Although it is identified often as an 
important consideration, throughout all policies it is not further elaborated how this will be 
operationalised. 
 
There are some key gender challenges and opportunities identified around over the policy landscape. 
First, the low participation of females in  Science, Technology, Engineering and Mathematics (STEM) 
subjects is identified as a key challenge facing the education sector, despite women having relatively 
high school completion rates. However, they fail to articulate how this challenge impacts women’s 
employment in the water and sanitation sectors. Second, Botswana’s climate policies discuss that 
measures should be gender sensitive, by empowering women and youth to actively participate in 
the implementation of climate response measures, adopting strategies that are targeted at increasing 
women and youth’s resilience to climate change, and including gender and climate change into 
academic curriculum at all levels.  
6.3 Brazil 
Key Messages 
Brazil has a high rate of access both to improved water sources for drinking water and to improved 
sanitation services. Brazil’s National Policies for Water and Sanitation identify the importance of WASH 
in daily life and lay out clear objectives to ensure the impact on human livelihoods and activities. These 
policies present a number of opportunities within the sector, for instance working to increase 
environmental education and capacity building in technology and science, and both seek to 
incorporate gender equality in all actions and to develop ongoing training that promotes female 
Page 153

=== PAGE 157 ===
Page 46 of 143 
 
leadership. In addition, at the Federal level, policies emphasise the need to achieve equality, including 
from a gender perspective. There are few specific recommendations with regard to WASH and gender 
at either national or Federal level, however. 
Headline data on access to WASH services 
Access to drinking water: 99.3% of the population has access to an improved water source for drinking 
water, of which the majority, 85.8%, has access to safely managed drinking water, and only 0.1% has 
access to a limited level of drinking water.  
Access to sanitation: 90.1% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 
48.7% has access to safely managed sanitation service, while 9.8% of the population uses unimproved 
sanitation services, including 0.3% who practice open defecation2.   
Policy framework 
Brazil’s National Policies for Water and Sanitation both discuss in-depth the importance of WASH in 
daily life and lay out clear objectives to ensure the impact on human livelihoods and activities, such as 
business, is limited. They examine the negative impacts that water scarcity creates in vulnerable 
communities, including increase in conflict, siltation of water, water pollution, and changes in natural 
characteristics from high degradation. They add the impact of climate change leaders to further 
alterations to available water resources and impacts both the energy and agricultural sector. They 
present innovative opportunities within the sector, all working to increase environmental education 
and capacity building in technology and science for water resource management. They also seeks to 
incorporate gender equality in all actions and develop ongoing training that promotes female 
leadership and empowerment, but does not provide any detail on practical implications of this 
intention.   
In terms of sanitation, Brazil’s policies identifies issues with parts of the population continuing to lack 
access to basic sanitation facilities and/or proper disposal of solid waste and associated health 
consequences.  There are clear objectives and programmes to overcome identified issues, such as 
increasing and improving infrastructure, management, and monitoring of sanitation facilities, and the 
suggestion of creating a circular economy for solid waste disposal. It does not explicitly mention 
gender in the policy objectives. The cross between health and climate change is brought up the 
country’s climate change policies, discussing the impacts extreme weather has on the health and 
livelihoods of people, which includes the effect it has on water reserves in the country. 
As a federal state, Brazil’s lower tiers of government play an important role in the provision of 
sustainable WASH services. The current National Policy for Regional Development II (2019) 
emphasises achieving equality, within regions and between regions. It seeks to ensure equal access to 
economic opportunities within all the regions, both from a socio-economic and gender perspective. 
However, there is no specific discussion of WASH-related challenges and objectives in the policy, nor 
are there are gender specific strategies within outlined climate action measures. Brazil’s policies 
further explore the importance of including a gender lens in the sustainable use of water, and 
emphasises the importance of valuing women’s contribution to farm work and equal participation in 
leadership and decision making. Moreover, the Education and Health Plans aim to reduce inequality 
in their respective systems. 
 
 
 
Page 154

=== PAGE 158 ===
Page 47 of 143 
 
6.4 Djibouti 
Key messages 
Djibouti generally has high levels of access to improved sources of drinking water and relatively high 
levels of access to improved sanitation services. It’s policy framework identifies a number of areas of 
connection between WASH and climate change and although connections between WASH and gender 
aren’t typically made explicitly, it’s Vision 2035 acknowledges women’s’ role in enforcing new 
behaviours and practices as well as the importance of engaging men to change their views to women’s 
role. Policies also acknowledge that various stakeholders, including women and youth, should have a 
larger role in the policy-making process. Finally, policies recognise opportunities to incorporate 
women into the workforce due to their higher levels of education. 
Headline data on access to WASH services 
Access to drinking water: 90.8% of the population has access to an improved source of drinking 
water, although for the majority (76%), this is basic access[1] and women are the main collectors of 
water.[2] 
Access to sanitation: 73.8% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 
37.1% has access to safely managed sanitation services. 
Policy framework 
There are a number of key policies which relate to WASH and gender in the Djibouti policy 
environment and which offer opportunities for engagement. For instance, the Djibouti 2035 Vision 
(2010–2035) presents a long-term developmental vision for sustained growth and with respect to 
WASH, the vision focuses primarily on water supply throughout the country and its implications for 
other areas of the primary sector, with a lesser focus on sanitation and hygiene. This is complemented 
by the climate section of the vision, which includes sustainable water management as an area of 
priority, amongst other strategy objectives unrelated to WASH. Furthermore, Vision 2035 identifies 
the link between climate change and WASH, specifically in terms of how they will experience 
increased instances of droughts and flooding which will impact food production and supplies 
throughout the country. 
 
Vision 2035 includes a specific section on gender which acknowledges womens’ role in enforcing new 
behaviours and practices; however, it emphasises how ’in order to improve women's situation, men 
should be included in the project. It is important to work with men to push them into changing their 
approaches towards women and their approach with themselves’ (Djibouti 2035 Vision, 2010, p. 98). 
 
The 2010 Development Plan for the Primary Sector and the 2015 Strategy of Accelerated Growth and 
Promotion of Employment echo the focus on access to water for agriculture and livestock, as well as 
the hygiene risks that a lack of sanitation infrastructure poses in cities. Although the latter two policies 
touch on gender and the need to address gender inequity throughout the primary sector and for 
growth, they do not directly link WASH issues to gender. The problems identified, such as health 
consequences and hard to reach water points, are known to generally affect women more, but this is 
not specified in the policies. They do acknowledge that various stakeholders, including women and 
youth, should have a larger role in the policy-making process. 
 
Page 155

=== PAGE 159 ===
Page 48 of 143 
 
Education is a strong focus of Djibouti’s 2011–2021 Gender Policy, the policy that most identifies how 
water and sanitation challenges are an extra burden on women. The policy covers accessibility to 
water and sanitation facilities in their communities, remoteness of water points and sanitation deficits 
in their immediate environments. Currently, the completion rate for upper secondary is higher for 
women than for men; this presents a great opportunity to incorporate women into the workforce 
due to their higher levels of education. To this end, the 2018—2022 National Strategy for Social 
Protection identifies a role for women in the country’s goal to increase primary sector employment, 
specifically in fishing and agriculture. 
Stakeholder Mapping 
In Djibouti, the Ministry of Agriculture, Water, Fisheries, Livestock and Fisheries Resources and the 
National Office of Water and Sanitation have primary responsibility for WASH. Other national 
Ministries with some WASH responsibility include the Ministry of Health, the leading ministry in 
implementing sanitation and hygiene programmes at national level, and the Ministry of Housing, 
Urbanism, Environment and Territorial Development, who monitor WASH service delivery.  
6.5 Indonesia 
Key Messages 
Indonesia has both high levels of access to improved water sources for drinking water and access to 
improved sanitation services. Faced by the challenges of climate change and rapid urbanisation, in 
terms of the policy environment it sees water supply and increasing sanitation facilities as a key 
component to ensure self-reliance and development for all people. National climate policies highlight 
that climate-related challenges disproportionally impact women due to issues such as limited access 
to water supplies for domestic and economic use and lower rates of access to safe sanitation facilities. 
WASH and health policies recognise the need for gender mainstreaming to reach the country’s long-
term goals but entry points in WASH are not explored, such as providing career opportunities in the 
WASH sector. However, the National Plan identifies more broadly the need to increase women’s role 
in development and decision-making, especially within health, education and the labour market. 
Headline data on access to WASH services 
Access to drinking water: 93.3% of the population has access to an improved water source for drinking 
water, of which almost all, 92.4%, have access to a basic level of drinking water. 
Access to sanitation: 92.6% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 
86.5% has access to a basic level of sanitation service, and 6.1% who practice open defecation136.  
Policy framework 
As a country with over 200 million spread out over 6,000 islands, and a major reliance on raw water 
supply to provide for their water resource, Indonesia has placed natural resource promotion and 
recovery as a priority. With the growing effects of climate change impacting the water cycle, rapid 
urbanization, and contamination of water supply it sees water supply and increasing sanitation 
facilities as a key component to ensuring self-reliance and development for all people. Whilst it 
 
136 JMP 2020 
Page 156

=== PAGE 160 ===
Page 49 of 143 
 
recognises the need for gender mainstreaming in policies to reach the country’s long-term goals, the 
specific gender dimensions of WASH are not explored.   
 
The National Plan talks about using the 3R approach - Reduce, Reuse, Recycle - and the circular 
economy to increase effective waste disposal, to address cross-cutting issues of climate and WASH. 
They aim for 90% of the population to have access to safe and proper sanitation. As for water 
resources, they seeks to improve infrastructure to meet the goal of 100% of the population having 
access to safe drinking water. Policies see community involvement as a main way to ensure 
implementation and management of safe and adequate water and sanitation facilities and practices. 
This includes increasing women’s role in development and decision-making, especially within 
health, education, labour market, economy, and public sectors. National climate policies highlight 
that climate-related challenges are something that disproportionally impacts women due to limited 
access to water supplies for domestic and economic use, limited access to safe sanitation facilities, 
and an imbalance in capacity for understanding the importance of safe water and sanitation practices.  
Health concerns due to poor handwashing and water contamination persist in the country, and this is 
attributed to the lack of progress by local governments. The national Health Strategy presents 
opportunities for governments to partner with NGOs and the private sector, subsidise communal 
sanitation facilities, and encourage community engagement. The Health Strategy does not consider 
gender-related issues in community-based sanitation and there is no consideration to the specific 
sanitation needs of women. However, all the policies discuss increasing the capacity of communities 
and workers to lead to increased community engagement and awareness of these issues, but there is 
limited or no discussion about providing women specifically with career opportunities in the WASH 
sector.  
6.6 Ivory Coast 
Key Messages  
Ivory Coast has average levels of access to improved sources of drinking water as well as improved 
sanitation services, although they perform stronger on drinking water access. A high proportion of 
their population still practice open defecation. It’s policy framework has a strong focus on structural 
economic reforms, balanced with an understanding that these go hand in hand with the development 
of human capital. The two largest problems that are brought out are very low water availability, 
worsening due to climate change, as well as high levels of disease due to poor water and sanitation 
facilities. Policies argue for diversified representation and improved governance from better 
consideration of gender in public policies. However, all policies are quite gender blind and not propose 
how to create gender-inclusive WASH environments, other than brief mention of increasing inclusion 
of women in the employment sector. 
Headline data on access to WASH services: 
Access to drinking water: 79.8% of the population has access to an improved water source, of which 
only 35.2% has access to safely managed drinking water. 20.2% of the population access unimproved 
Page 157

=== PAGE 161 ===
Page 50 of 143 
 
drinking water, including 6% who only access surface water.137 Women are the main collectors of 
water.138  
 
Access to sanitation: 57.1% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of whom 
34.6% has access to basic sanitation service, while 25.3% of the population still practices open 
defecation. 
Policy framework 
Ivory Coast’s national development plan has a strong focus on structural economic reforms, which is 
balanced with an understanding that these go hand in hand with the development of human capital, 
one of which is water and sanitation. The challenges that Ivory Coast faces in water and sanitation 
range from governance of resources, quality of health services and the population’s health, and lastly, 
lacking infrastructure. Beyond the general lack of access to water and sustainable sanitation systems, 
it is acknowledged there is a heavier burden on populations in cities that are rapidly urbanizing. 
Consequences, such as the rapid spread of diseases like cholera, and food shortages due to water 
scarcity often impacts women more severely. In terms of solutions, they aim to increase accessibility 
to drinking water, build new water pumps and transfer the responsibility and ownership of adopting 
laws and regulations to local communities. Local communities should also include women’s 
associations and further diversified representation.  
Climate Change programmes further discuss health consequences of water scarcity and cleanliness, 
in changing circumstances. Suggested strategies focus on more than just the construction of new 
facilities and assure uptake of these new facilities to ensure their effectiveness. They have 
demonstrated that urban populations are more vulnerable, but there is no mention of the 
differentiating impact between women and men. The National Development Plan does, however, 
identify how improved governance will result from better consideration of gender and the 
environment in public policies. With regard to gender, the strengthening of the institutional and 
regulatory framework will be necessary, as will the development of the skills of actors who apply it in 
plans, programs and projects. Furthermore, the plan proposes increased vocational training for 
women to include them in the employment sector, boosted by the improvements in water and 
sanitation infrastructure.  
Stakeholder Mapping 
In Ivory Coast, the Ministry of Hydraulics, Sanitation and Hygiene has primary responsibility for WASH. 
Other national Ministries with some WASH responsibility include the Ministry of the Environment of 
Urban Sanitation and Sustainable Development, who have a key role in the adaptation to climate 
change, and the Ministry of Health and Public Hygiene, leading on hygiene policy and programmes 
 
 
.

6.7 Kenya 
Key Messages 
Kenya has average levels of access to improved sources of drinking water as well as improved 
sanitation services, although they perform stronger on drinking water access. It’s policy framework 
focuses on climate change, lacking infrastructure and lack of strategy as the main causes of lacking 
water and sanitation access. Approaches for improvement focus on capacity building, public 
participation, adoption of innovative technical approaches, and multi-sectoral collaboration. 
Women’s more disadvantaged position in society, and the negative consequences poor WASH 
facilities have on health and education are recognised throughout and a big emphasis is put on job 
opportunities and the inclusion of women in leadership. The impacts of climate are discussed but do 
not relate directly to gender. 
Headline data on access to WASH services 
Access to water: 71.1% of the population has access to an improved water source, of which 61.6% has 
access to basic drinking water and 9.5% has access to drinking water categorised as ‘limited.’  
 
Access to sanitation: 58.2% of the population has access to improved sanitation services while 41.8% 
of the population uses unimproved sanitation services, with 8.5% practicing open defecation.139   
Policy framework 
The drive to advance livelihoods in Kenya is laid out through the four key areas, dubbed the Big Four 
Agenda, within the National Development plan. Water, sanitation and hygiene continues to be a key 
sub-focus area. Within the plan it sees environmental degradation, climate change, inadequate 
infrastructure and lack of water conservation strategies as hindrances to the development of a clean, 
safe environment and access to water and sanitation services. Approaches identified to combat 
these challenges are capacity building, public participation, adoption of innovative technical 
approaches, multi-sectoral collaboration and adequate water and sanitation facilities.  
 
There is mention of the under-valuation of women’s contribution to the economy, under-
employment and skills mismatch. In relation to health issues, key issues are identified such as the 
mortality and morbidity burden associated with unsafe water and sanitation and the risk of rape and 
domestic violence for women and girls due to inadequate sanitation facilities. Moreover, within 
schools, girls tend to be more affected by inadequate WASH facilities, and this impacts their 
attendance and can expose them to increased risks of gender-based violence. To limit the impact on 
children’s schooling, the National Education Sector Strategic Plan includes, among its objectives, the 
construction of facilities for safe and clean drinking water in schools and separate sanitation facilities 
for boys and girls.  
 
To address these issues, they intend to scale up access to improved rural and urban sanitation and 
build governance and leadership capacity for sanitation. The policy discusses an increase in 
education, training and skill development for sanitation, including increasing the availability of skilled 
personnel in the planning and management of sanitation facilities, though it does not suggest these 
efforts are focused on any one gender/sex. Interwoven throughout these policies is the continual 
discussion on the inclusion of equal opportunities for stakeholder participation throughout the 
planning, implementation and management of water and sanitation projects/programmes and the 
 
139 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2020) 
Page 159

=== PAGE 163 ===
Page 52 of 143 
 
push for participation of female leaders on community-led committees through gender 
mainstreaming. While most policies include opportunities for job creation, many do not discuss career 
opportunities for women specifically, with a specific focus on seeking to encourage women’s 
participation in the technologies and sciences and STEM based courses.  
 
With climate change being a major discussion in most of the policies, the National Climate Change 
Policy examines how increases in weather extremities, including droughts, floods and water 
salinisation greatly impacts the water, sanitation and hygiene sector, with the biggest focus being on 
inadequate sanitation systems which lead to clogged drains and water contamination.  
Stakeholder Mapping 
In Kenya, the Ministry of Water and Irrigation has primary responsibility for WASH. Other national 
Ministries with some WASH responsibility include the Ministry of Health, responsible for sanitation 
and Hygiene provision and policy, and the Ministry of Environments and Forestry, who set 
environmental standards, and the Ministry of Housing, who handle WASH infrastructure.  
6.8 Namibia 
Key Messages 
Namibia has a high level of access to improved sources of drinking water, with over 90% of the 
population, while less than half of the population has access to improved sanitation services. It’s policy 
framework looks at water and sanitation as two separate sectors, with the former focusing on water 
availability and infrastructure and the latter focuses on health consequences and more on delivering 
through advocacy efforts. While all policies reference gender as a cross-cutting issue in the water and 
sanitation sectors, none explore in any great detail how women, youths and marginalised groups are 
impacted nor the roles that these groups can play in these sectors. The policy landscape recognizes 
that women continue to be under-represented, and highlights the importance of education. 
Headline data on access to WASH services 
Access to drinking water: 91.4% of the population has access to an improved source of drinking water, 
of which 84.3% has access to basic drinking water and 7.1% has access to limited drinking water. 
 
Access to sanitation: 48.1% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 
35.3% has access to basic sanitation services. 47.2% of the population continues to practice open 
defecation.  
Policy framework 
Namibia’s National Development Plan looks at water and sanitation as two separate sectors, rather 
than looking at water and sanitation through a combined WASH lens. As such, analysis of the 
challenges faced in the water and sanitation sectors fall within two separate pillars. The first is 
economic progress, focusing on water availability and demand in urban areas and for agriculture and 
infrastructure. The second is social transformation, focused on improved sanitation systems and 
infrastructure as well strengthening advocacy. 
Namibia’s Water Supply and Sanitation Policy (2008) goes into detail on the challenges themselves, 
the context, the health consequences and Namibia’s policy objectives in response to these challenges. 
Page 160

=== PAGE 164 ===
Page 53 of 143 
 
They continue to handle water availability and infrastructure separately from sanitation challenges. 
Namibia’s 2011 National Policy on Climate Change also analyses the issues of water supply and 
provision of sanitation services, but focuses on the impact of climate change on both sectors, as well 
as the potential health consequences and Namibia’s vulnerability of water supply to climate change is 
recognized. 
While all policies reference gender as a cross-cutting issue in the water and sanitation sectors, none 
explore in any great detail how women, youths and marginalised groups are impacted nor the roles 
that these groups can play in these sectors. Namibia’s National Gender Policy explicitly tackles the 
gender issues affecting the water and sanitation sectors. The policy argues that women continue to 
be under-represented in policy and decision-making forums on the utilisation of natural resources, 
environmental development and water management. The policy also explores the roles that women 
play within the water and sanitation sectors, as well as exploring the health inequalities related to 
water and sanitation. A lack of these basic amenities has a detrimental effect on the health of rural 
communities, and this disproportionately affects women and female-headed households.  
The policy states that the government will include gender and climate change, including issues such 
as water supply, in the curriculum of education and training programmes. However, all policies are 
very limited in their consideration of women’s employment levels in the water and sanitation sectors 
and in terms of opportunities for women to access employment in these sectors. It is put forward that, 
as a strategy for gender mainstreaming, women should be involved in interventions to achieve the 
objectives of environmental sustainability, including in the water and sanitation sectors. 
Stakeholder Mapping 
In Namibia, the Ministry of Agriculture, Water and Land Reform has primary responsibility for WASH. 
Other national Ministries with some WASH responsibility include Ministry of Urban and Rural 
Development, ensuring implementation of national WASH strategy for urban settlements, and the 
Ministry of Health and Social Services, implementing and enforcing hygiene practices.  
6.9 Nigeria 
Key Messages 
Nigeria has relatively high levels of access to improved water sources, although much of this is still at 
a basic level, but less than half of their population has access to improved sanitation services. It’s 
policy framework presents a balance of WASH-related issues between economic impacts, governance 
and coordination issues, and health and culture. The importance of including women in the planning, 
implementation and management of programmes is recognised throughout, seeking to integrate a 
gender perspective in all their solutions. In all policies reviewed, there was a clear theme around 
capacity building and job creation. Their policies do not go much further than recognising the 
importance of inclusion into suggesting gender specific policies and opportunities. 
Headline data on access to WASH services 
Access to drinking water: 78% of the population has access to an improved water source for drinking 
water, of which the majority, 55.9%, is access to a basic level of drinking water. 40% of the population 
are women who are the main collectors of water. 
  
Page 161

=== PAGE 165 ===
Page 54 of 143 
 
Access to sanitation: 43% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 30.5% 
has access to safely managed sanitation service. 18.7% practice open defecation.140 
Policy Framework 
As Nigeria continues to experience rapid urbanisation and growth, they also aim to socially develop in 
areas of water resources and sanitation, water and gender equity and employment, and job creation. 
Challenges identified include ensuring economic diversification, private sector investment in WASH 
services, lack of coordination and communal disputes, negative impacts of climate change, 
contamination of water sources and it’s impacts on health and poor operation and maintenance 
culture. Some of the main ways they seek to overcome these challenges is by continuing to improve 
sanitation services, extending water supply access and adopting an integrated planning and policy 
formulation approach, while also increasing training, developing the bioeconomy and circular 
economy, and increasing access to finance and technical support. 
 
One recurring theme in their policies is the increase in negative impacts on Nigeria’s development 
brought about by climate change. Their climate change policy examines the impact that extreme 
weather places on an already strained water, sanitation and hygiene sector. Nigeria seeks to 
overcome issues related to climate change and inadequate water and waste infrastructure through 
the implementation of a circular economy, as well as increasing infrastructure and reforms in the 
establishment, management, operation and maintenance of water supply and sanitation schemes and 
services. They recognise the importance of including women in the planning, implementation and 
management of programmes, and seek to integrate a gender perspective in the adoption of new 
technologies and infrastructure. 
 
In all the policies reviewed, there was a clear theme around capacity building and job creation. Within 
the Climate Change Policy, there is discussion on collaboration with the Federal Ministry of Women 
Affairs to ensure equitable policies and programmes. This discussion seeks to encourage women’s 
participation in the planning and management of climate change programmes. This includes 
investment to promote water security and climate change resilient growth and development to 
sustain jobs, employment, economic growth and social stability. The National Gender Policy of 2006 
looks at the importance of including women in the work force and actively participating in decision 
making, calling for equal inclusion throughout.  
Stakeholder Mapping 
In Nigeria, the Ministry of Water Resources has primary responsibility for WASH. Other national 
Ministries with some WASH responsibility include The National Council on Water Resources, a water 
resources policy formulating body, and the Ministry of Health and Ministry of Education, who have 
roles formulating community sanitation and hygiene.

6.10 Morocco 
Key Messages 
Morocco has a very high level of access to improved water sources and relatively high levels of access 
to improved sanitation services. It’s policy framework is centred around creating a sustainable water 
and sanitation system as it identified the important crossover between climate change and WASH. 
Throughout its policies, there is an emphasis on combatting social inequalities; however, they often 
remind quite gender blind and don’t present concrete action points on how to create gender-sensitive 
programmes and systems. There is recognition that women are more vulnerable to WASH-related 
health and climate impacts, and places importance on equal participation, as well as capacity building 
and training for all communities. 
Headline data on access to WASH services 
Access to drinking water: 90% of the population has access to an improved water source for drinking 
water, of which the majority, 79.9%, has access to safely managed drinking water. 
  
Access to sanitation: 87% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, while 9.5% of 
the population uses unimproved sanitation services, including 1.7% who practice open defecation.141  
Policy Framework 
Due to continued threats to available water resources throughout the country, the National 
Sustainable Development Policy 2017 (NSDP) recognises the importance of water, sanitation and 
hygiene improvements for the development of the country. Not only does it seek to improve facilities, 
access and programmes around water, sanitation and hygiene, it identifies the opportunities available 
for transitioning to a circular economy through recycling water usage. It also examines the need for 
human development and combat social inequalities and promotes equality, however, it remains 
quite gender blind with regard to the incorporation of gender in policies.  
 
Moroccan Climate Change Policy 2014 highlights the impact of climate change on health and water 
access in the country, which impact women and girls more than men as women are typically 
responsible for managing water. It also points to the importance of increasing financing and 
programmes focused on combating climate change, including education and awareness on 
alternative solutions and the re-use of water resources. Morocco’s water policies also sets out the 
need to protect communities against floods, especially for sanitation facilities and agricultural 
drainage systems.  
 
Within all these policies, there is no clear examination of how to increase job opportunities for 
women. However, they discusses increasing employment opportunities more generally. The Gender 
Strategy sees the value of ensuring there is equal participation of women and men in communities, 
especially around decision-making, and identifies the impact that inadequate water and sanitation 
facilities place on women, such as leaving them more vulnerable to illness, it also discusses the 
importance of equal access to education. The Climate Change Policy further discusses the importance 
of capacity building and training for individuals, communities, interest groups and public and private 
enterprises.

Stakeholder Mapping 
In Morocco, the Ministry of Interior has primary responsibility for WASH. Other national Ministries 
with some WASH responsibility include The General Directorate of Hydraulics, the lead agency for 
water resource planning, and The Ministry of Health, responsible for drinking water quality control 
and water-related laws.  
6.11 Uganda 
Key Messages 
Uganda has a relatively high access to improved sources of drinking water, but has low access to 
improved sanitation services. It’s policy framework strikes a balance between a good quality of life for 
all and economic productivity, therefore focusing their water programmes on investing in the effective 
management of the entire WASH value chain segments. The country’s policies are built around the 
worsening impacts of climate change. Connections between climate change, WASH, and gender are 
quite explicit throughout, with emphasis on the disproportionate burden that women and girls suffer. 
There is a commitment to mainstream gender issues throughout policy and practice, and a strong 
focus on including women in employment or income-generating opportunities. Although there is a 
lack of practical points to implement gender-sensitive policies, there is a commitment to stakeholder 
participation throughout all phases of a programme. 
Headline data on access to WASH services: 
Access to drinking water: 83.1% of the population has access to an improved water source for drinking 
water, of which a large part, 39.2%, has access only to a basic level of drinking water. 
 
Access to sanitation: 37.4% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, while 62.6% 
of the population uses unimproved sanitation services, including 4.7% who practice open 
defecation.142  
Policy Framework 
There has been a recent decreased attention to water, sanitation and hygiene in national policy in 
Uganda. Nevertheless, water, sanitation and hygiene remain a core area of Uganda’s Human 
Development Programme, one which seeks to improve labour productivity for increased 
competitiveness and better quality of life for all. It also foresees investing in the effective 
management of the entire WASH value chain segments, including containment, emptying, 
transportation, treatment, safe reuse and disposal. 
It is recognised that all development will be more challenging due to the effects of climate change, 
with more extreme weather patterns leading to increased water stress, impacting water availability 
and quality for both domestic and commercial uses as well as the water and sanitation infrastructure 
already in place. The Uganda National Climate Change Policy (2015—2020) articulates the challenges 
further and highlights that, given their domestic role, women are likely to experience the effects of 
climate change on water and sanitation facilities most keenly. It commits to mainstream gender 
issues across climate change adaptation and mitigation actions including ensuring climate change 
related policies and activities are gender sensitive. 
 
142 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2020) 
Page 164

=== PAGE 168 ===
Page 57 of 143 
 
The National Water and Sanitation Gender Strategy seeks to ‘empower men, women, boys, girls and 
vulnerable groups through ensuring equity in access to and control of resources in the water and 
sanitation sub-sector’. The Strategy highlights the burden and risks for women and girls that come 
with poor access to water, sanitation and hygiene. In Uganda’s Water Policies, the most direct benefits 
to women and girls are likely to come through the rehabilitation and expansion of water and sanitation 
facilities, as well as support so women and girls can take advantage of employment or income-
generating opportunities. The Water and Sanitation Gender Strategy recognises the importance of 
building the capacity of Ministry of Water and Education staff on gender and of encouraging a gender-
sensitive work environment, but stops short from promoting actions to increase women’s 
participation in formal roles in the planning, development, management and maintenance of water 
and sanitation facilities. Interestingly, the National Development Plan III outlines clear plans for 
expanding education provision in Science, Technology, Engineering and Mathematics and increasing 
the number of graduates in these areas, but places no emphasis on encouraging women specifically 
into education and careers in these areas.  
There is a commitment to stakeholder participation in the planning, implementation and 
maintenance of water and sanitation projects. Whilst this commitment provides opportunities for 
women and girls to influence the development of water and sanitation infrastructure in their 
communities, they bring an additional time burden which women and girls may struggle to take on. 
Social traditions which encourage women and girls to defer to men may inhibit women and girls’ 
abilities to participate further. The Second National Health Policy and the National Education and 
Sports Strategic Plan also highlights that, as the main users of primary health centres, women and 
girls can be severely affected by inadequate water, sanitation and hygiene practices in these centres. 
Similarly, inadequate toilet facilities are often a barrier to girls’ attendance at school. The Education 
and Sports Sector Plan includes, amongst its objectives, the expansion and improvement of safe water 
supply infrastructure to government supported primary schools as well as the construction of 
sanitation facilities primarily in primary schools.  
Stakeholder Mapping 
In Uganda, the Ministry of Water and Environment has primary responsibility for WASH. Other 
national Ministries with some WASH responsibility include the Ministry of Health, responsible for 
sanitation and hygiene, as well as WASH in health care facilities, and the Ministry of Education and 
Sports, which has responsibility for WASH in schools.

Problem Tree Analyses 
The global evidence reviews for WASH, Wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems, and port 
infrastructure, have identified the most pertinent issues related to gender and climate change in these 
sectors. To pull together our findings and present the main causes and effects of issues from a gender 
perspective per sector, problem tree analyses have been created per sector. They have been prepared 
in line with GCF guidance and draw on and distil the main issues emerging from the global evidence 
reviews, and the national policy reviews for WASH. By unpacking the causes and effects, it identifies 
some of the central issues that CI2 investments need to address to promote gender equality, issues, 
which have directly informed the opportunities presented in the section 8 of this report.    
7.1 WASH 
The WASH global evidence review and national policy review have both highlighted the multiple ways 
in which women and girls are affected by inadequate WASH infrastructures and services. They have 
also pointed to some of the causes of this situation. Whilst national policies in focal countries commit 
to tackling this core problem, in addition to tackling climate change and mainstreaming gender 
through policy action, few countries appear to have a considered and comprehensive approach 
promoting gender equality through investments in the WASH sector. The problem tree analysis 
presented here seeks to unpack the causes and effects of inadequate WASH infrastructure and 
services from a gender perspective.

7.2 Wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems 
As regards wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems, the Gender Assessment found that women are 
mostly excluded from the formal sector and most profitable parts of value chains in oceans-based 
economies, as well as from dialogue and decision making around community-based protection and 
management initiatives. At the same time, and in spite of gaps in the evidence base on climate-related 
impacts, they are likely to be disproportionately affected by damage to ecosystems. The causes and 
effects of this situation are summarized in the Problem Tree Analysis below (Figure 5).

Opportunities for promoting greater gender equality  
Although global evidence with a combined focus on gender, WASH and climate remains relatively 
sparse, our review has highlighted the significant burden placed on women and girls by inadequate 
access to WASH, access that volatilities associated with climate change are exacerbating. Our national 
policy and context analysis echoes this, pointing to the opportunity costs associated with the time 
women and girls spend collecting water and their increased risk to disease and gender-based violence. 
Distilling this evidence in a problem tree analysis highlights the need for more investment in climate 
resilient WASH, investment which women and girls have the opportunity to influence so that it 
responds to their needs and from which they should expect to benefit.  
The existing evidence base on the intersect between gender, climate and, each of, the protection and 
management of wetlands, coastal and marine ecosystems and climate resilient port infrastructure is 
more scant still, but likewise suggests a lack of understanding of the gender-differentiated impacts of 
climate change in these sectors. The literature also points to widespread exclusion of women from 
participation in formal economies, as well as dialogue and decision making around relevant initiatives. 
Despite this sombre picture, our policy analysis shows that national governments in the focal countries 
have made important commitments to continue improving access to WASH for households, as well as 
in health and education facilities, and to do this in a climate-smart way. Generally speaking, there is 
an intention to do this in a gender-responsive way, with opportunities for women to shape service 
design, delivery and management.  
Drawing on our problem analyses, and the evidence we have reviewed, we can identify four areas of 
opportunity for CFM to promote gender equality through CI2 investments. These areas of opportunity 
are discussed in this section 8 of the gender assessment.  The gender action plan in Annex 6 
summarises the actions to be taken by CFM, and at Fund level, in order to mainstream gender issues 
in the investment lifecycle of the CI2 fund.  
8.1 Opportunity Area 1 
Making better quality, climate-smart WASH facilities more accessible in urban and rural 
communities and in schools and health facilities  
Building on the progress already made to improve the population’s access to WASH facilities has the 
potential to bring multiple direct benefits to women and girls. Such benefits include: 
▪ 
Reducing the time women and girls spend collecting and cleaning water, freeing up time 
for other activities, such as attending school, or engaging in income generating activities. 
▪ 
Reducing the incidence of illness in the family and the associated care burden that women 
and girls carry. 
▪ 
Reducing women and girls’ risk of being subjected to GBV as a result of travelling long 
distances to fetch water, to inadequate sanitation services, and to conflict in the 
household associated with expenditure on WASH services. 
▪ 
Increasing women and girls’ physical and mental well-being for being better able to care 
for themselves, including during menstruation, and for an improved sense of personal 
dignity. 
Page 169

=== PAGE 173 ===
Page 62 of 143 
 
▪ 
Development of WASH facilities in primary and secondary schools to contribute to 
improving girls’ school attendance and their completion of basic education. This brings 
benefits to the individual girls concerned, but as evidence suggests, also brings it to their 
families and to society more generally. 
▪ 
Development of WASH facilities in health centres and hospitals, not only to reduce the 
incidence of disease but also to help lower mortality rates. 
JPWSSH data suggests that in many of the focal countries, good progress is being made in providing 
the population with access to an improved water source for drinking water, although there is clearly 
scope for improving the level of service by making drinking water available on premises and improving 
water quality. However, there is an urgent need to tackle the much lower levels of access to improved 
sanitation services and to ending the practice of open defecation. 
Whilst improving physical access to WASH services is essential to benefit women and girls, maximising 
the benefits of this also requires financial, attitudinal and safety barriers to be addressed. This calls 
for infrastructure development initiatives to complement other initiatives addressing these barriers. 
Examples of complementary initiatives which could be considered include: 
▪ 
Building family and community understanding of the health benefits of good WASH 
practices: this appreciation is critical if families, especially men, are to accept the need to 
use scarce financial resources to cover costs associated with WASH services. Such 
interventions could also usefully cover the importance of good hygiene practices in an 
effort to erode the taboos often associated with menstruation. As men often control 
financial decision-making in households, involving men in these conversations and 
garnering their support is critical. 
▪ 
Cross-subsidisation approaches to make sustainable WASH facilities accessible to poorer 
sections of the community: in such scenarios, wealthier sections of the community would 
pay higher rates so that user rates for the poorest households could be reduced or 
eliminated. 
▪ 
Adapting WASH facilities so they are physically accessible to women and girls with 
disabilities. 
It should be noted that working with households and communities on good hygiene practices, 
including during menstruation, can help increase demand for WASH facilities and therefore the return 
on investment. 
8.2 Opportunity Area 2  
Giving women voice in the planning, design and management of facilities and initiatives 
If WASH facilities, wetland, coastal and marine ecosystem protection and management initiatives, and 
climate-resilient port infrastructure are to meet the needs of women and girls, then their planning, 
design and management need to be done with an awareness of those needs and a sensitivity to them. 
As we have seen in the WASH policy analysis, most countries foresee community participation in the 
sector. There are, however, strong social norms which devalue and often silence women’s voices, even 
when formal public consultation spaces are available.    
A different approach to public consultation and participation is needed to truly provide space for 
women and girls to inform the planning, design and management of WASH facilities, wetland, coastal 
and marine ecosystem protection and management initiatives, and climate-resilient port 
infrastructure. This approach needs to first and foremost identify women and girls as a specific 
Page 170

=== PAGE 174 ===
Page 63 of 143 
 
stakeholder group (rather than part of ‘the community’) and put in place an engagement strategy 
which counters the power dynamics which often silence women and girls to allow them to be heard 
and understood. Such an approach might involve: 
▪ 
Recognising that within the broad category of ‘women’ and ‘girls’, there are intersecting 
sub-groups with different needs e.g. indigenous women, women with disabilities, 
chronically poor women and adolescent girls, and that it is important for each of these 
sub-groups to be given the opportunity to be heard. Ways to reach out to and convene 
representatives of these sub-groups will need to be identified. 
▪ 
Identifying women’s leaders to work with to convene appropriate fora to hear a range of 
women’s and girls’ voices about their needs and challenges. Leaders within critical sub-
groups of women and girls could also be identified as a conduit for engaging the wider 
group. 
▪ 
Working with community leaders and men’s leaders to help them understand the 
rationale for engaging women in the planning of facilities and initiatives, and to build their 
support for that, thereby avoiding any backlash. 
▪ 
Holding consultation meetings at times and at venues which are convenient to the women 
and girls being targeted, considering how the direct and indirect costs of attending can be 
off-set, particularly for the chronically poor and for women and girls with disabilities. 
Taking this approach to engaging with women and girls has the potential to bring real insights into 
their needs and experiences, but these insights will only lead to gender responsive facilities and 
initiatives if those employed in designing them are sensitive to the importance of benefitting women 
and girls, and most crucially, decision-making gives due attention to such issues. Enabling this will 
require those leading the design to set the tone in this regard, clearly communicating expectations 
and taking action to ensure those expectations are met.  
If women are to influence the management of WASH facilities and oceans ecosystem protection 
initiatives involving community led committees, as is sometimes intended, strategic consideration of 
how to create the space for women’s voices to be heard will similarly be needed. The challenge is the 
same: how to counter social norms and power differentials which tend to silence women. The 
approach is likely to be context specific, but could involve:  
▪ 
Identifying and recruiting to the committee women in the community who are perceived 
by men and other women to have legitimacy in speaking out on behalf of other women, 
including women from different sub-groups.  
▪ 
Building the skills of the women representatives on the committee to consult with a 
diverse range of women and girls in the community to get their feedback on the WASH 
facilities or oceans ecosystem protection initiative and to represent these views in 
committee meetings and decision-making. 
▪ 
Building male committee members’ understanding of the need for management to be 
informed by women and girls’ experiences and developing the skills to ensure women’s 
voices inform committee decision-making. 
 
 
 
 
Page 171

=== PAGE 175 ===
Page 64 of 143 
 
8.3 Opportunity Area 3  
Providing women with formal employment opportunities 
Low levels of secondary education completion amongst girls in several of the focal countries can make 
the formal employment of women in the WASH sector challenging. There is, however, positive 
experience emerging in South and South East Asia of women being successfully employed in WASH 
enterprises e.g. as sanitation sales agents, in meter reading and in rainwater harvesting tank 
construction. 
Opportunities for promoting women’s employment in low-skill positions in the WASH sector warrant 
exploration in the design of CI2 supported projects. This strategy is not well documented in available 
literature and further investigation into available experience would be beneficial. Understanding how 
employers successfully engaged these women and how men’s concerns about women’s 
independence, mobility and safety were allayed are important aspects to explore. 
Challenges in employing women in the technical design and development of WASH facilities are 
particularly acute, with much smaller numbers of women going into tertiary education and even fewer 
focusing on STEM subjects such as engineering, although data on this is hard to find. These challenges 
will not be resolved quickly and will require medium to long-term support to encourage women to 
remain in education and to pursue careers in STEM. Possible support might involve scholarships for 
women to study STEM subjects in demand in WASH and work experience placements for women STEM 
students to gain practical experience in the workplace.  
The literature review suggests that if cleaners or caretakers are not in post to maintain WASH facilities 
in schools and health centres, this work can often fall to female staff members. Any efforts to improve 
WASH facilities in schools and health centres should therefore include a resourced plan for the on-
going cleaning and maintenance of the facilities.  
As regards the protection and management of wetlands, coastal and marine ecosystems, relevant 
livelihoods initiatives for women have tended to accommodate, but failed to address, social norms 
which perpetuate gendered inequalities within industries and value chains. Initiatives to support 
sustainable practices in oceans-based industries should therefore not only take into account such 
restrictions – on women’s time of mobility, for instance – but actively seek to tackle discriminatory 
norms which limit women’s livelihoods opportunities. 
The development of new climate-resilient ports offers opportunities to recruit women as employees.  
Attracting women recruits, and supporting their meaningful integration and progression means 
providing a combination of initiatives to build their skills and confidence, and provide a supportive 
work environment. These include training incentives, mentorship programmes to connect women to 
those in senior level roles, as well as family-friendly HR policies and safe (separate) sanitation facilities 
for women. Initiatives should include a focus on areas from which women tend to be most excluded, 
most notably port operations and management. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Page 172

=== PAGE 176 ===
Page 65 of 143 
 
8.4 Opportunity Area 4  
Understanding the gender equality benefits of gender-responsive facilities and initiatives  
Our review has suggested that there is a limited body of experience of interventions working at the 
nexus between gender, climate change and each of WASH, protection and management of wetlands, 
coastal and marine ecosystems, and port infrastructure. There is then an enormous opportunity for 
CFM to contribute to building the evidence base on how gender equality can be promoted through, 
climate-smart WASH, ocean ecosystems protection initiatives and port infrastructure, using CI2 as the 
vehicle.  
If CFM wishes to capitalise on this opportunity, it will need to operationalise robust monitoring, 
evaluation and learning frameworks for its new investments. This would involve: 
▪ 
Having clear objectives for how gender equality is to be promoted through the investment 
and the intended outcomes. 
▪ 
Specifying indicators with which to measure changes in gender equality and the 
achievement of intended outcomes. 
▪ 
A methodology, which ideally makes use of quantitative and qualitative approaches to 
measuring change, and which collects data at different time points to understand the pace 
of change and offer opportunities to adapt interventions. Of particular interest would be 
conducting an impact assessment post project completion, when communities have had 
some time to enjoy the benefits of the investment. 
▪ 
A skilled team to design and operationalise the monitoring, evaluation and learning, 
including team members with gender expertise.   
▪ 
Financial resources to cover the cost of operationalising a robust monitoring, evaluation 
and learning framework. 
Such an investment would contribute to CFM’s internal learning, but if results could be published, they 
would be a public good, offering others working in the gender, climate change, WASH, ocean 
ecosystems and port infrastructure spaces the opportunity to learn from CFM’s experience. 
It is worth noting that data captured on initiatives to protect ocean ecosystems should include relevant 
informal economies (where women tend to be concentrated) as well as specific parts of value chains 
women in which they are represented disproportionately. 
 
 
Page 173

=== PAGE 177 ===
Page 66 of 143 
 
 
Understanding the results and impact of gender mainstreaming   
Monitoring and reporting will be undertaken in accordance with the principles set out in CFM’s 
Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS). CFM will monitor and evaluate the actions 
taken to promote gender equality, their results and impact in two ways. Firstly, by monitoring and 
evaluating key impact indicators at the Fund level. Secondly, by implementing monitoring plans for 
individual projects.  
Fund Level Monitoring and Evaluation 
The KPIs established for individual programmes will supplement the key impact indicators that have 
been adopted for the CI2 funds and against which CI2 is committed to monitor performance in all 
projects. These indicators are linked to selected UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The 
impact indicators that are relevant to gender are shown in Table 5 below.  
Table 5 CI2 Impact Indicators  
UN SDG 
Gender-Relevant CI Two Impact Indicators 
SDG 5 - Gender Equality 
• Number of full-time, equivalent female employees 
• Number of females in management positions 
SDG 6 - Clean Water and Sanitation 
• Total number of people (gender disaggregated) with improved 
access to clean drinking water and sanitation 
SDG 8 – Decent Work and Economic 
Growth 
• Number of new direct jobs created; gender disaggregated  
• Catalytic effect (EUR million) (total DFI and commercial financing) 
 
Project level monitoring and evaluation 
Project companies will implement a monitoring and evaluation plan, which sets out: 
• 
The indicators to be used to monitor how women and girls, as well as other priority social 
groups e.g., indigenous women, women from chronically poor households and women with 
disabilities, are being affected - both positively and negatively - by project interventions, and 
the impact these projects are having on their lives.  
• 
The methodology for gathering and analysing monitoring data, as well as reporting and using 
the findings. 
Each monitoring and evaluation plan will be bespoke to individual projects. However, they will contain 
comparable quantitative and qualitative indicators and data collection methodologies where 
appropriate. Depending on a project’s life cycle, monitoring data will be collected where possible at 
regular intervals to understand progress during project implementation. The aim of project 
monitoring will be to provide timely information to make project adjustments and address any 
negative effects.  
Relevant components of the monitoring and evaluation plan may be incorporated into contracts for 
EPCs or other sub-contractors, with reviews of the plan’s implementation forming part of the 
contractor’s normal review process. 
There is a need for more evidence on effective approaches to promoting gender equality through 
WASH investments. In response to this, CFM may publish some of the results and learnings from its 
CI2 investments.

Annex 3: Stakeholder mapping interview guide 
 
Climate Investor 2 Gender Assessment Stakeholder Engagement 
Interview Guide 
 
Introduction 
Interviewer introduces her/himself and asks respondent to introduce her/himself. Note down: 
• 
Name 
• 
Organisation 
• 
Role 
We are working with Climate Fund Managers and the Green Climate Fund on a project proposal to 
leverage private sector investment into the development of climate resilient water and sanitation 
infrastructure. [Country X] is one of the countries where funding will be made available to leverage 
large scale private sector investment.  As part of the proposal development process, we are seeking 
to understand how future private sector investments in WASH infrastructure development can help 
promote gender equality, for example, by improving access to quality water and sanitation services, 
enabling women’s participation in the design and management of water and sanitation services and 
enabling women to benefit from the infrastructure development, including through formal and 
informal employment opportunities. We are keen to learn from the experience of experts in this 
field and would like to ask you a few questions. Your feedback will be anonymous. It will be used to 
inform the proposal to be submitted to the Green Climate Fund for financial support and the design 
of private sector investment leveraged through Climate Fund Managers. The interview will take 
approximately 45 minutes. Are you happy to proceed? 
Interview Questions 
1. In [Country X], what work is being done to promote gender equality in water and sanitation 
infrastructure development? Explore: 
a. Expansion of WASH services to reduce domestic and health burden on women and 
girls; 
b. Involvement of women in the design and management of water and sanitation 
services; 
c. Employment of women in the development of water and sanitation infrastructure 
and services? 
 
2. Which organisations are involved in this work, and what are their roles?  
 
3. From your perspective, which organisations are most involved in promoting gender equality 
in water and sanitation infrastructure and services? What do they do to promote gender 
equality in water and sanitation infrastructure and service development? At what level(s) do 
they operate (national, sub-national, community)?  
 
4. The assessment we are in the process of conducting identified four opportunities for 
promoting gender equality in water and sanitation infrastructure and services, which we 
shared with you in advance of this interview. We would like to discuss two of them in more 
detail with you.  
 
Page 185

=== PAGE 189 ===
Page 78 of 143 
 
a. The first opportunity we’d like to discuss with you is giving women voice in the 
planning, design and management of WASH facilities. How do you think the project 
companies leading the infrastructure design and development can do this? Who do 
they need to work with and what do they need to do to ensure women have a voice 
in the planning, design and management of WASH facilities? 
b. The second opportunity we’d like to discuss with you is providing women with 
formal employment opportunities in the WASH sector. What do you think private 
sector investors and project management companies can do to provide women with 
formal employment opportunities in the WASH sector?  
 
5. Before we close, are there any recommendations you would like to give us to consider as we 
develop the proposal for private sector investment in WASH infrastructure?  
 
Thank the respondent for their time and helpful feedback.
