Annex 5: Full Country Analyses 
9.1 
Bangladesh 
 
Water, sanitation and hygiene in Bangladesh at a glance 
 
Access to Drinking Water: 98.9% of the population has access to an improved source for drinking 
water, of which 58.5% has access to safely managed drinking water and 39.2% has access to basic 
drinking water.143 Women are the main collectors of water144 with the majority of women (76.6%) 
spending about 30 minutes to collect water.145 
 
Access to Sanitation: 78% of the population has access to an improved facility, of which 38.7% has 
access to safely managed sanitation services, 15.5% has access to basic sanitation services and 
23.8% has access to limited sanitation services. None of the population is reported to practice open 
defecation. The remaining proportion of the population uses unimproved sanitation services.146  
 
Hygiene: 58.4% of the population has access to handwashing facilities with soap and water.147 
 
WASH in schools: Nationally, 82.2% of schools have access to basic service for drinking water, 56.3% 
of schools have access to basic service for sanitation and 50.7% of schools have access to basic 
service for hygiene.148  
 
WASH in health facilities: 64.4% of health care facilities have basic service for drinking water, 31.3% 
have basic service for sanitation and 37.6% have basic service for hygiene.149 
 
Policy context 
Improving water, sanitation and hygiene is a key part of Bangladesh’s national policy framework, as 
identified in the National Sustainable Development Strategy (2010). Recognising the increasing 
impacts of climate change, rapid urbanisation and water contamination, including salinity intrusion in 
fresh water and severe reduction on available groundwater, the National Sustainable Development 
Strategy addresses water, sanitation and hygiene under strategic priorities 7 on environment, natural 
resources and disaster management, and strategic priority 5 on the urban environment. To address 
the challenges faced, the policy aims to: 
• 
Diversify water supply, with a shift to surface water and encourage rainwater harvesting; 
• 
Encourage water recycling.  
• 
Expand the use of technology to improve the efficient use of scare resources, including water. 
• 
Enhance investment in safe drinking water.  
 
143 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2020) 
144 MICS (2019) 
145 MICS (2019) 
146 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2020) 
147 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2020) 
148 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2019) 
149 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2019) 
Page 188

=== PAGE 192 ===
Page 81 of 143 
 
Two other policies, the National Strategy for Water Supply and Sanitation (NSWSS) of 2014 and the 
National Hygiene Promotion Strategy for Water Supply and Sanitation Sector (NHPSWSS) of 2012, 
flesh out further policy intentions in the sector. The NSWSS identifies challenges around climate 
change adaption, rapid urbanisation, need for improved water resource and sanitation (both solid 
waste and faecal sludge) management and continued advancement up the sanitation ladder. It lays 
out 17 strategic objectives to combat these challenges, including ensuring safe drinking water, 
undertaking specific approaches for vulnerable people, gender mainstreaming, improving hygiene 
promotion, adapting to climate change and undertaking integrated and accountable development 
approaches.  
 
The NHPSWSS seeks to promote the sustainable use of improved water supply and sanitation 
infrastructures, ensuring the creation of an environment for hygiene promotion and practice to reduce 
water and sanitation related diseases. It recognises challenges associated with increases in vulnerable 
water sources from weather extremities, including droughts and saline intrusion; continued open 
defecation in wetlands leading to a rise in water-borne illnesses; and continued, inequitable access to 
safe water and sanitation facilities, especially for women and people with disabilities. The policy 
aspires to overcome these challenges through 10 strategic objectives, including: exploring and 
replicating successful hygiene promotion models, gender mainstreaming, ensuring the potential use 
of the social organisations, ensuring hygiene promotion for hard-to-reach areas.  
 
Strategy 10 seeks to include gender mainstreaming in all the service planning, implementation and 
monitoring by shifting organisational culture and ways of thinking and by developing procedures 
relating to gender responsibility, accountability, coordination and personnel policy and the 
consideration of women’s role in hygiene promotion, both as a token role (such as gender advisor or 
officer) and also as an empowered advocate.   
 
Improving hygiene awareness in schools is addressed in the National Education policy. This includes 
hand washing and installing modernized toilets for both girls and boys to decrease the spread of 
water-borne and vector-borne diseases.  
 
Bangladesh’s location makes it highly susceptible to the adverse effects of climate change, hindering 
its development, including access to safe and economic opportunities for women and other vulnerable 
groups. The Bangladesh Climate Change and Action Plan (BCCSAP) of 2008 looks further into the 
negative impacts caused by increased weather extremities, with climate-related impacts leading to a 
decrease in water availability and an increase in water contamination from rapid urbanisation, saline 
intrusion, poor sewerage management and riverbank erosion. It also looks at the impact this has on 
women and children, especially since women are responsible for water management in their families, 
bear the burden of collecting water from further distances due to degradation of water and are 
vulnerable to health risks associated with drinking saline water. It seeks to tackle these challenges 
through action under 6 pillars:  
 
1. Food security, social protection and health;  
2. Comprehensive disaster management;  
3. Infrastructure;  
4. Research and knowledge management;  
5. Mitigation and low-carbon development;  
6. Capacity building and institutional strengthening.  
Page 189

=== PAGE 193 ===
Page 82 of 143 
 
Pillar 1 places an importance on women and girls by increasing community involvement in planning 
and increasing resilience of vulnerable groups, including women and children, through development 
of community-level adaptation, livelihood diversification and better access to basic services and social 
protection. Programme T1P9 also seeks to complete a comprehensive study on the impact of climate 
change on women and gender relations and to develop recommendations to address these in all 
actions under the BCCSAP.  
 
None of the policies reviewed explicitly considered advancing women’s formal roles in the WASH 
sector, but many discussed the need for community involvement in the planning, implementation, 
operation and management of water and sanitation facilities. Alongside this, some policies also 
consider the advancement of women’s involvement in entrepreneurial activities, with a focus on the 
non-farming sector in rural areas; training and use of technologies suitable for women of various 
socioeconomic status; creating initiatives for entrepreneurs for proper waste treatment before 
industrial waste disposal; and gender mainstreaming in all aspects of water, sanitation and hygiene 
supply chain, including WASH related committees and employment. Within the National Women 
Development Policy, there is a broader emphasis, within all sectors, on career development 
opportunities for women employees and equal participation of women in decision-making and 
governance at all levels. Additionally, due to only 21.1% of women completing upper secondary 
schooling150 and 19.8% enrolment in tertiary education,151 women are limited in the careers prospects 
within the WASH sector, and a focus on policies is placed on opportunities in low-skilled jobs.  
Stakeholder Mapping 
In Bangladesh, the Policy Support branch of the Ministry of Local Government, Rural Development 
and Cooperatives has primary responsibility for Water and Sanitation Supply and Strategy. Other 
national Ministries with some WASH responsibility include the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, 
for hygiene, and Ministry of Water Resources (MoWR), covering irrigation, flooding, and climate-
related water protection. There are a range of agencies involved in the delivery of WASH services at 
the sub-national levels, including government agencies at the city, district, upazila, union, and ward 
level, as well as the private sector, national and international CSOs, and community-based 
committees. A wide range of development partners are involved in supporting government, private 
sector and CSO engagement in WASH. Figure A provides a visual mapping of the actors involved.

9.2 Botswana 
Water, sanitation and hygiene in Botswana at a glance 
 
Access to drinking water: 92.2% of the population has access to a basic level of drinking water and 
4.7% of the population has access to a limited level of drinking water.  1.7% of the population 
relies on unimproved drinking water whilst 1.3% of the population has access only to surface 
drinking water. There is no data available for the proportion of the population with access to 
safely managed drinking water.152 
 
Access to sanitation: 80% of the population has access to basic sanitation services and 5.6% of the 
population has access to a limited level of sanitation services. 4.6% of the population relies on 
unimproved sanitation services whilst 9.8% of the population practices open defecation in 
sanitation services. There is no data available on the proportion of the population with access to 
safely managed sanitation services.153 
 
Data on hygiene, WASH in schools and WASH in health facilities is not available. 
 
 
Policy context 
Botswana’s 11th National Development Plan covers the period April 2017 – March 2023. It was 
adopted in December 2016 and followed the country’s celebration of the fifty years anniversary of 
Independence and the launching of Botswana’s Vision 2036. The Vision 2036 provided an opportunity 
for the country to define its roadmap for the next twenty years. With an all-encompassing theme of 
“Achieving Prosperity for All”, the Vision provides an inclusive development path to be achieved 
through the expansion of the domestic economy while empowering Botswanans to meaningfully 
participate in the development of their country. Achieving the goals and objectives of Vision 2036 will 
require a refocus of Botswana’s current development model towards an export-oriented, labour-
intensive, and private sector driven model. 
 
Chapter two of the 11th National Development Plan looks at Botswana’s population situation and 
development prospects, with sub-sections on the key development challenges facing Botswana’s 
population. This includes a subsection on population and water resources and a subsection on 
migration, population distribution and urbanisation. In these subsections, some of Botswana’s key 
WASH sector challenges are outlined. These challenges include water shortages and droughts in the 
water sector, and the inadequate capacity of sanitation infrastructure, and pressure on sanitation 
services because of increasing levels of urbanisation.  
 
The plan outlines the negative health consequences of poor sanitation and waste management issues 
such as pollution, littering and illegal dumping. It also outlines the negative health consequences of 
water shortages and droughts, which cause food insecurity leading to malnutrition. The plan states 
 
152 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene (2020) 
 
153 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene (2020) 
 
Page 192

=== PAGE 196 ===
Page 85 of 143 
 
that the Government will continue with measures to address environmental health risks associated 
with poor sanitation, hygiene and waste management, and pollution throughout the value chain from 
the source of environmental contaminants to the affected populations. To combat water scarcity and 
its impact on the agricultural sector leading to food insecurity and malnutrition, the plan states that 
considerations will be made to adopt ‘smart agriculture’. This approach will help Botswana to 
transform its agricultural systems towards more productive, efficient, resilient and sustainable 
systems.  
 
The plan largely ignores specific gender considerations in the water and sanitation sectors. The plan 
does however state that the Government recognises the need for equal participation and 
representation of women and men in all development processes, and that NDP 11 provides an 
opportunity for all sectors to articulate gender issues that they will address in the execution of their 
respective mandates.  
 
Although the policy largely ignores gender issues related to employment in the water and sanitation 
sectors, NDP11 does discuss the low participation of females in Science, Technology, Engineering and 
Mathematics (STEM) subjects as a key challenge facing the education sector, despite women having 
an upper secondary completion rate of 66.3% (compared to 62.6% for men) (UNIS), and a tertiary 
enrolment rate of 31.95% (compared to 26.09% for men) (UNIS). The policy fails to articulate how this 
challenge impacts women’s employment in the water and sanitation sectors.  
 
While the National Development Plan discusses the water and sanitation sectors, it does so in general 
terms within the broader context of Botswana’s development as a whole. Botswana’s National Water 
Policy however discusses the sectors in specific detail and provides a national framework that will 
facilitate access to water of suitable quality and standards for the citizenry and provide the 
foundations for sustainable development of water resources in support of economic growth, 
diversification and poverty eradication. The policy therefore aims to provide a strategic framework for 
the following goals:  
• 
Protection, conservation and restoration of the nation’s water resources  
• 
Promotion of effective, sustainable management of water resources  
• 
Promotion of the equitable and efficient use of water resources  
• 
Reduction of the subsidies associated with water supply  
• 
Assurance of access and affordability of water for all  
• 
Protection and restoration of the environment  
• 
Promotion of productive uses of water 
Like the National Development Plan, the National Water Policy (2012) identifies water shortages and 
droughts and water supply for agriculture and industry as key challenges facing the sector. The policy 
also raises water use efficiency and the protection of water resources as important issues. The policy 
offers policy statements and objectives in a variety of thematic areas across the sector including Water 
for Growth, Water Demand Management and Conservation, Domestic Water Supply and Sanitation, 
Water for Environment and Tourism, Water for Agriculture, Water for Mining and Industry, Water for 
Energy, Information Management and Dissemination, Research and Development, International 
Cooperation and Monitoring & Evaluation.  
 
However, also like the National Development Plan, women’s roles in the water and sanitation sectors 
are largely ignored. The policy does state that gender and social equity in accessing water resources 
will be ensured and, in particular, women shall be empowered to participate fully in issues and 
Page 193

=== PAGE 197 ===
Page 86 of 143 
 
decisions relating to sustainable development and management of water resources but does not go 
into specifics on how this will be achieved.  
 
As the National Water Policy offers a strategic policy framework for Botswana’s water and sanitation 
sectors, the National Health Policy (2011) offers the same for the health sector. The National Health 
Policy offers valuable insight into the consequences of the challenges facing the water and sanitation 
sectors on public health. The National Health Policy discusses the impact of water shortages and their 
potential for causing malnutrition, and also identifies the negative public health consequences of a 
lack of access to adequate sanitation facilities and access to safe drinking water.  
 
The National Health Policy also discusses the interplay between climate change, the water sector and 
public health. The policy discusses water scarcity and fluctuating rainfall patterns caused by climate 
change, asserting that the prevalence of malaria is expected to increase as temperatures rise and 
conditions are more conducive for mosquitoes to breed during rainy seasons. Cholera is also likely to 
increase during the same period. With a reduction in rainfall, food production is likely to decrease and 
may lead to malnutrition in the longer term. The policy does not however discuss the gender dynamics 
of these issues.  
 
Botswana’s draft Climate Change Response Policy (2016) and the National Climate Change Strategy 
for Botswana (2018) continue to look at the effects of climate change on the water and sanitation 
sectors. The draft Climate Change Response Policy is a statement of intent and vision of what 
Botswana aims to achieve on climate change, and is complemented by the National Climate Change 
Strategy, which aims to help operationalise the tenets of the draft policy and provide necessary 
guidance on how the policy objectives will be achieved.  
 
The draft Climate Change Response Policy discusses several issues related to the water and sanitation 
sectors, including water availability, exacerbated water stress leading to food insecurity and water and 
sanitation management strategies. Like the National Health Policy, the draft Climate Change Response 
Policy also discusses how climate change is likely to negatively affect human health directly through 
increased temperatures, drought and floods, and also through the spread of water borne, water-
related and vector borne diseases.  
 
The policy recognises the need to mainstream gender considerations into development planning to 
ensure that climate change response measures are gender sensitive. It states that this shall be 
achieved through empowering women and youth to actively participate in the implementation of 
climate response measures, adopting strategies that are targeted at increasing women and youth’s 
resilience to climate change, and including gender and climate change into academic curriculum at all 
levels.  
 
The National Climate Change Strategy for Botswana helps to operationalise the draft Climate Change 
Response Policy’s discussion of the water and sanitation sectors through the below policy goals to be 
realised for the sectors:  
• 
Utilisation of shared water courses for the benefit of Botswana 
• 
Integrating climate change response measures in the water planning processes across all 
economic sectors 
• 
Consideration of defining potential water aquifers and adopting appropriate measures of 
protection for water security and sustainability 
Page 194

=== PAGE 198 ===
Page 87 of 143 
 
• 
Promotion of rainwater harvesting, water re-use, and recycling for domestic, agricultural, 
industrial, and commercial purposes 
• 
Promotion of integrated watering systems for livestock, particularly in rural areas 
• 
Employing accounting and valuation tools to support water management decision systems 
These specific policy goals for the water and sanitation sectors feed into the broader policy objectives 
of the strategy, which are:  
• 
To ensure climate change adaptation and mitigation action supports the achievement of 
Botswana’s Vision 2030 of prosperity for all. 
• 
To ensure climate change adaptation and mitigation action supports the achievement of 
current and future Botswana’s National Development Plans (NDPs). 
• 
To ensure climate change adaptation and mitigation action supports the achievement of the 
global Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) in Botswana. 
• 
To ensure climate change adaptation and mitigation action supports the achievement of 
Botswana’s NDC, including the GHG emissions reduction target of 15% by 2030 
• 
To ensure Botswana has a clear strategic agenda on climate change adaptation and mitigation 
actions, allowing for a spectrum of actors to be involved in implementation. 
The National Climate Change Strategy devotes special attention to gender equality and ensuring that 
it serves the needs of women. There is a much more thorough review of gender as a cross-cutting 
issue than in the draft Climate Change Response Policy, and actionable Gender Based Strategies with 
strategic interventions including:  
• 
Develop a national Climate Change Gender Action Plan (CC GAP), with an emphasis on women 
as drivers of climate resilience, and women’s role in water and energy and healthcare 
provision in households. 
• 
Promote equitable participation of women farmers and female-headed households in Climate 
Smart Agriculture (CSA) programmes, agritourism, and access to conservation agriculture 
technologies. 
• 
Ensure equitable gender access to the proposed endowment fund providing low-cost finance 
to climate change adaptation projects, aligned to existing similar projects. 
• 
Ensure that women’s voices are included in natural resources management through their 
equitable participation in CBNRM processes. 
• 
Ensure that gendered differences of climate change are mainstreamed into climate change 
education. 
• 
Ensure the full participation of women and female-headed households in disaster 
management public gatherings, to address both the higher vulnerability of women and 
children, and to plan for the higher post-disaster burden placed on women due to their dual 
roles as producers and carers. 
9.3 Brazil 
 
Water, sanitation and hygiene in Ivory Coast at a glance 
Access to drinking water: 99.3% of the population has access to an improved water source for 
drinking water, of which 85.8% has access to safely managed drinking water, 13.5% has access to 
Page 195

=== PAGE 199 ===
Page 88 of 143 
 
a basic level of drinking water, and 0.1% has access to a limited level of drinking water. The 
remainder of the population uses unimproved sources for drinking water1.  
Access to sanitation: 90.1% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 
48.7% has access to safely managed sanitation service, 41.4% has access to a basic level of 
sanitation service, whilst 0.1% has access to a limited level of sanitation service. 9.8% of the 
population uses unimproved sanitation services, including 0.3% who practice open defecation2.   
Data on hygiene is not available.   
WASH in health facilities: Nationally, 80.2% had a basic service in drinking water, data on access to 
hygiene and sanitation is insufficient3.   
WASH in schools: Nationally, 61% has a basic service in hygiene, data on access to drinking water 
and sanitation is insufficient4.  
 
Policy context 
The National Policy for Water Resources (2022) and the National Policy for Basic Sanitation (2019) 
both discuss in-depth the importance of WASH in daily life and lay out clear objectives to ensure the 
impact on human livelihoods and activities, such as business, is limited. The National Policy for Water 
Resources reviews the lack of subterrain water resource development, and waters use in agriculture, 
tourism, industry, and domestic use. It examines the negative impacts that water scarcity creates in 
vulnerable communities, including increase in conflict, siltation of water, water pollution, and changes 
in natural characteristics from high degradation. It adds the impact of climate change leaders to 
further alterations to available water resources and impacts both the energy and agricultural sector. 
It seeks to increase environmental education and capacity building in technology and science for water 
resource management, establish a formal and sustainable water management system, utilize 
knowledge and experience of indigenous people and communities, promote post-graduate courses in 
water resources and environmentalism, support the increase in knowledge of ICT in climate change, 
and encourage the involvement of the population in identifying problems and finding solutions. It also 
seeks to incorporate gender equality in all actions and develop ongoing training that promotes female 
leadership and empowerment in the management of water resources but does not provide any detail 
on practical implications of this intention.   
The National Policy of Basic Sanitation identifies issues with parts of the population continuing to lack 
access to basic sanitation facilities and/or proper disposal of solid waste as well as a burden on the 
current resources that are available, to not keep up the demand in solid waste disposal. Due to this 
there continues to be increases in health risks and the proliferation of parasites and infectious diseases 
caused by poor or inadequate sanitation. The policy lays out clear objectives and programmes to 
overcome these issues, such as increasing infrastructure, management, and monitoring of sanitation 
facilities, improve infrastructure to capture and store water resources, and create access to water in 
non-conventional ways such as the use of cisterns. It also sees a value in the utilization of the circular 
economy for solid waste disposal. It does not explicitly mention gender in the policy objectives.    
The National Environmental Policy (1981, with several amendments, with the most recent being 
issued in 2013) and the National Policy for Climate Change (2010) elaborates on the importance of 
mitigating and adapting policies, programmes, and actions to prevent the impact on climate change 
for humans and economic activity but remains gender blind in both the challenges and objectives of 
the policy. The National Environmental Policy and its associated updates consider the degradation of 
sanitary conditions and the adverse effects that climate change has on the biota. It seeks to overcome 
Page 196

=== PAGE 200 ===
Page 89 of 143 
 
these challenges through rationing the use of water, expanding the use of environmental licensing for 
activities that utilise environmental resources, increase education on climate change and the 
environment, and incentivise studies and research related to technologies to use for the rationing and 
protection of the environment. The National Policy on Climate Change discusses the impacts extreme 
weather has on the health and livelihoods of people, which includes the effect it has on water reserves 
in the country. It reviews the idea of reusing water in urban planning and calls for action to protect 
water resources in the National Policy for Water Resources and National Policy for Basic Sanitation. It 
places civil responsibility on reducing the impact to climate change, as a key manner to achieve climate 
change actions, such as the reduction of green house gases (GHG).    
The National Plan of Politics for Women (2013) explores the importance of including a gender lens in 
the sustainable use and handling of water, promotion of public policies to defend water as a public 
good, ensure access to water by building cisterns, ensuring agricultural security, and utilization of 
environmental technologies. It emphasises the importance of valuing women’s contribution to farm 
work and equal participation in leadership and decision making. Finally, the Plan seeks to amplify 
access for women in the labour market, including within the agricultural sector and formal education 
in science and technology.   
Though neither the National Education Plan (2014) nor the National Policy for Health (2017) discuss 
WASH specific issues or objectives, they both outline important factors related to gender equality and 
access to basic services. In Brazil 67.32% of the population has completed Upper Secondary school, of 
which 72.73% are female and 62.18% are male.5 While only 55.14% of the population is enrolled in 
tertiary education, a larger proportion are female, 64.35% compared to 46.26% males.6 The National 
Education Plan seeks to reduce discrimination and inequality, of all forms while also promoting socio-
environmentalism in the education system. The National Policy for Health seeks to create equal access 
to heathcare and teach on the intersection of health, sustainable development, and the 
environment.   
Several of the policies reviewed touch on employment opportunities e.g., the National Sanitation 
Policy promotion of employment within the sector, and the National Water Resource Policy promoting 
education in the ICT sector, but none of them explicitly consider employment opportunities for 
women in WASH related sectors. Brazil has a high percentage of entrepreneurship activity, with about 
21% of the population in early stages of entrepreneurial activity, and 10% in established businesses7, 
and 8 to 10 ratio of female to male entrepreneurs8 in the country. The National Plan of Politics for 
Women (2013) wishes to promote economic and financial autonomy of women seeking 
entrepreneurial activities.  
As a federal state, Brazil’s lower tiers of government play an important role in the provision of 
sustainable WASH services, and most federal policies are created with broad objectives leaving each 
regional government responsible to expand on the objectives based on their needs.  The first National 
Policy for Regional Development (2007) placed greater importance on climate resilience measures and 
gender equality, the current National Policy for Regional Development II (2019) emphasises achieving 
equality, within regions and between regions, strengthening science, technology, and innovation 
sectors, and climate action. The policy outlines 8 main principles to focus on in regional development, 
including sustainable development and the valorization of the environment. It also seeks to ensure 
equal access to economic opportunities within all the regions, both from a socio-economic and gender 
perspective. However, there is no specific discussion of WASH-related challenges and objectives in the 
policy, nor are there are gender specific strategies within outlined climate action measures.   
9.4 Djibouti 
Water, sanitation and hygiene in Djibouti at a glance 
Page 197

=== PAGE 201 ===
Page 90 of 143 
 
 
Access to drinking water: 90.8% of the population has access to an improved source of drinking 
water, although for the majority (76%), this is basic access.154 None of the population has access to 
drinking water classified as safely managed. The remaining 9.2% of the population has access to 
either unimproved drinking water or surface water only.155 Women are the main collectors of 
water.156 88.6% of the population has improved water available on the premises, while for 3%, it 
takes 30 minutes or less to get to an improved drinking source.157 
 
Access to sanitation: 73.8% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 
37.1% has access to safely managed sanitation services, 29.6% has access to basic sanitation 
services and 7.1% has access to limited sanitation services. 10% of the population uses unimproved 
sanitation services whilst 16.2% practices open defecation.158 
 
Data on hygiene, WASH in schools and WASH in health facilities is not available.  
 
Policy context 
After several decades of implementing various developmental policies that focused on the short and 
medium term, the Djibouti 2035 Vision (2010–2035) presents a long-term developmental vision for 
sustained growth. The vision has a strong focus on economic growth and private sector development, 
and water, sanitation and hygiene, as well as other social development factors, remain a secondary 
goal to support the achievement of this vision. Water, sanitation and hygiene remain a strong area of 
focus within the 2035 vision, specifically given the climate challenges Djibouti is starting to face. The 
vision sets out an overall strategy within which various ministries are tasked with setting up a more 
detailed action. The vision focuses on the following policy objectives for water, sanitation and hygiene: 
• 
Providing good sanitation for the urban environments to have clean cities. 
• 
Identifying more water sources and improving access to existing water sources.             
• 
Modernization of infrastructure (sanitation, water access and port development) and 
increased quality of education.   
• 
Simplifying the irrigation systems to ration water use.            
The vision focuses primarily on water supply throughout the country and its implications for other 
areas of the primary sector, with a lesser focus on sanitation and hygiene. This is complemented by 
the climate section of the vision, which includes sustainable water management as an area of priority, 
amongst other strategy objectives unrelated to WASH. Furthermore, Vision 2035 identifies the link 
between climate change and WASH, specifically in terms of how they will experience increased 
instances of droughts and flooding which will impact food production and supplies throughout the 
country.  
The 2010 Development Plan for the Primary Sector and the 2015 Strategy of Accelerated Growth and 
Promotion of Employment echo the focus on access to water for agriculture and livestock, as well as 
 
154 A basic level of access to drinking water means that the water is from an improved source and that the time for collection is not more 
than 30 mins for a roundtrip including queuing, Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene . 
155 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2020) 
156 MICS (2006) 
157 MICS (2006) 
158 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2020) 
Page 198

=== PAGE 202 ===
Page 91 of 143 
 
the hygiene risks that a lack of sanitation infrastructure poses in cities. The main consequences 
identified in these policies are the spread of disease and further contamination of water sources and 
structural water distress for the population of Djibouti. Although the latter two policies touch on 
gender and the need to address gender inequity throughout the primary sector and for growth, they 
do not directly link WASH issues to gender. The problems identified, such as health consequences and 
hard to reach water points, are known to generally affect women more, but this is not specified in the 
policies. They do acknowledge that various stakeholders, including women and youth, should have a 
larger role in the policy-making process.  
Beyond water and food shortages, Vision 2035 also identifies sanitation and hygiene issues related to 
water insecurity. Liquid and solid waste management is identified as one of the main WASH 
challenges, identifying that few households in Djibouti are connected through a collective waste 
management system. This contributes to the deteriorating health of the population. Although not 
directly linked, the strategy also identifies the disproportionate effects of poverty on women, including 
the provision of health care as evidenced by the growing impacts of the AIDS epidemic on women and 
children. Health consequences of open sewage and poor water management prove more of an 
obstacle for women, while water provision to health infrastructure (e.g., hospitals) also presents a 
challenge as women are the main users of primary health centres. The vision proposes to provide good 
sanitation facilities and modernise water and sanitation infrastructure, with no real specifics on how 
they will achieve this. They also emphasise the importance of identifying more water sources and 
increasing access to these, also by simplifying irrigation systems to ration water use. Lastly, they 
mention that, alongside infrastructure development, comes increased quality of education.  
Education is a strong focus of Djibouti’s 2011–2021 Gender Policy, the policy that most identifies how 
water and sanitation challenges are an extra burden on women. The policy covers accessibility to 
water and sanitation facilities in their communities, remoteness of water points and sanitation deficits 
in their immediate environments. It identifies the woman’s role as mainly within the domestic sphere, 
meaning they carry the burden of walking to water points or suffer the consequences most severely 
when there are shortages within the household. Moreover, women disproportionately suffer the 
effects of desertification, caused by changes in the climate, in accessing water. The gender policy 
primarily advocates for education on hygiene practices, such as washing hands with soap, ways to 
preserve quality of drinking water at home and proper use of latrines in schools and in communities. 
The Gender Policy also most clearly outlines the informal role that women play in the household, and 
consequently, the informal role they might be able to play in reinforcing new sanitation practices in 
their household. This informal role can be supported through capacity building programmes for 
women and by relieving the time-poverty than women experience due to the burden of other 
housework. 
The section on gender in Vision 2035 also acknowledges women’s role in enforcing new behaviours 
and practices; however, it emphasises how ’in order to improve women's situations, men should be 
included in the project. It is important to work with men to push them into changing their approaches 
towards women and their approach with themselves’ (Djibouti 2035 Vision, 2010, p. 98). Both policies 
further outline informal and formal roles to promote change, which can be seen as opportunities 
moving forward. Local authorities are mentioned as needing to play a decisive role in the planning, 
programming and budgeting of development actions to ensure gender mainstreaming. Tribal chiefs 
and traditional systems play an important role in community influence. Both policies discuss the 
inclusion of women in budgeting processes as an important starting point to establish their role in 
Page 199

=== PAGE 203 ===
Page 92 of 143 
 
managing resources, both within households as well as at higher community or government levels. 
Lastly, the political, economic and social introduction of youth and teenagers to equality and equity 
concerns is highlighted in order to allow them to contribute to the country’s development. 
Currently, the completion rate for upper secondary is higher for women than for men; this presents a 
great opportunity to incorporate women into the workforce due to the higher levels of education they 
can take into the workforce. The 2018—2022 National Strategy for Social Protection identifies a role 
for women in the country’s goal to increase primary sector employment, specifically in fishing and 
agriculture. The social protection strategy does not further specify jobs for women, but states that it 
aims to extend protection to more workers by (i) helping to strengthen the role of the private sector 
in creating jobs and (ii) seeking to integrate the maximum of informal sector assets into the formal 
sector, a women-dominated sector. Moreover, they suggest that the issue of water and food scarcity 
can be best managed in the short-term by immediate cash transfers to households. Overall, as with 
all other policies, they identify that the awareness of all the institutional, private and associative 
actors, the seriousness of the situation and how their contribution is most important. Djibouti’s 
2020—2024 Sanitation policy further echoes this and emphasizes that rural households should be 
taken care of specifically as their access issues are especially severe. The Sanitation policy does not 
further identify any issues or opportunities specific to women or men, making the policy gender blind.   
Stakeholder Mapping 
In Djibouti, the Ministry of Agriculture, Water, Fisheries, Livestock and Fisheries Resources and the 
National Office of Water and Sanitation have primary responsibility for WASH. Other national 
Ministries with some WASH responsibility include the Ministry of Health, the leading ministry in 
implementing sanitation and hygiene programmes at national level, and the Ministry of Housing, 
Urbanism, Environment and Territorial Development, who monitor WASH service delivery. There are 
a range of agencies involved in the delivery of WASH services at the sub-national levels, including 
regional water directorates, the private sector, national and international CSOs. A wide range of 
development partners are involved in supporting government, and international and national CSO 
engagement in WASH. Figure B provides a visual mapping of the actors involved.

9.5 Indonesia 
 
Water, sanitation and hygiene in Ivory Coast at a glance 
 
Access to drinking water: 93.3% of the population has access to an improved water source for 
drinking water, of which 92.4% has access to a basic level of drinking water, and 0.9% has access to 
a limited level of drinking water. The remainder of the population uses unimproved sources for 
drinking water159. 
 
Access to sanitation: 92.6% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 
86.5% has access to a basic level of sanitation service, whilst 6.1% has access to a limited level of 
sanitation service. 7.5% of the population uses unimproved sanitation services, including 6.1% who 
practice open defecation160.  
 
Access to hygiene: 94.1% of the population has access to basic hygiene while 5.9% has limited 
access161.  
WASH in health facilities: Nationally, 80.2% has a basic service in drinking water, data on access to 
hygiene and sanitation is insufficient162.  
 
WASH in schools: Nationally, 72.7%% has a basic service in drinking water, 40.4% has a basic service 
in sanitation, and 58.9% has basic access to hygiene.163 
 
 
As a country with over 200 million spread out over 6,000 islands, and a major reliance on raw water 
supply to provide for their water resource, Indonesia has placed natural resource promotion and 
recovery as a priority in its Long-Term National Development Plan (2005). With the growing effects of 
climate change impacting the water cycle, rapid urbanization, and contamination of water supply it 
sees water supply and increasing sanitation facilities as a key component to ensuring self-reliance and 
development for all people. Whilst it recognises the need for gender mainstreaming in policies to 
reach the country’s long-term goals, the specific gender dimensions of WASH are not explored.   
 
National Medium-Term Development Plans serve to identify policy priorities in a given period, within 
the overarching framework of the Long-Term National Development Plan.  The most recent National 
Medium-Term Development Plan (2020) elaborates further the importance of maintaining natural 
resources, improving sanitation, and mitigating the impacts of climate change to economic growth. 
The plan lays out 5 directives: human resource development, infrastructure development, regulatory 
simplification, bureaucratic simplification, and economic transformation. The Plan foresees the use of 
the 3R approach - Reduce, Reuse, Recycle - and the circular economy to increase effective waste 
disposal. It aims for 90% of the population to have access to safe and proper sanitation, including 
wastewater management. As for water resources, it seeks to improve infrastructure, including dam 
capacity and forest restoration, to meet the goal of 100% of the population having access to safe 
drinking water. The policy also sees community involvement as a main way to ensure implementation 
 
159 JMP 2020 
 
160 JMP 2020 
161 JMP 2020 
162 JMP 2019 
163 JMP 2019 
Page 202

=== PAGE 206 ===
Page 95 of 143 
 
and management of safe and adequate water and sanitation facilities and practices. This includes 
increasing women’s role in development and decision-making, especially within health, education, 
labour market, economy, and public sectors.  
 
While the Long-Term National Development Plan establishes the importance of improving water and 
sanitation access and infrastructure, the Long-Term Strategy for Low Carbon and Climate Resilience 
2050 (2021) explores further the impacts that climate change has on the country’s development, 
especially when it comes to WASH-related sectors. It notes that more extreme weather patterns are 
causing increased flooding and longer dry seasons, resulting in reduced availability of raw water 
supply, something which is exacerbated by rapid urbanisation and mass deforestation to increase 
economic activity. The Strategy identifies the limited access to channelled wastewater sewage for 
treatment of grey water as a high risk. It sees climate-related challenges as something that 
disproportionally impacts women due to limited access to water supplies for domestic and economic 
use, limited access to safe sanitation facilities, and an imbalance in capacity for understanding the 
importance of safe water and sanitation practices.  It outlines objectives for both mitigation and 
adaptation to overcome these growing challenges while ensuring an equal balance to continue 
economic growth, including improving infrastructure and increasing community knowledge and 
capacity on water and sanitation management. 
 
Along-side the Long-Term Strategy for Low Carbon and Climate Resilience, the National Strategy for 
Community-Based Total Sanitation (2008) touches on on-going sanitation challenges in Indonesia, 
with socio-cultural issues, continued health concerns due to poor handwashing and water 
contamination, and lack of progress by local governments being the main reasons given for the 
persistence of poor sanitation practices. The Strategy suggests Government will partner with NGOs 
and the private sector, subsidise communal sanitation facilities, and encourage community 
engagement to ensure every household has safe drinking water treatment and access to handwashing 
stations in public facilities. The Strategy does not consider gender-related issues in community-based 
sanitation and there is no consideration to the specific sanitation needs of women.  
 
All the policies discuss increasing the capacity of communities and workers to lead to increased 
community engagement and awareness of these issues, but there is limited or no discussion about 
providing women specifically with career opportunities in the WASH sector. The Long-Term Strategy 
foresees supporting sanitation entrepreneurship in areas with potential, facilitating entrepreneurship 
to create products that comply with standards and the Long-Term Strategy for Low Carbon and 
Climate Resilience seeks to improve job activities with the consideration of gender equality. Based on 
available education data, women seem relatively well placed to take on roles in the WASH sector. Of 
the 62.7% of women complete upper secondary education, compared to 58.4% of men.164 Whilst 
enrolment at tertiary levels of education remains relatively low at 36.3%, 38.9% of women enrol in 
tertiary education, compared to 33.7% of men.165  
 
The Presidential Instructions on Gender Mainstreaming (2000) emphasise the need to mainstream 
gender considerations in all policies and programmes. Most policies do recognise this but do not map 
out the implications or consistently differentiate how strategies will specifically target the different 
needs of women and men.

Water, sanitation and hygiene in Ivory Coast at a glance 
 
Access to drinking water: 79.8% of the population has access to an improved water source, of which 
35.2% has access to safely managed drinking water, 35.7% has access to basic drinking water and 
8.9% has access to limited drinking water. The remaining 20.2% of the population access

Those with a basic level of access to drinking water spend less than 30 minutes collecting water 
whilst those with a limited level of access to drinking water spend more than 30 minutes collecting

25.3% of the population practices open defecation whilst the remaining 17.6% of the population 
uses unimproved sanitation services.169  
 
Hygiene: 21.6% of the population has access to handwashing facilities with soap and water.170 
 
There is insufficient data on WASH in schools and health facilities available. 
 
Policy context 
In line with its 2040 Vision, the overall aim of Ivory Coast’s 2016—2020 National Development Plan is 
to create a state in which the population as a whole is better off, translating into a significant reduction 
of poverty and a rise of the middle class to ensure economic growth. This development plan is more 
proactive than previous plans, and as such is a reflection of the country’s relative stability and the 
increased levels of productivity the country has enjoyed in recent years. The 2016—2020 National 
Development Plan has five strategic areas that focus on: 
1. Quality institutions and governance;  
2. The preparedness and capacities needed for women and men to forge an emergent and 
prosperous Côte d’Ivoire;  
3. Changes in production and consumption patterns so as to bring Ivory Coast onto the global 
stage;  
4. Development of strategic infrastructure acting as a lever for Ivory Coast to move onto the 
global stage while abiding by environmental sustainability principles;  
5. Beneficial integration into regional and global trade and other networks. 
 
Although the plan has a strong focus on structural economic reforms, this is balanced with an 
understanding that these go hand in hand with the development of human capital. Water and 
sanitation is a cross-cutting issue that is identified under several of these five pillars of the plan, most 
 
166 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2020) 
167 MICS (2016) 
168 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2016)

Page 204

=== PAGE 208 ===

prominently, under pillars 1, 2 and 5. This highlights the challenges that Ivory Coast faces in water and 
sanitation, ranging from governance of resources, quality of health services and the population’s 
health, and lastly, lacking infrastructure, especially in light of worsening climatic change. Beyond the 
general lack of access to water and sustainable sanitation systems, the national plan clearly 
acknowledges the heavier burden on populations in cities that are rapidly urbanizing and the lack of 
social safety nets to secure livelihoods for more vulnerable populations, including women amongst 
others. Consequences, such as the rapid spread of diseases like cholera, and food shortages due to 
water scarcity often impacts women more severely.  
The 2015—2020 National Climate Change programme further discusses the health consequences of 
water scarcity and cleanliness. They have identified a positive correlation between variations in 
rainfall, temperature and the incidence rate of malaria, respiratory infections and diarrheal diseases. 
They have also demonstrated that urban populations are more vulnerable, particularly during the 
rainy season, due to inadequate wastewater drainage infrastructure. These challenges stem from the 
differences in rainfall between wet and dry seasons increasing, a drop in surface water availability and 
further effects of climate change on temperature and rainfall leading to extreme weather events. 
There is no mention of the differentiating impact between women and men, but the significant health 
consequences they identify present a clear challenge for the female population who are already at a 
disadvantage in this area. The programme promoted the introduction of climate change adaptation in 
all new legislation and policy instruments, identifying the importance of including diverse actors in 
their creation, including women’s associations and NGOs who have localised knowledge of challenges 
and opportunities. 
The National Development Plan broadly outlines that Ivory Coast should aim to increase accessibility 
to drinking water, build new water pumps and transfer the responsibility and ownership of adopting 
laws and regulations to local communities. Local communities should also include women’s 
associations and further diversified representation. The wider goal in sanitation and drainage is to 
create a larger and more interconnected network through infrastructural development as well as 
improving the hygiene and sanitation conditions in the most vulnerable communities. Ivory Coast’s 
Sector Policy Note on Sanitation (2012—2025) goes into more detail on improving sanitation in the 
country. Close to 5.5 million people do not have access to a latrine and practice defecation outdoors, 
mainly in rural areas, and many schools and health centres do not have improved sanitation facilities. 
Where there are improved sanitation facilities, this note identifies that these systems are not financed 
in a sustainable way, therefore upkeep is difficult. In terms of sanitation, there is a strong focus on 
eradicating open defecation, which has many negative impacts on women in terms of insecurity as 
well as hygiene and long-term, sustainable infrastructure improvements. The infrastructure 
development programme proposed raises the following considerations: 
• 
Create demand for sanitation and create competition between communities’ territories to 
promote on-site sanitation. 
• 
Strengthen coordination with public and private actors. 
• 
Strengthen the implementation and operating capacities of public and private actors and create a 
culture of social responsibility for these actors. 
• 
Strengthen the sector's financial resources. 
These suggested strategies focus on more than just the construction of new facilities and assure 
uptake of these new facilities to ensure their effectiveness. These strategies do not specifically 
Page 205

=== PAGE 209 ===
Page 98 of 143 
 
mention the inclusion of women or men in different ways. The National Development Plan does, 
however, identify how improved governance will result from better consideration of gender and the 
environment in public policies. With regard to gender, the strengthening of the institutional and 
regulatory framework will be necessary, as will the development of the skills of actors who apply it in 
plans, programs and projects. Furthermore, the plan proposes increased vocational training for 
women to include them in the employment sector, boosted by the improvements in water and 
sanitation infrastructure. Women and girl’s education levels are low, however, with only 14% of girls 
completing upper secondary171 and 8.5% of women enrolling in tertiary education.172         
Stakeholder Mapping 
In Ivory Coast, the Ministry of Hydraulics, Sanitation and Hygiene has primary responsibility for WASH. 
Other national Ministries with some WASH responsibility include the Ministry of the Environment of 
Urban Sanitation and Sustainable Development, who have a key role in the adaptation to climate 
change, and the Ministry of Health and Public Hygiene, leading on hygiene policy and programmes. 
There are a range of agencies involved in the delivery of WASH services at the sub-national levels, 
including regional and municipal government bodies and national and international CSOs. A wide 
range of development partners are involved in supporting government, private sector and CSO 
engagement in WASH. Figure C provides a visual mapping of the actors involved.

9.7 Kenya 
 
Water, sanitation and hygiene in Kenya at a glance

has access to basic drinking water and 9.5% has access to drinking water categorised as ‘limited.’ 
The remaining 28.8% of the population uses unimproved water sources for drinking water, of 
which 19% only have access to surface water.173

Policy context 
The drive to advance livelihoods in Kenya is laid out through the four key areas, dubbed the Big Four 
Agenda, within the National Development plan, named the Kenya Vision 2030 (2008–2030). These 
include achieving food security, affordable housing, manufacturing and affordable healthcare. Water, 
sanitation and hygiene continues to be a key sub-focus area within the Social Pillar outlined in the 
Third Medium Term Plan (MTP III), 2018—2022, the midterm plan within the National Development 
Plan of 2030. Within the plan it sees environmental degradation, climate change, inadequate 
infrastructure and lack of water conservation strategies as hindrances to the development of a clean, 
safe environment and access to water and sanitation services. It explains that approaches to combat 
these challenges come from capacity building, public participation, adoption of innovative technical 
approaches, multi-sectoral collaboration and adequate water and sanitation facilities. Within this 
policy, there is no discussion on how inadequate water, sanitation and hygiene facilities and practices 
impact women, but it does touch on the under-valuation of women’s contribution to the economy, 
under-employment and skills mismatch, and the implementation of the two-thirds gender rule that 
ensures that no more than 66% of one gender is represented in the national assembly and senate.  
 
The Kenya Environmental Sanitation & Hygiene Policy (KESHP) 2016, launched two years before the 
MTP III, recognises the importance of water, sanitation and hygiene in achieving Kenya Vision 2030 
and lays the foundation to include a people-centred approach to sanitation. The policy highlights key 
issues such as rapid urbanisation, the mortality and morbidity burden associated with unsafe water 
and sanitation and the risk of rape and domestic violence for women and girls due to inadequate 
sanitation facilities. To address these issues, the Policy intends to scale up access to improved rural 
and urban sanitation (Strategy 1) and build governance and leadership capacity for sanitation 
(Strategy 5). The policy discusses an increase in education, training and skill development for 
sanitation, including increasing the availability of skilled personnel in the planning and management 
of sanitation facilities, though it does not suggest these efforts are focused on any one gender/sex.  
 
While the Policy discusses the importance of improved facilities, it does not discuss the negative 
impacts inflicted by climate change. However, the Ministry of Water and Sanitation (MOWS) Strategic 
Plan 2018 brings to light the impacts climate change has on the quality, quantity, timing, form and 
intensity of precipitation leading to an increase in natural disasters It also highlights the decrease in 
available safe water supply. In addition, it touches on the importance of developing water 
infrastructure over focusing on water resources and management, and the impact that inadequate 
water and sanitation infrastructure and management have on the health of the population. In order 
to combat these challenges, the MOWS Strategic Plan lays out eight Key Result Areas (KRA), with 
Water and Sanitation Coverage (KRA 4 and 5), Human Resource Capacity (KRA 6), and Research and 
Technology (KRA 7) playing major roles in enhancing infrastructure, capacity building, employment 
opportunities and handling the adverse effects of climate change, though there is no clear discussion 
on a focus of women and girls.  
 
The National Policy on Gender and Development (NPGD) elaborates on the burden women and girls 
face due to clean water deficits, with the need to travel long distances to collect water for domestic 
use, which affects girls’ attendance at school and women’s opportunities for income generating 
activities. The NPGD seeks to reduce these burdens by ensuring the availability of water for multiple 
Page 208

=== PAGE 212 ===
Page 101 of 143 
 
uses to women and to develop and review the environment management laws to ensure gender and 
equity compliance.  
 
With climate change being a major discussion in most of the policies, the National Climate Change 
Policy examines how increases in weather extremities, including droughts, floods and water 
salinisation greatly impacts the water, sanitation and hygiene sector, with the biggest focus being on 
inadequate sanitation systems which lead to clogged drains and water contamination. The Ministry of 
Water and Sanitation Strategic Plan similarly addresses reversing degradation and climate change to 
ensure safe water and sanitation by strengthening capacity building in vulnerable communities.   
 
Interwoven throughout these policies is the continual discussion on the inclusion of equal 
opportunities for stakeholder participation throughout the planning, implementation and 
management of water and sanitation projects/programmes and the push for participation of female 
leaders on community-led committees through gender mainstreaming. While most policies include 
opportunities for job creation, many do not discuss career opportunities for women specifically. With 
only 9.73% enrolment of women in tertiary education,177 there continues to be a gap in highly skilled 
job opportunities within WASH. Policies focus more on low-skilled jobs, such as the KESHP, which 
includes training programmes for artisans and operators in sanitation and job creation in areas such 
as labour-intensive construction, sustainable livelihoods and long-term entrepreneurial activities. 
Finally, the NGDP seeks to encourage women’s participation in the technologies and sciences and 
STEM based courses.  
 
Whilst the KESHP and MOWS Strategic plan discuss the impact of inadequate water, sanitation and 
health in daily life, the National Education Sector Strategic Plan and the National Health Policy examine 
the importance of safe water, sanitation and hygiene within school and health facilities. Within 
schools, girls tend to be more affected by inadequate WASH facilities, and this impacts their 
attendance, especially when they are menstruating, and can expose them to increased risks of gender-
based violence. To limit the impact on children’s schooling, the National Education Sector Strategic 
Plan includes, among its objectives, the construction of facilities for safe and clean drinking water in 
schools and separate sanitation facilities for boys and girls.  
 
As the main users of primary health centres, poor WASH facilities in health centres disproportionately 
affects women and girls. The National Health Policy seeks to improve their WASH facilities to limit 
water-borne disease. The Policy also foresees mainstreaming gender issues in planning and 
implementation of programmes to minimise impacts on women and girls.  
 
Stakeholder Mapping 
In Kenya, the Ministry of Water and Irrigation has primary responsibility for WASH. Other national 
Ministries with some WASH responsibility include the Ministry of Health, responsible for sanitation 
and Hygiene provision and policy, and the Ministry of Environments and Forestry, who set 
environmental standards, and the Ministry of Housing, who handle WASH infrastructure. There are a 
range of agencies involved in the delivery of WASH services at the sub-national levels, including local 
water- and public health departments, the private sector, national and international CSOs. A wide 
range of development partners are involved in supporting government, private sector and CSO 
engagement in WASH. Figure D provides a visual mapping of the actors involved.

9.8 Namibia 
Water, sanitation and hygiene in Namibia at a glance 
 
Access to drinking water: 91.4% of the population has access to an improved source of drinking 
water, of which 84.3% has access to basic drinking water and 7.1% has access to limited drinking 
water. The remainder of the population relies on unimproved water sources, with 4.9% of the 
population having access to surface water only.178 Data on the proportion of the population with 
access to a safely managed water source is not available. 
 
Access to sanitation: 48.1% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 
35.3% has access to basic sanitation services and 12.8% has access to limited sanitation services. 
47.2% of the population continues to practice open defecation whilst 4.8% of the population uses 
unimproved sanitation services.179 Data on the proportion of the population with access to safely 
managed sanitation services is not available.  
 
Data on hygiene, WASH in schools and WASH in health facilities is not available.  
 
Policy context 
Namibia’s 5th National Development Plan (NDP5) focuses on the period 2017/18–2021/22 and charts 
a course toward the cumulative targets outlined in Namibia’s Vision 2030. The plan is organised 
around four key pillars for achieving sustainable development, namely Economic Progression, Social 
Transformation, Environmental Sustainability and Good Governance. NDP5 also identifies five ‘game 
changers’ that will move Namibia from a reactive, input-based economy towards a proactive, high-
performing economy.  
The plan looks at water and sanitation as two separate sectors, rather than looking at water and 
sanitation through a combined WASH lens. As such, analysis of the challenges faced in the water and 
sanitation sectors fall within two separate pillars of NDP5. Discussion of the water sector falls under 
the Economic Progression pillar of the plan, under the game changer subsection ‘increase investment 
in infrastructure development.’ The plan highlights key issues around safe water access in the water 
sector, including:  
• 
Availability of water and reliable water supply.  
• 
Increasing water demand for economic activities and urban consumption.  
• 
Water demand from the agriculture sector for irrigation.  
• 
Provision and financing of water infrastructure such as desalination plants in coastal areas.  
• 
Improving management of existing water sources.  
 
178 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2020) 
179 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2020) 
Page 211

=== PAGE 215 ===
Page 104 of 143 
 
Discussion of issues related to sanitation and hygiene fall under the Social Transformation pilar of the 
policy, under the ‘social development’ subsection. The key issues in the sanitation sector that the 5th 
NDP highlights includes:  
• 
Access to improved sanitation systems in rural areas. 
• 
Promoting coordinated investment on sanitation infrastructure. 
• 
Strengthening advocacy to tackle sanitation challenges. 
While NDP5 summarises the key challenges of safe water supply and sanitation services access, 
particularly in rural areas, Namibia’s Water Supply and Sanitation Policy (2008) goes into much more 
detail on the challenges themselves, the context, the health consequences and Namibia’s policy 
objectives in response to these challenges.  
The Water Supply and Sanitation Policy states that essential water supply and sanitation services 
should be available to all Namibians, that the improvement of water and sanitation services should be 
achieved equitably, that communities should have the right to determine which water and sanitation 
services are acceptable to them, and that utilisation of water resources should be environmentally 
sustainable. NDP5 then translates these broad policy objectives into actionable strategies as part of 
NDP5’s results framework.  
As with the NDP5, the Water and Sanitation Policy deals with water supply and sanitation in isolation, 
concentrating on supply for domestic use, supply for economic activities and rural water supply in the 
water sector, as well as provision of rural and urban sanitation services in the sanitation sector.  
Namibia has indicated its commitment to responding to climate change challenges through its 
National Policy on Climate Change (NPCC). The NPCC, adopted in 2011, is implemented through the 
National Climate Change Strategy Action Plan (NCCSAP) that was finalised in 2015 for the period 2013–
2020.  
Like the NDP5 and the Water and Sanitation Policy, Namibia’s 2011 National Policy on Climate Change 
also analyses the issues of water supply and provision of sanitation services but focuses much more 
heavily on the impact of climate change on both sectors, as well as the potential health consequences. 
The policy highlights the climate sensitivity of the two sectors, with Namibia’s already variable and 
unpredictable rainfall patterns being worsened by climate change. The policy argues that this in turn 
will have a significant impact on water supply and sanitation.  
Namibia ratified the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) in 1995 and 
thus became obligated to prepare and submit national communications, completing national 
communications in 2002, 2011 and 2015. Namibia’s Fourth National Communication to the UNFCCC, 
submitted in 2020, covers the policy framework of the NPCC and summarises progress against the 
NCCSAP. The communication acknowledges the vulnerability of water supply to climate change, as 
Namibia relies on rainfall as it’s natural water source as well as dams, ephemeral rivers and aquifers 
in the absence of perennial rivers. The communication also highlights how high evaporation rates 
coupled with the country’s semi-arid climate means that Namibia is now at a water deficit.  
While NDP5, the Water and Sanitation Policy, NPCC, the National Policy on Climate Change and the 
Fourth Communication to the UNFCCC all reference gender as a cross-cutting issue in the water and 
sanitation sectors, none explore in any great detail how women, youths and marginalised groups are 
impacted nor the roles that these groups can play in these sectors. In contrast, Namibia’s National 
Page 212

=== PAGE 216 ===
Page 105 of 143 
 
Gender Policy explicitly tackles the gender issues affecting the water and sanitation sectors. The policy 
argues that women continue to be under-represented in policy and decision-making forums on the 
utilisation of natural resources, environmental development and water management. The policy also 
explores the roles that women play within the water and sanitation sectors. For example, in most rural 
communities, women and girls constitute 75% of the workforce responsible for fetching water. 
Fetching water is primarily a female task with 36% of rural households having daughters fetch water 
compared to 15% which have sons fetch water. 
In addition, the policy explores the health inequalities related to water and sanitation. As outlined in 
the other policies discussed, Namibia has a limited infrastructure with which to provide people, 
especially rural populations, with access to water and sanitation facilities. A lack of these basic 
amenities has a detrimental effect on the health of rural communities, with diseases linked to water 
and sanitation such as tuberculosis and malaria more prevalent in rural areas. Because more women 
than men live in rural areas and female-headed households tend to be poorer than male-headed 
households, a lack of rural water and sanitation infrastructure disproportionately affects women and 
female-headed households.  
There is some discussion of women’s education on water and sanitation sector issues in the National 
Policy on Climate Change for Namibia. The policy states that the government will include gender and 
climate change, including issues such as water supply, in the curriculum of education and training 
programmes. However, the policy stops short of discussing women’s positioning for formal 
employment in the water and sanitation sectors. Currently, the completion rate for upper secondary 
education for women is relatively high, which implies that there is opportunity for WASH education 
policies that position women for employment in the sector to be successful.   
All of the above policies are very limited in their consideration of women’s employment levels in the 
water and sanitation sectors and in terms of opportunities for women to access employment in these 
sectors. However, the National Gender Policy (2010–2020) does put forward that, as a strategy for 
gender mainstreaming, women should be involved in interventions to achieve the objectives of 
environmental sustainability, including in the water and sanitation sectors. The policy stops short of 
discussing how this engagement could take place.  
Stakeholder Mapping 
In Namibia, the Ministry of Agriculture, Water and Land Reform has primary responsibility for WASH. 
Other national Ministries with some WASH responsibility include Ministry of Urban and Rural 
Development, ensuring implementation of national WASH strategy for urban settlements, and the 
Ministry of Health and Social Services, implementing and enforcing hygiene practices. There are a 
range of agencies involved in the delivery of WASH services at the sub-national levels, including 
district-level government agencies and traditional bodies, the private sector, national and 
international CSOs. A wide range of development partners are involved in supporting government, 
private sector and CSO engagement in WASH. Figure E provides a visual mapping of the actors 
involved.

9.9 Nigeria 
Water, sanitation and hygiene in Nigeria at a glance 
Page 214

=== PAGE 218 ===
Page 107 of 143 
 
  
Access to drinking water: 78% of the population has access to an improved water source for drinking 
water, of which 21.7% has access to safely managed drinking water, 55.9% has access to a basic 
level of drinking water and 5% has access to a limited level of drinking water. The remainder of the 
population uses unimproved sources for drinking water, including 5.6% who only have access to 
surface water.180 40% of the population are women who are the main collectors of water. It takes 
32.7% of the population, with improved drinking sources, 30 mins or less to collect water.181 
  
Access to sanitation: 43% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 
30.5% has access to safely managed sanitation service, 12.2% has access to a basic level of 
sanitation service whilst 19.5% has access to a limited level of sanitation service. 19.1% of the 
population uses unimproved sanitation services, including 18.7% who practice open defecation.182 
  
Hygiene: 33.2% of the population has access to handwashing facilities with soap and water.183 
  
WASH in schools: Nationally, 36.4% of schools have a basic service for drinking water, 37.8% have 
a basic service for sanitation and 27.6% have a basic service in hygiene.184 
  
WASH in health facilities: 46.4% of health care facilities have a basic service for drinking water, 
17.2% of health care facilities have basic service for sanitation and 65.8% of health care facilities 
have basic service for hygiene.185 
  
Policy context 
As Nigeria continues to experience rapid urbanisation and growth, it seeks to improve infrastructure 
throughout the country to promote development opportunities, establish a strong foundation for a 
diversified economy and alleviate poverty. One of the main ways they aspire to achieve this is through 
social development, which covers water resources and sanitation, water and gender equity and 
employment and job creation, as outlined in the Mid Report of the National Development Plan (2021). 
The Mid Report points to the progress in improving water service delivery over the years, while also 
reviewing continued challenges. Challenges identified include ensuring economic diversification, 
private sector investment in WASH services, lack of coordination and communal disputes, negative 
impacts of climate change, contamination of water sources and it’s impacts on health and poor 
operation and maintenance culture. The report also touches on the importance of WASH in light of 
the COVID-19 pandemic. Some of the main ways it seeks to overcome these challenges is by continuing 
to improve sanitation services, extending water supply access and adopting an integrated planning 
and policy formulation approach, while also increasing training, developing the bioeconomy and 
circular economy, and increasing access to finance and technical support for businesses and projects 
in environmentally sustainable sectors.  
 
 
180 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2020) 
181 MICS (2017) 
182 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2020) 
183 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2020) 
184 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2020) 
185 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2020)
