=== PAGE 219 ===
Page 108 of 143 
 
One recurring theme in the National Development Plan is the increase in negative impacts on Nigeria’s 
development brought about by climate change. In order to address this increasing threat, the National 
Climate Change Policy for Nigeria was launched in 2021. The Climate Change Policy addresses these 
challenges through 10 strategic objectives, including implementing adaptation and mitigation 
measures that promote low-carbon development (Strategy 1); mainstreaming gender, youth and 
other vulnerable groups into all climate change interventions (Strategy 5); and promoting climate-
proofing of construction and infrastructural development (Strategy 6). This policy examines the impact 
that extreme weather places on an already strained water, sanitation and hygiene sector due to an 
increase in droughts and floods. Nigeria seeks to overcome issues related to climate change and 
inadequate water and waste infrastructure through the implementation of a circular economy, as well 
as increasing infrastructure for wastewater management and reforms in the establishment, 
management, operation and maintenance of water supply and sanitation schemes and services. The 
policy recognises the importance of including women in the planning, implementation and 
management of related programmes, and seeks to integrate a gender perspective in the adoption of 
environmentally friendly and gender responsive technologies, and the development of gender-
responsive, socially inclusive and resilient water and sanitation infrastructure.  
 
One of the biggest challenges that Nigeria faces with implementing these policies and ensuring the 
adequate implementation of WASH facilities and infrastructure is the 3-tiered government structure 
which leads to a lack of coordination in policy and programme implementation as discussed in Nigeria 
Water and Sanitation Profile.186  
 
In all the policies reviewed, there was a clear theme around capacity building and job creation. Within 
the Climate Change Policy, there is discussion on collaboration with the Federal Ministry of Women 
Affairs to ensure equitable policies and programmes. This discussion seeks to encourage women’s 
participation in the planning and management of climate change programmes. This includes 
investment to promote water security and climate change resilient growth and development to 
sustain jobs, employment, economic growth and social stability. With 66.8% of women completing 
upper secondary school187 and no data available on those enrolled in tertiary education, there 
continues to be discrepancies in access to some highly skilled careers in WASH.  
 
While most policies reviewed discussed the importance of WASH and ensuring water security in the 
country, the National Gender Policy of 2006 looks at the importance of including women in the work 
force and actively participating in decision making. The policy calls for equal inclusion in the design, 
implementation and management of all projects/programmes implemented. It also highlights the key 
role women play in water, sanitation and hygiene as women are usually the collectors of water and 
are in charge of maintenance of facilities, including water, within the family. However, they are usually 
the ones greatly impacted by the consequences of unsafe drinking water. This health risk is highlighted 
further in the National Health Policy which recognises that women and children face higher mortality 
and morbidity rates due to an increase in communicable diseases stemming from poor water and 
sanitation. Although not specifically related to WASH, both policies emphasize the importance of the 
equal participation of women and increased education and awareness. They also encourage 
community engagement in best practices.

9.10 Morocco 
 
Water, sanitation and hygiene in Morocco at a glance 
 
Access to drinking water: 90% of the population has access to an improved water source for drinking 
water, of which 79.9% has access to safely managed drinking water, 10.5% has access to a basic 
level of drinking water and 5.3% has access to a limited level of drinking water. The remainder of 
the population uses unimproved sources for drinking water, including 1.5% who only have access 
to surface water.188 
  
Access to sanitation: 87% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 
39.3% has access to safely managed sanitation service, 47.9% has access to a basic level of 
sanitation service whilst 1.5% has access to a limited level of sanitation service. 9.5% of the 
population uses unimproved sanitation services, including 1.7% who practice open defecation.189  
  
Data on hygiene and WASH in health facilities is not available.  
  
WASH in schools: Nationally, 83.6% of schools have a basic service for drinking water, 69.9% has a 
basic service for sanitation and 89% has a basic service for hygiene.190 
  
Policy context 
Due to continued threats to available water resources throughout the country, the National 
Sustainable Development Policy 2017 (NSDP) recognises the importance of water, sanitation and 
hygiene improvements for the development of the country, as outlined in Strategic Focus 6: ensure 
the conservation and rational management of water resources, Strategic Focus 14: promotion of 
integrated waste management to implement a circular economy, and Strategic Focus 16: securitize 
the needs (by ensuring needs are meet) and strengthen water resources integrated management. Not 
only does it seek to improve facilities, access and programmes around water, sanitation and hygiene, 
it identifies the opportunities available for transitioning to a circular economy through recycling water 
usage. It also examines the need for human development and combat social inequalities within Stake 
6: promote human development and reduce social and territorial inequalities. While the 6th Stake 
touches briefly on the importance of equality for all, it remains quite gender blind with regard to the 
incorporation of gender in policies. The NSDP further connects the importance of fighting climate 
change as a factor to ensure the advancement of water, sanitation and hygiene within the country, 
especially in regard to discrepancies between regions within the country.  
 
Moroccan Climate Change Policy 2014 highlights the impact of climate change on health and water 
access in the country. The increase in temperatures and extreme weathers is leading to salinisation of 
water, desertification and an increase in water-borne diseases which impact women and girls more 
than men as women are typically responsible for managing water. It also points to the importance of 
 
188 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2020) 
189 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2020) 
190 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2020) 
Page 218

=== PAGE 222 ===
Page 111 of 143 
 
increasing financing and programmes focused on combating climate change, including education and 
awareness on alternative solutions and the re-use of water resources.  
 
While the Climate Change Policy touches on the importance of tackling climate change to ensure 
equitable access to water, sanitation and hygiene, the National Water Plan (2013) examines the 
current situation within Morocco, with the Moroccan climate being regulated by the Saharan 
depression and the anticyclones of the Azores leading to greater extremities when weather patterns 
change. With 20% of all water resources coming from ground water, a continued degradation of 
surface water, negative impacts from climate change and water contamination, Morocco has placed 
water as an important factor in its development. It outlines the importance of mobilising water 
resources, desalting ocean water, increasing the re-use of water for irrigation, enhancing water 
treatment and reusing wastewater. It also sets out the need to protect communities against floods, 
especially for sanitation facilities and agricultural drainage systems. Within the health policy that was 
reviewed, there was no mention of WASH specific strategies, though it does discuss the increase in 
water-borne diseases and the need to ensure adequate facilities.  
 
Within all these policies, there is no clear examination of how to increase job opportunities for women. 
However, the National Sustainable Development Plan discusses increasing employment opportunities 
more generally. This includes strategic planning of the building usage as an economic opportunity with 
resource consumption (such as organizing the flow of water and waste) and adapting training to meet 
the transition to green-based trades as well as ensuring equitable representation of women and men 
within all sectors as outlined in the National Strategy for Gender Equality & Equity 2006. The Strategy 
sees the value of ensuring there is equal participation of women and men in communities, especially 
around decision-making, and identifies the impact that inadequate water and sanitation facilities 
place on women, such as leaving them more vulnerable to illness, it also discusses the importance of 
equal access to education. With only 29% of females completing upper secondary191 and 41.7% of 
women completing tertiary education,192 there continues to be a disproportion of women having the 
skills needed for certain WASH-related careers. The Climate Change Policy discusses the importance 
of capacity building and training for individuals, communities, interest groups and public and private 
enterprises to better adapt to and mitigate climate change. It seeks to do so by strengthening human 
and technical capacities of stakeholders, training public actors to take ownership of the vision, 
concepts and integrated approach, organizing workshops for capacity building on issues related to 
climate change and strengthening STEM and climate-related education and training. Additionally, the 
Water Plan discusses the strengthening of capacity building, including skill building for highly skilled 
positions, through building efficient water planning and management, and advancement of 
techniques, construction and management adapted to a national context.

water resource planning, and The Ministry of Health, responsible for drinking water quality control 
and water-related laws. There are a range of agencies involved in the delivery of WASH services at the

9.11 Uganda 
Water, sanitation and hygiene in Uganda at a glance 
 
Access to drinking water: 83.1% of the population has access to an improved water source for 
drinking water, of which 16.6% has access to safely managed drinking water, 39.2% has access to a 
basic level of drinking water and 27.3% has access to a limited level of drinking water. The remainder 
of the population uses unimproved sources for drinking water, including 4.7% who only have access 
to surface water.193    
 
Access to sanitation: 37.4% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 
19.8% has access to a basic level of sanitation service whilst 17.6% has access to a limited level of 
sanitation service. 62.6% of the population uses unimproved sanitation services, including 4.7% 
who practice open defecation.194  
 
Hygiene: 22.6% of the population has access to handwashing facilities with soap and water.195 
 
WASH in schools: Nationally, 68.4% of schools have a basic service for drinking water, 79.6% has a 
basic service for sanitation and 30.1% has a basic service for hygiene.196  
 
WASH in health facilities: 44.3% of health care facilities have a basic service for drinking water. Data 
is not available on sanitation and hygiene facilities in health facilities.197 
 
Policy context 
The shift away from a focus on poverty reduction to one of wealth creation through industrial 
development in Uganda’s National Development Plan III (2020—2025) has resulted in decreased 
attention to water, sanitation and hygiene in national policy. Nevertheless, water, sanitation and 
hygiene remain a core area of Uganda’s Human Development Programme, a sub-programme in the 
NDP III, and one which seeks to improve labour productivity for increased competitiveness and better 
quality of life for all, with the following targets having been set: 
• 
Increased access to safe water supply from 70 to 85% (rural) and from 74 percent to 100% 
(urban). 
• 
Increased access to basic sanitation from (improved toilet) 19 to 40% and hand washing from 
34 to 50%. 
In addition, the NDP III also foresees investing in the effective management of the entire WASH value 
chain segments, including containment, emptying, transportation, treatment, safe reuse and disposal. 
The NDP III recognises that achievement of these, and other development targets, will be more 
challenging due to the effects of climate change, with more extreme weather patterns leading to

196 Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene  (2019)

increased water stress, impacting water availability and quality for both domestic and commercial 
uses as well as the water and sanitation infrastructure already in place. The Uganda National Climate 
Change Policy (2015—2020) articulates the challenges further and highlights that, given their domestic 
role, women are likely to experience the effects of climate change on water and sanitation facilities 
most keenly. It commits to mainstream gender issues across climate change adaptation and mitigation 
actions including ensuring climate change related policies and activities are gender sensitive and 
developing a gender-sensitive approach in monitoring and evaluating systems.  
The National Water and Sanitation Gender Strategy,198 launched in 2018 (two years earlier than the 
National Development Plan III) retains a focus on poverty reduction and seeks to ‘empower men, 
women, boys, girls and vulnerable groups through ensuring equity in access to and control of 
resources in the water and sanitation sub-sector’. The Strategy highlights the burden and risks for 
women and girls that come with poor access to water, sanitation and hygiene. It reports that, on 
average, women and girls spend up to six hours a day fetching water, limiting their time for economic 
activities and attending school; that women and girls face shame and risk of attack as a result of 
inadequate sanitation facilities; and that women and girls are more at risk of health conditions such 
as diarrhoea, dysentery and cholera as a result of their domestic responsibilities. It does not, however, 
recognise the negative implications climate change is likely to have on the burden and risks women 
and girls face. 
The Water and Sanitation Gender Strategy has five strategic objectives: 
1. Integrate gender into all government policy, guidelines, plans and budgets; 
2. Build the capacity of the Ministry of Water and Environment as well as other stakeholders to 
be aware of and to address gender differentials in water, sanitation and hygiene sectors and 
to promote a gender-sensitive work environment at the Ministry of Water and Environment; 
3. Economically empower men, women, boys, girls and vulnerable groups, especially those with 
disabilities, through equitable access to and control of water resources, supply, sanitation and 
hygiene; 
4. Collection and reporting of gender disaggregated data, with attention to gender issues in 
monitoring and reporting; 
5. Building partnerships within the Sub-sector, Line Ministries, Civil Society, districts and Local 
Governments to move forward the gender agenda; 
6. Gender coordination, partnership and networking. 
 
The most direct benefits to women and girls are likely to come through strategic objective 3, which 
foresees the rehabilitation and expansion of water and sanitation facilities in rural and urban locations, 
as well as support so women and girls can take advantage of employment or income-generating 
opportunities associated with development of water and sanitation facilities.  
This policy and other policies express a commitment to stakeholder participation in the planning, 
implementation and maintenance of water and sanitation projects, with the National Water Policy 
(1999) setting a minimum of at least half of Water and Sanitation Committees members being women. 
Whilst this commitment provides opportunities for women and girls to influence the development of 
water and sanitation infrastructure in their communities, they bring an additional time burden which 
 
198 Uganda’s Water and Sanitation Policy has not been accessible online and so has not formed part of our review. Interestingly, Uganda’s

women and girls may struggle to take on. Social traditions which encourage women and girls to defer

Whilst the Water and Sanitation Gender Strategy does not identify inadequate water, sanitation and 
hygiene facilities within schools, health centres and hospitals as an issue, the Second National Health 
Policy and the National Education and Sports Strategic Plan do. As the main users of primary health 
centres, women and girls can be severely affected by inadequate water, sanitation and hygiene 
practices in these centres. Similarly, inadequate toilet facilities are often a barrier to girls’ attendance 
at school, especially during menstruation. With this in mind, the Education and Sports Sector Plan 
includes, amongst its objectives, the expansion and improvement of safe water supply infrastructure 
to government supported primary schools as well as the construction of sanitation facilities primarily 
in primary schools. The National Health Policy also foresees improvements in health infrastructure 
and in waste disposal but does not provide specifics. 
The Water and Sanitation Gender Strategy recognises the importance of building the capacity of 
Ministry of Water and Education staff on gender and of encouraging a gender-sensitive work 
environment, but stops short from promoting actions to increase women’s participation in formal 
roles in the planning, development, management and maintenance of water and sanitation facilities. 
Interestingly, the National Development Plan III outlines clear plans for expanding education provision 
in Science, Technology, Engineering and Mathematics and increasing the number of graduates in these 
areas, but places no emphasis on encouraging women specifically into education and careers in these 
areas. Currently the proportion of women who have completed upper secondary school or enrolled 
in tertiary education remains low, with 20.2% and 4.33% respectively.

delivery of WASH services at the sub-national levels, including district-level government agencies, the

Annex 6: Gender Action Plan 
Introduction 
This document sets out Climate Fund Managers (CFM) action plan for promoting gender equality in 
water, sanitation and hygiene (WASH), protection and management of wetlands, coastal and marine 
ecosystems, and climate resilient port infrastructure through investments by the Climate Investor Two 
(CI2) fund. By adopting a gender mainstreaming approach to CI2 and the projects it supports, CFM 
expects not only to mitigate the risks for women and men associated with funded projects, but also 
to benefit women and men equally, reducing gender differentials associated with vulnerabilities that 
are exacerbated by climate change, and promoting sustainable and equitable development.   
 
CFM is committed to promoting gender equality and women’s empowerment externally (in countries 
and communities where CFM’s Managed Vehicles are invested) and internally, within CFM itself.199 It 
does so through a twin-track approach; firstly, by mainstreaming gender considerations into its 
investments,200 and secondly, by putting in place organisational systems to promote gender equality 
within CFM. Procedures relating to the former are described in CFM’s Environmental and Social 
Management System.  
 
The development of this Gender Action Plan (GAP) has been guided by the Green Climate Fund (GCF) 
approach to gender mainstreaming (Figure 1). It has been informed by a Gender Assessment which 
comprised:  
i) 
A review of global evidence in the areas of WASH, the protection of wetlands, coastal and 
marine ecosystems and climate resilient port infrastructure;  
ii) 
A review of national policies, strategies, plans and contexts, relating to WASH, gender and 
climate change for 11 CI2 focal countries: Bangladesh, Botswana, Brazil, Djibouti, Indonesia, 
Ivory Coast, Kenya, Morocco, Namibia, Nigeria, Uganda;  
iii) 
Stakeholder analysis for eight out of the 11 countries;201  
iv) 
A problem tree analysis for each of the three areas, which, following GCF guidance, drew on 
the Gender Assessment findings to unpack the causes and effects of the core problem 
identified; and  
v) 
Identification of opportunities for promoting gender equality through CI2 investments, based 
on the evidence reviewed. 
The GAP presents how CFM will use CI2 to take forward the opportunities for promoting gender 
equality identified in the Gender Assessment and provides a performance measurement framework 
which will be used to monitor CFM’s implementation progress.  As CI2 is expected to cover up to 25 
countries, and financing investments which have yet to be identified, the actions included in the GAP 
are, by necessity, high level. Project specific GAPs, which will be developed during the project design 
phase, will allow this high-level framework to be tailored more directly.  
 
199 CFM Gender Policy 2021 
200 Gender mainstreaming is the systematic process of ensuring that women and men have equal access and control over resources, 
development benefits and decision-making at all stages of the development process through ensuring that the respective needs, interests 
and priorities of women and men are integrated into in all policies, programmes and projects. CFM Gender Policy 2021

In the WASH sector, the Gender Assessment found that women’s and girls’ domestic and care-giving 
roles mean that they are disproportionately affected by inadequate access to WASH infrastructure 
and services, a situation which is being exacerbated by volatilities associated with climate change. 
Whilst national policies often recognise this and make commitments to addressing gender issues 
across their policy implementation, there is very little detail as to how this will be done in practice. 
The causes and effects of this situation are multiple and are summarised in the Problem Tree Analysis 
below (Figure 2).

As regards wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems, the Gender Assessment found that women are 
mostly excluded from the formal sector and most profitable parts of value chains in oceans-based 
economies, as well as from dialogue and decision making around community-based protection and 
management initiatives. At the same time, and in spite of gaps in the evidence base on climate-related 
impacts, they are likely to be disproportionately affected by damage to ecosystems. The causes and 
effects of this situation are summarized in the Problem Tree Analysis below (Figure 3).

Overview of Gender Actions 
Responding to the causes identified in the problem tree analyses, the Gender Assessment identified 
opportunities for promoting gender equality through CI2 investments in four main areas (Figure 3). 
These opportunities directly underpin the action areas presented below and in the action plan in Table 
2, with a fifth action area being added to address the organisational environment needed within CFM 
Project Companies and contractors, to facilitate the Gender Action Plan’s full implementation.  
Page 229

=== PAGE 233 ===
Page 122 of 143 
 
 
Figure 5: Opportunities for promoting gender equality identified in the Gender Assessment 
 
 
Gender Action Areas for CI2 
Improving women and girls’ access to WASH  
As far as possible, CFM and its Project Companies will use its investment projects and community 
development projects to address the physical, financial, attitudinal and safety barriers that may limit 
women’s and girls’ access to WASH.  
Using normal project development processes e.g., the Environment and Social Impact Assessment 
(ESIA), the Community Needs Assessment and Local Gender Assessment etc., CFM will explicitly 
consider the barriers which affect women’s and girls’ access to WASH, and how the investment design 
can address these. It will incorporate appropriate actions into the design of investment projects, 
where possible, to address these barriers, and include relevant indicators in the results frameworks 
to monitor progress. As part of this, CFM will consider the barriers faced by different groups of women 
and girls, such as those from chronically poor households, from indigenous households and women 
and girls with disabilities. CFM will also consider the specific needs some of these groups might have 
in accessing WASH facilities.  
Page 230

=== PAGE 234 ===
Page 123 of 143 
 
CFM will seek to use community development projects to increase the direct benefits for women and 
girls resulting from investments, by addressing identified barriers which cannot be addressed through 
the main investment. Examples of potential projects include: 
• 
Working with families and communities to build their understanding of and support for good 
WASH practices, including during menstruation, so that families are willing to invest scarce 
resources into WASH services, and communities are proactive in encouraging all members to 
observe good hygiene practices. Targeting men is critical in such interventions as they often 
control the use of financial resources in households.     
• 
Modifying water points and toilet facilities so they can be used by women and girls with 
disabilities. 
• 
Providing good lighting in places used as shared water points or sanitation facilities.  
It is recognised that improving WASH facilities in schools and health centres often brings significant 
benefits to women and girls. Opportunities for doing this will be considered as part of the investment 
project and community development project design process.   
Agreed objectives and actions to improve women’s and girls’ access to WASH, either through the 
investment project itself and/or through community development projects, will be set out in the 
project design, with relevant indicators to track progress and outcomes incorporated into the project’s 
results framework (see Action 4 below). For the investment projects, these objectives will also be 
reflected in contracts with the Engineering Procurement Contractor (EPC) and will be reviewed as part 
of normal EPC performance review processes.  
2. Giving women a voice in the planning, design and management of facilities and 
initiatives 
As a priority beneficiary group for CI2 investments, it is essential that stakeholder engagement and 
consultation processes service the needs and experiences of women and girls. This is to ensure that 
investment projects and community development projects can be responsive, as far as possible, to 
the needs of women and girls.  
Social norms in CI2 focal countries often devalue and silence women’s voices, even when public 
consultation mechanisms are in place. To counter this and to ensure women’s and girls’ needs inform 
investment design, CFM and its Project Companies will adopt a stakeholder engagement approach 
that provides conducive spaces for women and girls to voices their needs and experiences. This 
process will involve: 
• 
Identifying women and girls as a distinct stakeholder group with whom to engage directly, 
whilst recognising that, within this broad category, there are intersecting sub-groups with 
diverse needs and experiences who also need to be involved, e.g., indigenous women, women 
with disabilities and chronically poor women. 
• 
At the community level, identifying women’s leaders to work with to convene appropriate 
fora to hear a range of women’s and girls’ voices about their needs and challenges. 
• 
Working with community and male leaders to help them understand the rationale for 
engaging women in the planning of facilities and initiatives and build their support for that to 
avoid domestic arguments or backlash. 
• 
Holding consultation meetings at times and at venues which are convenient to the women 
and girls being targeted and considering how the direct and indirect costs of attending can be 
offset, particularly for the chronically poor, and for women and girls with disabilities. 
Page 231

=== PAGE 235 ===
Page 124 of 143 
 
• 
Using consultation sessions with women and girls as an opportunity to inform the design of 
the grievance mechanism so that it is accessible and so that women and girls have confidence 
in it as a system of redress and accountability. As part of this, a zero tolerance to all forms of 
gender-based violence and sexual harassment perpetrated by any staff or contractor from 
CFM, its Project Companies or contractors will be communicated.   
In some projects, community-led management committees may play a role in the management of 
WASH facilities and ocean ecosystem protection initiatives. Where this is the case, CFM and its Project 
Companies will support committees to operate in a way that provides space for women’s voices to be 
heard. The approach is likely to be context specific, but might involve:  
• 
Identifying and recruiting to the committee local women who are perceived (by men and 
other women) to have some legitimacy in speaking out on behalf of the community. These 
women are likely to have other leadership roles in the community and have experience of 
publicly expressing their views. 
• 
Building the skills of the women representatives on the committee so that they can consult 
with a diverse range of women and girls in the community to get their feedback on the WASH 
facilities, and to represent these views in committee meetings and decision-making. 
• 
Building male committee members’ understanding of the need for management to be 
informed by women’s and girls’ experiences, and to develop the skills to ensure women’s 
voices inform committee decision-making. 
Planned actions to provide a safe space for women’s voices in the planning, design and management 
of facilities and initiatives will be set out in the investment’s stakeholder engagement plan. Routine 
monitoring of the plan’s implementation will give particular attention to actions taken to capture 
women’s voices and to how project design and management is responding to the feedback received. 
3. Providing women with formal employment opportunities  
Low levels of secondary education completion amongst girls in several of the CI2 focal countries, 
combined with gender norms around acceptable productive roles for women and men, can make the 
formal employment of women in the WASH sector, as well as ocean-based industries and operation 
of port operations, challenging.  
Project Companies will be proactive in hiring a gender-balanced team, making adjustments to working 
practices where this could level the playing field between male and female candidates. It is, however, 
recognised that in several focal countries there are systemic issues beyond CFM’s control which are 
likely to limit the Project Companies’ ability to recruit senior female project staff. In an effort to 
ameliorate this situation, CFM will scope out, in some focal countries, opportunities to encourage 
more women to enter the WASH sector. Options might include: providing scholarships for women 
embarking on tertiary education in relevant Science, Technology, Engineering and Mathematics 
(STEM) fields, providing work placements on projects for current female STEM students, or supporting 
women involved in the WASH sector to visit secondary and tertiary education facilities to encourage 
women to consider a career in the sector. Viable opportunities that can be accommodated within the 
overall investment will be pursued as pilot projects. 
At the project level, Project Companies will identify employment opportunities for women in WASH 
enterprises and put in place appropriate mechanisms to recruit, train and support women in these 
roles. Opportunities might include roles as sanitation sale agents, in meter reading, water tank 
construction or cleaning, amongst others. It is recognised that there is limited experience of such 
approaches, and work done in this area through CI2 will be done on a pilot basis with the intention of 
Page 232

=== PAGE 236 ===
Page 125 of 143 
 
increasing knowledge about effective approaches. Figure 4 provides an example of how CFM has 
promoted women’s employment opportunities in Sierra Leone. Where investments include improving 
WASH facilities in schools and health centres, Project Companies will seek to work with service 
managers to put in place a resourced plan for the on-going cleaning and maintenance of the facilities, 
and which does not default to traditional gender roles.   
 
As regards the protection and management of wetlands, coastal and marine ecosystems, projects 
supporting sustainable practices in oceans-based industries should not only take into account such 
restrictions – on women’s time of mobility, for instance – but actively seek to tackle discriminatory 
norms which limit women’s livelihoods opportunities. 
Project Companies will seek to attract women recruits to climate-resilient port operations, and 
support their integration and progression, by providing a combination of initiatives to build their skills 
and confidence and provide a supportive work environment.  
Understanding the results and impact of gender mainstreaming  
Monitoring and reporting will be undertaken in accordance with the principles set out in CFM’s 
Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS). CFM will monitor and evaluate the actions 
taken to promote gender equality, their results and impact, in two ways. Firstly, by monitoring and 
evaluating key impact indicators at the Fund level. Secondly, by implementing monitoring plans for 
individual projects.  
Fund Level Monitoring and Evaluation 
The KPIs established for individual programmes will supplement the key impact indicators that have 
been adopted for the CI2 funds, and against which CI2 is committed to monitor performance in all 
Box 1. 
Supporting women business leaders and women’s employment in Sierra Leone 
CFM’s investment concerns the development of a waste to value portfolio in the city of Freetown, 
Sierra Leone. The assets comprise modular units which process organic waste and generate clean 
energy and digestate that can be used as fertiliser, thus contributing to the circular economy and 
providing a much-needed solution to the problem of waste management in the city. Currently 
most of the waste ends up in the city’s waterways and in the marine environment. 
 
The waste is collected by waste collection micro-enterprises (“youth groups”), which were 
established, trained and equipped by Freetown City Council to conduct door-to-door household 
waste collection in the city, and are jointly responsible for collecting up to 50% of the waste in 
city. The modules can be installed at a wide variety of premises and existing customers include a 
women’s maternal health centre in Freetown.   
 
The company in which CI2 is investing is a woman-led business as is its partner organisation. 
Female operators are actively sought, and the youth groups are approximately 30% female. With 
CI2’s investment, a local gender action plan has been established, along with a programme of 
community development that includes a focus on women as well as youth groups.

Project level monitoring and evaluation 
Project companies will implement a monitoring and evaluation plan, which sets out: 
• 
The indicators to be used to monitor how women and girls, as well as other priority social 
groups e.g., indigenous women, women from chronically poor households and women with 
disabilities, are being affected – both positively and negatively – by project interventions, and 
the impact these projects are having on their lives.  
• 
The methodology for gathering and analysing monitoring data, as well as reporting and using 
the findings. 
Each monitoring and evaluation plan will be bespoke to individual projects. However, they will contain 
comparable quantitative and qualitative indicators and data collection methodologies where 
appropriate. Depending on a project’s life cycle, monitoring data will be collected where possible at 
regular intervals to understand progress made during project implementation. The aim of project 
monitoring will be to provide timely information to make project adjustments and address any 
negative effects.  
Relevant components of the monitoring and evaluation plan may be incorporated into contracts for 
EPCs or other sub-contractors, with reviews of the plan’s implementation forming part of the 
contractor’s normal review process. 
There is a need for more evidence on effective approaches to promoting gender equality through 
investments in WASH, protection of ocean ecosystems and climate-resilient port infrastructure. In 
response to this, CFM may publish some of the results and learnings from its CI2 investments. 
 
Creating an enabling environment for gender mainstreaming in the CI2 Fund 
and the Project Companies  
CFM recognises the need to draw on specialist skills and advice to guide the company’s leadership on 
how to operationalise its gender commitments across the business and within the investments it 
manages. This is expected to be achieved through a network of gender and inclusion specialists who 
can be hired on an as-needed basis to provide both fund and project specific support. This network 
will benefit from contacts with local organisations with a strong track record in addressing gender 
issues in WASH, protection and management of wetlands, coastal and marine ecosystems, and climate 
resilient port infrastructure. It is anticipated that these specialists and local organisations could 
Page 234

=== PAGE 238 ===
Page 127 of 143 
 
potentially partner with Project Companies as a delivery agent e.g., in managing the stakeholder 
consultation with women or in community development activities. 
CFM will support Project Companies to put in place essential internal systems and capacities that will 
facilitate gender mainstreaming through the project and its operations, thereby operationalising this 
CI2 Fund Gender Action Plan. Arrangements will include:  
• 
Developing a local (project-specific) gender action plan based on an analysis of gender-related 
challenges and opportunities within the local context. This will integrate project-specific and 
organisational intentions for promoting gender equality. 
• 
Agreeing and implementing a project-specific Gender Equality Policy which sets out the 
company’s commitments to gender equality in their own internal operations and through the 
investment. This policy will set out the Project Company’s position on gender mainstreaming 
and on specific topics such as Gender Based Violence and Harassment (GBVH) and the 
mechanisms in place to safeguard staff from it. 
• 
Taking proactive measures to recruit a gender-balanced project team, seeking, where 
possible, to recruit qualified women into Project Company roles traditionally held by men. 
• 
Building the understanding and support of company staff to promote gender equality, both 
within their organisational context and in the project itself.  
• 
Integrating expectations of gender equal behaviours into staff job specifications and 
performance appraisal systems, with company leadership actively demonstrating a 
commitment to gender equality and setting Project Company expectations in this regard.   
• 
Providing the project team responsible for health, safety, social and environmental 
management with the necessary training and skills to guide the Project Company in the 
effective implementation of its gender equality commitments.  
• 
Implementing and managing a grievance mechanism for staff to report complaints, including 
cases of GBVH in the workplace.  
• 
Monitoring and reporting progress against agreed indicators, tracking the impacts of the 
project on women and girls. 
Project Companies will also take steps to require its principal contractors203 to give due attention 
to gender issues in their own operations. Requirements to be imposed on contractors are likely to 
include: 
• 
An operational Gender Equality Policy. 
• 
Proactive measures to recruit a gender-balanced team, seeking, where possible, to recruit 
qualified women into roles traditionally held by men. 
• 
A grievance mechanism for the contractor (and sub-contractor) workforce to report 
complaints, including cases of GBVH.  
These requirements would form part of the contract. Progress on delivering against these 
requirements would form part of the normal contractor performance review process. 
 
 
203 In many cases the project is built by one principal contracting company – referred to as the Engineering, Procurement and 
Construction (EPC) contractor. In other cases a split contract may be awarded to different principal contracting parties. In such cases, each 
party would be required to follow the same requirements, with the actions coordinated by the Project Company.

=== PAGE 239 ===
Page 128 of 143 
 
Gender Action Plan (GAP) 
The action plan on the following page summarises the actions to be taken by CFM, and at Fund level, 
in order to mainstream gender issues in the investment lifecycle of the CI2 fund.  
CFM will monitor progress against this Gender Action Plan once a year using the indicators provided. 
Indicators may be updated over the life of CI2 to ensure they align well with CI2 investments and 
procedures.
